 WILLISTON BASIN RESOURCE STUDY FOR COMMERCIAL-SCALE
 SUBSURFACE HYDROGEN STORAGE

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

 The Energy & Environmental Research Center (EERC), in partnership with the U.S.
Department of Energy (DOE) National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL), the EERC’s State
Energy Research Center (SERC), MPLX Operations LLC, and TC Energy Development Holdings
Inc. (a subsidiary of TC Energy Corporation), studied the potential for subsurface hydrogen storage
and recovery in the Williston Basin of western North Dakota. The project’s goal was to evaluate
the feasibility of large-scale, secure geologic H2 storage to support future hydrogen generation,
storage, and use. This work included laboratory testing, H2–rock–fluid exposure experiments,
literature reviews on H2 embrittlement, and reservoir modeling and simulations. The study
included an assessment of storage potential across three types of storage reservoirs using both
reservoir simulation and DOE’s web-based tool SHASTA-HELP (Subsurface Hydrogen
Assessment, Storage, and Technology Acceleration – Hydrogen Estimator for Logistical
Planning), as well as investigation of potential H2 production and markets for commercial-scale
deployment.

 Building on prior EERC gas storage research, three storage options were selected for detailed
evaluation: the Broom Creek Formation (a clastic saline reservoir), the Dickinson Lodgepole
Mounds (DLM) complex (carbonate mud mound structures) of the Lodgepole Formation (an
active oil and gas producing reservoir), and the Dunham Salt Interval of the Piper Formation (to
be used for engineered salt cavern development). These targets were prioritized based on prior
EERC research using datasets related to seal capacity, reservoir quality, mechanical integrity, and
injectivity.

 Exposure tests on Broom Creek and DLM samples showed mineral dissolution and
precipitation that increased brine salinity and altered reservoir rock surfaces. Although these
results provide useful insight, they are limited by small sample sizes and short-term (30-day)
exposure, requiring further study to assess long-term storage integrity. Salt formations were not
tested because of their known nonreactivity and established mechanical stability.

 Results of reservoir simulations performed for a single site demonstrated that the Broom
Creek Formation may be capable of receiving up to 42,000 tonnes of injected H2 over 7 months
via one well. H2 recovery took place over 5 months, resulting in approximately
26,000 tonnes (~62% without cushion gas [CG]). This work suggests water production may be
important and subsequent cycles of injection and production may perform more efficiently;
however, significant site-specific work in the future is needed to assess actual reservoir
performance of injection and withdrawal of H2 storage. For oil reservoir potential, a multiple-well
model was used to simulate injection of approximately 32,000 tonnes of H2 into a single wellbore
while simultaneously producing in place reservoir fluids from four offset wells to maintain
reservoir pressure. The simulation results suggested a high recovery (~98%); in addition, cost
advantages through existing infrastructure could be realized. Challenges in this reservoir include

 v

managing gas purity and leakage risks. In both scenarios, production of H2 takes place in a single-
well scenario with 10 cycles (7 months of injection and 5 months of production) over 10 years.

 Finally, the use of engineered caverns in the Dunhan Salt was evaluated, and the results
suggest that while they have a smaller capacity (<1000 tonnes per cavern), they exhibit nearly
complete gas recovery (>99%), fast response times, and low purity risk. While caverns in North
Dakota may be smaller in capacity, fields can be developed in galleries to accommodate the
volumetric needs and rapid turnaround times necessary to meet market demands. Geographic
limitations and thin salt intervals in North Dakota may represent less total storage potential than
salt domes elsewhere, but significant opportunities exist to expand this market for gas storage in
North Dakota.

 A basinwide assessment was performed to estimate a first-of-its-kind value for H2 storage
on a large scale. DOE’s SHASTA-HELP, combined with EERC simulation work, was used to
perform the assessment. Estimated H2 storage potential varied widely for each formation type. The
Broom Creek saline formation was estimated to have a storage potential of approximately
1.7–90.5 million tonnes (MMt). The DLM oil reservoirs were estimated to have 0.07–0.19 MMt
of capacity. Notably, each of these estimates relies on significant assumptions regarding reservoir
thickness, porosity, permeability, and CG needed for operation. Much research is needed to
understand the true site-specific storage resource potential of each formation. Using the Dunham
Salt Interval for cavern development may result in as much as 4.79 MMt (up to 2.87 MMt working
gas) of H2 storage potential. An important note for consideration is that the values presented here
need significantly more geological characterization and engineering assessments prior to gaining
confidence in performance. This will be a focal point for future research and development needs.
The basinwide evaluation also indicated that North Dakota has significant H2 generation potential,
with estimates up to a possible 13 MMt annually, suggesting a substantial opportunity for H2
market development and thus the need for commercial-scale H2 storage to facilitate growth and
resilience.

 Key Recommendations

 1. Conduct detailed site characterization (3D seismic, well logs, core sampling) to reduce
 geologic uncertainty.

 2. Perform techno-economic analyses incorporating market, regulatory, and incentive
 frameworks.

 3. Investigate long-term interactions among H2, CGs, well materials, and formations to
 assess risks.

 4. Develop pilot- and field-scale demonstrations to validate models and establish best
 practices.

 vi

 WILLISTON BASIN RESOURCE STUDY FOR COMMERCIAL-SCALE
 SUBSURFACE HYDROGEN STORAGE

1.0 INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND

 The Energy & Environmental Research Center (EERC), in partnership with the U.S.
Department of Energy (DOE) National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL), the EERC’s State
Energy Research Center (SERC), MPLX Operations LLC (and/or with its affiliates), and TC
Energy Development Holdings Inc., (a wholly owned subsidiary of TC Energy Corporation),
conducted fundamental research to better understand the potential for hydrogen storage and
recovery in a variety of subsurface geologic formations in the Williston Basin of western North
Dakota. The Williston Basin comprises geologic formations with properties that are likely
amenable to H2 storage and recovery, including saline formations, depleted oil and gas reservoirs,
and bedded salt formations.

 Underground hydrogen storage (UHS) involves injecting hydrogen gas into subsurface
geologic reservoirs including deep saline formations, depleted oil and gas reservoirs, or engineered
salt caverns in bedded salt layers. By leveraging natural geologic reservoir and seal pairs, UHS
may offer a safe and cost-effective means to store hydrogen in large quantities for extended periods
(Raad and others, 2022; Saeed and Jadhawar, 2023, 2024). The development of UHS is critical for
developing regional hydrogen hubs, supporting hydrogen refueling networks, and ensuring the
continuous operation of industrial hydrogen users. Moreover, large-scale storage is key to
stabilizing hydrogen supply chains, especially in regions aiming to expand renewable energy
development and achieve net-zero carbon goals.

 At present, opportunities for H2 production in North Dakota include widespread arrays of
wind turbines used for electricity generation and increasing natural gas supply coincidentally with
oil production from the prolific Bakken petroleum system. North Dakota’s coal resources can also
support H2 generation, as demonstrated by the Great Plains Synfuels Plant, which has operated in
western North Dakota since 1984, gasifying lignite coal into H2 and synthetic natural gas.
Figure 1 shows the distribution of wind turbines, natural gas processing plants, and natural gas
pipelines within the North Dakota portion of the Williston Basin.

 Gas storage in deep saline and depleted oil and gas formations must meet specific criteria to
be considered technically achievable. First, geologic formations of interest should contain
sufficient porosity and permeability to allow predetermined volumes of gas to be stored and, in the
case of hydrogen, produced at rates to accommodate the industrial process. Second, the storage
formation should be able to trap injected gas beneath a layer of rock that has permeability low
enough to prevent gas flow to unintended overlying formations. Third, the geologic formation
should be laterally extensive enough to contain the desired volume of gas while minimizing gas
losses over time. Additional factors for consideration include proximity of storage to market,
pipeline networks, population centers and land use, and environmental considerations such as
surficial fresh water and subsurface potable water resources. For engineered salt caverns, specific
geological criteria for consideration include depth and lateral extent of bedded salt, salt thickness,
purity of salt, and caprock integrity.

 1

 Figure 1. Study region showing the distribution of wind turbines, current location of H2
 production, and natural gas processing plants with associated pipeline distribution
 network.

 The EERC used these criteria to study the Williston Basin’s ability to store CO2 and natural
gas. Through these studies, invaluable knowledge, expertise, and datasets related to the
aforementioned criteria for subsurface gas storage have been generated and applied to this
investigation of H2 storage. While the evaluation of H2 storage and recovery can build on existing
CO2 and natural gas storage workflow procedures, additional work was needed to better
understand the significant differences in thermophysical and chemical properties of H2 versus CO2
and natural gas and how these may impact storage and recovery operations.

 The goal of this project was to support the future commercialization of H2 generation and
use by assessing the potential for high-volume, secure subsurface H2 storage with concomitant
high recovery from subsurface geologic reservoirs of the North Dakota portion of the Williston
Basin. Project activities were conducted to achieve this goal and advance the science behind H2
storage in North Dakota. Project activities included 1) identification of potential formations for
gas storage, 2) laboratory studies to determine rock properties and to evaluate potential
geochemical impacts of H2 exposure to subsurface geologic storage targets and formation fluids
3) a literature review of hydrogen embrittlement of wellbore casing material during storage,
4) geologic modeling and reservoir simulation to identify optimal metrics and scenarios for high-

 2

volume H2 injection and recovery, 5) H2 storage assessments of the Broom Creek Formation,
Dickinson Lodgepole Mounds (DLM), and bedded salt accumulations (Dunham Salt Interval) that
can have caverns developed for product storage; and 6) H2 production and markets investigation
for commercial-scale deployment potential in North Dakota.

2.0 TARGET SUBSURFACE FORMATIONS FOR H2 STORAGE

 The geologic targets explored as representatives of each type of UHS in North Dakota are
shown in the simplified North Dakota stratigraphic column shown in Figure 2. The Inyan Kara
and Broom Creek Formations are both well-characterized saline formations that have previous
data and geologic models available to the EERC for this study. Similarly, extracted core and
accompanying geologic models were available for oil and gas reservoirs within the Lodgepole and
Duperow Formations. Prior investigation of product storage in engineered salt caverns conducted
by the EERC (Smith and others, 2020) has shown that North Dakota’s shallowest-occurring
bedded salt formations—the Dunham Salt Interval (primary target) of the Piper Formation and the
Pine Salt Interval (secondary target) of the Spearfish Formation—are potential salt members for
cavern development.

 Out of these six potential formations suitable for UHS, the Broom Creek and Lodgepole
Formations and Dunham Salt Interval were selected for further investigation of storage in saline
and depleted oil and gas formations and engineered salt cavern use in bedded salts. Prior studies
by the EERC as well as ongoing CO2 injection for dedicated storage have confirmed that the
Broom Creek Formation has demonstrated permeability and injectivity, making it ideal for rapid
injection and withdrawal of gases, an important consideration for gas storage. In addition, the
Broom Creek Formation has been fully characterized for CO2 storage as part of the existing
authorized Class VI well injection permits in North Dakota, providing more extensive datasets and
models already developed and available that could be easily leveraged for this project. Lastly, the
Inyan Kara Formation is the primary injection target for produced water associated with oil
production in the Williston Basin. As such, H2 storage sites are limited due to the widespread
development of the Inyan Kara for disposal purposes. The Broom Creek Formation is the target
for three operating and five permitted CO2 storage projects in North Dakota, which would also
compete for pressure space with H2 storage (Burton-Kelly and others, 2025). The DLM, a type of
carbonate mud mound containing oil and gas in an area near Dickinson, North Dakota (Gorecki
and others, 2009; Knudsen and others, 2009), provide an ideal geologic structure for gas storage,
and the EERC has existing geologic and reservoir simulation models of the DLM for the Stadium
Field developed and calibrated using the historical production and waterflooding data for the
formation. In addition, because of its geometry, the DLM provide excellent containers (not similar
to other reservoirs in the state) for natural trapping of injected gas. The structure is also known to
contain a significant natural fracture network, enhancing the overall permeability of the system
and, in turn, improving injection and production. As such, the DLM were selected for the depleted
oil reservoir category. While the formation is not depleted yet, the site is modeled to accept H2 as
future production reaches depletion. As for the salt formation, the Dunham Salt Interval was
selected as it has lower impurities within salt content and more favorable geotechnical
characteristics compared to the Pine Salt Interval. Figure 3 shows the areal extent in western North
Dakota of the Broom Creek Formation, Dunham Salt Interval, and DLM complex investigated in
this study.

 3

Figure 2. Formations within the Williston Basin with potential H2 storage.

 4

Figure 3. Map showing the extent of the Broom Creek Formation, Dunham Salt Interval, and
DLM complex studied for UHS during this investigation. The Broom Creek is a siliciclastic
saline formation, the DLM are carbonate structures producing oil from the Lodgepole
Formation, and the Dunham Salt Interval is a bedded salt with potential for cavern
development for product storage.

 5

 2.1 Laboratory Investigation

 Laboratory work was conducted to accomplish project goals through 1) assessment of saline
and depleted oil and gas formations for H2 storage suitability through geologic modeling and
simulation and 2) characterization and assessment of the effects of long-term H2 exposure on
formation fluids, reservoir and confining unit rocks, and wellbore materials. Laboratory activities
included acquiring, analyzing, and testing core samples from the Broom Creek Formation and
DLM complex targeted for H2 storage investigation. Samples for these two formations were
obtained from the North Dakota Geological Survey Wilson M. Laird Core and Sample Library at
the University of North Dakota. Formations identified for sample selection were based on several
criteria: existing data on Williston Basin formations suitable for H2 storage, availability and size
of core for sampling, proximity of wells with available core to existing numerical models, and
preference for wells containing reservoir and confining zone intervals available. Salt
accumulations in the Williston Basin, such as the Dunham Salt Interval in the Piper Formation,
were previously characterized by Smith and others (2020) for general product storage needs
including natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), individual NGL components, or potentially H2;
to avoid redundancy, this project did not include further laboratory analysis of these formations.
However, while the other two formations have been characterized for CO2 storage, they were not
previously investigated for H2 storage; hence additional laboratory work was performed with a
focus on H2 storage.

 2.1.1 Property Determination of Broom Creek and Lodgepole Samples

 The experiments assessed key physical and chemical properties under reservoir conditions,
including interfacial tension (IFT), contact angle (CA), porosity, permeability, and capillary entry
pressure. These measurements were critical for understanding hydrogen behavior in subsurface
environments and for generating input data for reservoir-scale simulations. A summary of
laboratory results is provided here, and additional detail can be found in Appendix A.

 Results revealed distinct differences between the two formations. In the Broom Creek
Formation, the interaction between hydrogen and brine was dynamic, with IFT decreasing from
61.8 to 35.7 mN/m over 9 minutes, suggesting strong gas–liquid interactions that could enhance
hydrogen trapping. CA tests indicated initial hydrophobicity across lithofacies, with gradual
decreases over time. Sandstone samples exhibited excellent reservoir quality, with porosity up to
24% and permeability reaching 510 mD, making them ideal for hydrogen injection. Conversely,
mudstone and anhydrite displayed very poor reservoir quality and high entry pressures, confirming
their role as effective seals. Dolostone and tight sandstone showed intermediate properties,
functioning as baffles within the formation.

 The Lodgepole Formation presented a contrasting picture. IFT stabilized quickly at
approximately 13.1 mN/m in hydrogen–oil systems, indicating minimal interaction and high
hydrogen mobility. However, matrix porosity and permeability were extremely low, classifying
the formation as poor to intermediate in reservoir quality. Despite this, natural fractures—common
in Lodgepole reservoirs—are expected to significantly improve flow performance in the field.
Laboratory tests confirmed that higher injection pressures would be required to access smaller pore
throats in this formation.

 6

 Relative permeability experiments using helium as a proxy for hydrogen demonstrated
residual gas saturations of 22% in Broom Creek and 23% in Lodgepole after liquid reinjection,
highlighting the potential for hydrogen retention. Minimum miscibility pressure (MMP) testing
showed that hydrogen and crude oil remain immiscible under typical reservoir conditions, with an
MMP of approximately 16,355 psi at 233°F.

 Overall, the findings indicate that the Broom Creek Formation, particularly its sandstone
intervals, offers excellent potential for hydrogen storage, supported by strong sealing units and
favorable petrophysical properties. The Lodgepole Formation, while limited by matrix quality,
may still be viable due to fracture networks that enhance permeability. These laboratory results
provide essential data for simulation models and underscore the importance of lithofacies
variability and structural features in designing effective hydrogen storage systems.

 2.2 Geochemical Effects on Rock Formations

 A 30-day H2/rock/fluid exposure study assessed potential geochemical reactivity of
components comprising the geologic storage targets. These experiments focused on the Broom
Creek and Lodgepole Formations. The results of the H2 exposure studies provide insight regarding
the potential impact to reservoir properties including porosity, permeability, and changes to grain-
to-grain contacts and pore filling. Samples were taken from wells RTE 10, representing the
Opeche, and Knopik 1-11, representing the Broom Creek and Lodgepole Formations (Table 1).

 Geochemical batch reaction studies determined the potential for formation damage that may
result from the introduction of H2 in the Broom Creek or Lodgepole Formations. For the tests,
samples were cut to 7 × 20 × 12 mm and mounted in a custom-designed Teflon sample holder
(Figure 4). One surface of each sample was ion-milled to achieve the best possible polished finish
and remove the surface contamination for conducting geochemical reaction tests. To evaluate the
influence of H2 exposure on surface morphology and potential geochemical reactions that may
occur, three regions were selected for scanning electron microscope (SEM) analysis on each
sample’s ion-milled area. Subsequently, scanning electron microscopy images were acquired
before and after H2 exposure, ensuring the same location on pre- and post-exposure images. This
assessment compared the physical and mineralogical changes of the ion-milled surfaces before and
after exposure to H2.

 Each sample was inspected in its pre-exposure condition using a Hitachi SU-5000 field
emission SEM (FESEM) equipped with an energy-dispersive spectroscopy (EDS) system. This
inspection documented the physical properties of the ion-milled surface and provided a chemical
analysis to identify and characterize the material composition (mineralogy) and physical structure
of each sample, including mineral distribution, diagenetic relationships, and porosity
characterization.

 samples were chosen for the Lodgepole interval (a), and six were chosen for the Broom
 Creek interval (b).

 Two existing systems were modified for H2 testing to evaluate six Broom Creek rock
 samples in a 1-L reactor and two Lodgepole samples in a 0.5-L reactor (Figure 5). The samples
 from the Lodgepole, an oil-bearing formation, were saturated with Bakken oil before placement in
 the reactor, while Broom Creek samples were placed into the reactor presaturated with
 representative formation brine. Each reactor was then filled with synthetic brine prepared
 specifically to represent the brine present in each formation. The batch reactor systems were then
 set to operate for a reaction time of 30 days, with a pressure of 3000 psi of H2 gas for both reactors.
 The temperature for the Lodgepole reactor (0.5 L) was set at 230°F, while the Broom Creek

 8

  Figure 5. Illustration of the experimental setup used to conduct the reactor testing
  experiments: the Lodgepole reactor (0.5 L) is shown in the left image (a), while the Broom
  Creek reactor (1 L) is shown in the right (b).

reactor (1 L) was set at 160°F. Continuous mixing conditions were maintained to evaluate
geochemical effects. After the 30 days, gas samples were collected for analysis before bleeding
off the cell, and the remaining brines were gathered for chemical analyses. The gas samples
collected were analyzed using gas chromatography. As shown in Table 2, no significant changes
were observed in either the Broom Creek or the Lodgepole gas samples. Both reactors exhibited
gas compositions predominantly consisting of pure H2, with only trace amounts of nitrogen. This
is likely due to impurities in the H2 cylinder or residual N2 present in the gas-sampling bags.

 9

 2.2.1 Brine Chemistry Analyses

 Following the batch reaction, brine chemistry was analyzed for the Broom Creek and
Lodgepole synthetic brines before and after exposure. The analyses revealed significant alterations
in both formation brines (Figure 6). Both formation brines exhibited elevated levels of total
dissolved solids (TDS) due to the dissolution of specific minerals, as confirmed by SEM analysis
(Section 2.2.3) of surface morphology. Except for sulfate and magnesium, which exhibited distinct
behaviors in the two brines, nearly all other parameters exhibited consistent trends. In both brines,
elevated levels of chloride, sodium, calcium, and potassium were observed, while pH and
alkalinity parameters exhibited reduced values. Regarding the Broom Creek brine, elevated sulfate
levels were observed. Extensive dissolution of anhydrite cement was noted during SEM surface
morphology analysis. Consequently, a significant amount of sulfate was released into the brine,
potentially explaining the elevated sulfate values observed post-exposure.

 With respect to the Lodgepole brine, the precipitation of anhydrite was observed during SEM
surface morphology analysis, which may account for the observed sulfate reduction in the brine
following exposure. In general, the elevated levels of other cations and anions may be attributed
to the dissolution process, releasing these ions into the brine under the specific pressures and
temperatures of each reactor. Brine evaporation is another factor that can increase the overall
concentration of dissolved solids in each of the brines during the experiment. With respect to pH
levels and alkalinity, both brines demonstrate lower pH, indicating increased acidity and
diminished alkalinity. Notably, these alterations were more pronounced in the Broom Creek brine
(Figure 6).

 2.2.2 SEM Analysis

 Observation of the ion-milled surfaces of the samples listed in Table 1 before and after
exposure to H2 revealed significant dissolution and/or precipitation, particularly regarding
anhydrite dissolution in the Broom Creek samples (Figure 7, top). As a result of the H2–brine
reaction experiments, the Lodgepole samples exhibited various types of alteration, including the
dissolution of pore-filling carbonate (Ca/MgCO3) cement and anhydrite (CaSO4) cement.
Precipitation of salt (NaCl) cubes and anhydrite needle crystals were also observed in the
Lodgepole samples (Figure 7, bottom). Appendix B shows results of mineralogy, chemistry, and
SEM images for the Lodgepole Formation.

 10

  Figure 6. Brine analysis results for Broom Creek (top) and Lodgepole (bottom) brines before
  and after exposure to H2. The bar graphs are scaled to compare each individual ion and each
  group.

 Minerals such as calcite, dolomite, quartz, anhydrite, and pyrite are reactive with hydrogen
(Aftab and others, 2022). The results from this study confirm that the mineral reaction rate can be
significantly accelerated in the presence of brine. Flesch and others (2018) demonstrated that
significant changes in pore structure can occur in lithologies with high contents of pore-filling
anhydrite and carbonate cements due to geochemical reactions with hydrogen. Such a clear change
in the pore structures was noted in this study’s samples, with high contents of pore-filling anhydrite
and carbonate cements. The fundamental thermodynamic reaction between anhydrite and

 11

Pre-Exposure Post-Exposure

Figure 7. SEM images showing pore-filling anhydrite (CaSO₄) cement in study samples.
Left: pre-exposure; right: post-exposure to H₂–brine (Qz: quartz; An: anhydrite; K-Feld:
potassium feldspar; Cal: calcite). Top: Broom Creek Sample 3 (RTE 10 well); bottom:
Lodgepole Sample 8 (Knopik 1-11 well). Post-exposure images reveal significant anhydrite
dissolution (red arrow), open pore spaces, edge pits, partial/complete calcite dissolution, and
precipitation of halite cubes and anhydrite needles. See Appendix B for all samples.

 12

hydrogen entails the reduction of calcium sulfate by hydrogen, leading to the formation of calcium
sulfide and water (Fatah and others, 2024; Equation 1):

 CaSO4 (Anhydrite) + H2 → CaS + H2O [Eq. 1]

 Fatah and others (2024) emphasized that the reaction rate could differ depending on specific
conditions, such as temperature and pressure. Thus, the reactions would occur differently in each
of the reservoir types. This could explain the dissolution of calcite and anhydrite cement and
precipitation of anhydrite needles seen in the Lodgepole samples, while the Broom Creek samples
mainly demonstrated anhydrite dissolution.

 2.3 Effect of Hydrogen on Cement and Casing Materials

 Existing scientific literature was reviewed to identify the mechanisms of cement degradation
of casing materials under subsurface conditions during the H2 injection and withdrawal stages.
This information was gathered into an accompanying report of the laboratory data. The full report
with further detail and observations is included in Appendix C.

 Ensuring wellbore integrity is crucial for safe UHS; well integrity can also be compromised
by H2’s potential effects on materials used to construct the wellbore such as cement, casing, and
elastomers. Significant research has been conducted and published investigating such effects;
however, a thorough review of the topic is not publicly available. Therefore, a literature review
was conducted that compiled existing research and assessed any research gaps to improve the
mitigation of potential H2-induced impacts to wellbore materials during UHS operations. The
literature review was also performed to formulate recommendations for future work that would
address the research gaps identified. These findings are summarized in the following section.

 2.4 Laboratory Summary and Recommendations

 Two reservoir types, carbonate and siliciclastic formations—the Broom Creek and
Lodgepole, respectively—were selected to study the feasibility and storage capacity of H2. Results
of the laboratory activities suggest the following.

 2.4.1 Lodgepole Formation (carbonate H2–oil system)

• Twenty-two samples were tested from the Lodgepole Formation. Results of helium/nitrogen
  porosity and permeability align with the results of high-pressure mercury injection tests and
  indicate the reservoir quality is poor to intermediate. This is not uncommon for carbonate
  reservoirs. One aspect of carbonate reservoirs not captured in the assessment of porosity and
  permeability is the presence of natural fractures and their contribution to fluid flow. A literature
  review and core analysis provide evidence that this reservoir is fractured, thus overall reservoir
  permeability is expected to be higher than laboratory-derived data.

• The Lodgepole Formation demonstrated a relatively stable IFT. This rapid stabilization
  indicated minimal dynamic interaction between H2 and oil, suggesting a lower affinity of H2
  for the oil phase. Such stability may imply that H2 retains higher mobility in oil-bearing

 13

 formations, potentially affecting gas storage and retention differently from brine-bearing
 formations.

• The Lodgepole limestone exhibited relatively stable CA, suggesting consistent wettability in
  the presence of crude oil.

• Results of the 30-day H2 exposure show mineral dissolution with the development of edge pit
  structures in triangular or pyramidal shapes as well as partial or complete calcite crystal
  dissolution. Additionally, extensive precipitation of halite cubes and anhydrite needle crystals
  (red arrows in bottom-right image, Figure 7) was noted as pore-filling minerals.

• The post-exposure formation brine had higher TDS likely due to the dissolution of specific
  minerals, as confirmed by SEM analysis of surface morphology. Future study is necessary to
  confirm these findings.

 2.4.2 Broom Creek Formation (siliciclastic H2–brine system)

• The IFT results for the Broom Creek Formation indicated dynamic interactions between H2 and
  brine, likely due to diffusion and equilibration at the gas–liquid interface, until a stable
  equilibrium was achieved. The gradual decrease and eventual stabilization in IFT highlighted
  the impact of H2–brine interactions, which may promote gas-trapping in brine-saturated
  formations.

• The Broom Creek samples showed an initially high CA, indicating hydrophobicity, with
  gradual decreases over time, especially notable in the dolostone and tight sandstone samples.
  This observation along with the Lodgepole CA observation highlight the varying wettability
  characteristics between formations and lithofacies, which are critical for optimizing fluid–rock
  interaction in subsurface operations.

• The ion-milled surface before and after exposure to H2 showed significant dissolution and/or
  precipitation, particularly regarding anhydrite cement dissolution in the Broom Creek samples.
  Results suggest that the reaction rate can be significantly accelerated in the presence of brine.
  Further studies should be conducted to confirm and understand the extent to which the mineral
  reactions observed in this testing would extend into the anhydrite formations intended as sealing
  formations and the potential impacts on storage complex integrity.

• The post-exposure Broom Creek formation brine exhibited elevated TDS due to the dissolution
  of specific minerals, as confirmed by SEM analysis of surface morphology. Future study is
  necessary to confirm these findings.

 2.4.3 Review of H2 Exposure to Casing Materials and Wellbore Cements

 Results of the literature review suggest the following could improve the mitigation of
potential H2-induced impacts to wellbore cement, elastomers, and casing materials during UHS
operations:

 14

 • Sensor technology for real-time monitoring of cement integrity in UHS could trace the
 development of any potential voids or fractures within the cement seal. Given that H2–
 cement reactions are very slow, conducting experiments for extended periods are critical
 to understand the long-term impact, particularly to simulate multiple H2
 injection/withdrawal cycles. Therefore, comprehensive rock mechanics and rheological
 tests must be conducted on different cement class types at reservoir temperature and
 pressure conditions in a controlled environment (to minimize leaching and reactions
 unrelated to H2 exposure) in a laboratory-scale setting before initiating field trials.

 • Developing new materials that are more resistant to H2-induced corrosion is also critical.
 For example, high-strength steels, alloys, and coatings could be designed to resist H2
 permeation. In addition, more effective corrosion inhibitors to reduce the rate of corrosion
 could be developed.

 • In addition to developing new elastomeric materials, a system that can control the gas
 decompression rate in UHS target conditions could be developed for better durability of
 the UHS system. The risk of decompression increases with significant changes in
 temperature. Therefore, to further maintain wellbore integrity, any elastomer assembly
 used should be resistant to the risk of decompression to ensure longevity.

 • Wellbore casing investigations involving in situ H2 introduction (charging) under stress
 are necessary. Most of the H2-aided metal casing corrosion laboratory work is performed
 on H2-precharged specimens that only simulate a real-world scenario. H2-precharging is
 mostly performed electrochemically to rapidly introduce H2 into the casing material
 lattice before mechanical testing to simulate the effects of long-term exposure of the
 material under an H2 environment. However, the charging process during commercial
 operations is not consistent, and the conditions under which precharging is performed are
 generally not indicative of high-pressure operations.

 • The effects of different environmental factors such as temperature, pressure, pH, and the
 presence of other gases on H2-induced corrosion should be investigated to develop more
 accurate corrosion models and prevention strategies. Developing predictive models that
 can estimate the probability of H2-induced corrosion in different scenarios is critical to
 significantly improve safety and reduce the economic and environmental impacts of
 potential failures.

3.0 ENGINEERING ASSESSMENT OF HYDROGEN STORAGE

 Injection into subsurface reservoirs such as saline formations, depleted oil and gas reservoirs,
and engineered salt caverns offers a scalable and efficient method for large-scale hydrogen storage,
ensuring supply stability and energy security (Muhammed and others, 2022, 2023; Sambo and
others, 2022; Miocic and others, 2023). Moreover, large-scale storage is key to stabilizing
hydrogen supply chains, especially in regions aiming to expand renewable energy penetration and
achieve net-zero carbon goals. To fully realize the benefits of hydrogen as part of a future energy
portfolio, this engineering assessment was undertaken to evaluate the technical feasibility of
subsurface H2 injection, storage, and production in the Williston Basin, with a focus on saline
formations, depleted oil reservoirs, and salt caverns.
 15

 3.1 Simulation Models for Underground H2 Storage

 UHS leverages different geologic formations to meet various technical, economic, and
operational requirements. Each geologic formation has unique advantages and challenges as
summarized by various researchers (Muhammed and others, 2022, 2023; Sambo and others, 2022;
Miocic and others, 2023; Saeed and Jadhawar, 2023). Therefore, selecting the optimal UHS option
depends on project goals, storage capacity needs, operational flexibility, and site-specific geologic
conditions. For example, salt caverns are ideal for rapid response and short-term needs, while
saline formations and depleted reservoirs excel in large-scale, long-term energy storage. Balancing
cost, safety, and efficiency is crucial for designing reliable hydrogen storage systems to support
individual project conditions.

 The EERC has developed a variety of geologic models for saline formations and oil and gas
reservoirs for CO2 storage and/or enhanced oil recovery projects over the years. The first activity
in the engineering assessment involved selecting and building the H2 storage simulation models,
leveraging existing simulation models at the EERC, where appropriate, to assess hydrogen storage
capacities and recoveries of the formations identified suitable for gas storage in the North Dakota
portion of the Williston Basin.

 3.1.1 The Broom Creek Model for Saline Formation

 Prior petrophysical evaluations and rock physics modeling have been conducted in the
Broom Creek Formation to understand the variations in the petrophysical properties across this
formation, further aiding in assessing its suitability for gas storage (Smith and others, 2017;
Pothana and others, 2024). Additionally, EERC has developed extensive geological data sets in
support of numerous commercial CO2 storage in the Broom Creek Formation in North Dakota.
Therefore, the Broom Creek Formation was selected to model the UHS performance in saline
formations.

 The depositional model includes rock types representative of eolian dune migration, marine
transgression with partial reworking of dunes, carbonate mud deposition, and marine regression
with accompanying erosion. The formation reflects a dynamic environment with alternating
periods of terrestrial and marine influences (Rygh, 1990). In the modeled area, the formation lies
at depths between 6468 and 7398 ft. The sandstones within the formation exhibit high porosity
and permeability, interbedded with anhydrite and dolomite layers. The model has porosities of up
to 29.7% and permeabilities between 0.26 and 2488 mD, as shown in Figure 8. The model has
210, 210, and 110 cells in the X, Y, and Z directions, respectively. The dimensions of each cell are
1000 ft × 1000 ft in the X×Y directions and varied sizes in the Z direction. The Broom Creek
Formation is continuous in this part of the basin; hence, the reservoir model was set up to be
infinite-acting so the model’s boundaries do not affect fluid flow and pressure calculations. The
reservoir conditions exhibit a temperature of approximately 162°F and a calculated pressure of
3617 psi. A four-component (H2, CO2, N2, and CH4) equation of state (EOS) was developed and
used in the Broom Creek model. CO2, N2, and CH4 were added into the EOS as potential cushion
gases (CGs) to assess hydrogen recovery.

 16

 Figure 8. Schematic of the Broom Creek simulation model and permeability distribution.
 Model dimensions are approximately 40 miles × 40 miles.

 3.1.2 Stadium Field Model for Depleted Oil Reservoirs

 The Williston Basin hosts numerous depleted oil fields with decades of fluid production and
waterflooding histories. These reservoirs have demonstrated they can securely trap hydrocarbons
(HCs) over geologic timescales, suggesting potential for hydrogen containment. Effective
hydrogen storage requires impermeable caprocks to prevent gas migration. The Williston Basin’s
stratigraphy includes such sealing formations.

 The DLM are carbonate mud mounds containing oil and gas beneath Dickinson, North
Dakota (Gorecki and others, 2009; Knudsen and others, 2009). In this situation, rocks of the
Lodgepole Formation form both the porous and permeable oil and gas producing mound complex
as well as the sealing rocks that encapsulate the mud mounds. The mounds have an ideal geologic
structure for gas storage: the thick and tight Lodgepole Formation surrounding and above the
mounds reliably seals the stored gas, and the underlying shaly/tight Bakken Formation prevents
fluids from migrating downward. Jin and others (2020) selected the Stadium Field from the DLM
to evaluate the feasibility of using the field to store feedstock gas (mainly ethane and propane) for
a petrochemical plant in North Dakota. As part of that feasibility study, a reservoir simulation
model (Figure 9) was developed and calibrated with the historical production and injection data of
the field to evaluate the gas storage potential in the Stadium Field. This study used this model to
evaluate the feasibility of injection and recovery performance in the DLM for UHS in North
Dakota.

 17

 Figure 9. Simulation model of the Stadium Field.

 The EOS used for reservoir simulation was updated to include both H2 and H2S in the
composition table so it could simulate both UHS and potential geochemical reactions in the
reservoir as part of engineering activities. Table 3 shows the composition of the EOS model after
updating, which can simulate the possible interactions between H2 and reservoir fluids. Results
showed that the updated EOS can capture the phase behavior satisfactorily. Therefore, this updated
model was used to perform a detailed investigation of UHS in the DLM.

 Table 3. Composition of the EOS Model for the Depleted Reservoir Model
 No. Component No. Component
 1 H2 7 H2S
         2                CO2                   8                  IC4–NC4
         3                 N2                   9                  IC5 to C7
         4                CH4                  10                  C8 to C12
         5                C2H6                 11                 C13 to C19
         6                C3H8                 12                 C20 to C30+

 3.1.3 Dunham Salt Interval Model for Salt Cavern

 The North Dakota portion of the Williston Basin has bedded salt deposits in several
stratigraphic horizons (Nesheim and LeFever, 2009; Nordeng, 2009) including the Dunham, Pine,
Opeche, Charles, and Prairie Salt Intervals. The Dunham Salt Interval within the Piper Formation
consists predominantly of halite with some interbeds of reddish-brown mudstone and anhydrite
(LeFever and LeFever, 2005). The Dunham Salt Interval has a maximum thickness of 205 ft and

 18

extends over most of western North Dakota, with a few isolated lenses outside of the main salt
body. The Pine Salt Interval is the thickest of the three Spearfish Formation salts and has the
greatest area. The Pine Salt Interval reaches a maximum thickness of approximately 250 ft (Smith
and others, 2023). The potential salt caverns in these deposits will be different from conventional
domal caverns in the United States in several key aspects, including thinner bedded salt
accumulations resulting in smaller cavern volumes, greater depths, and higher cavern temperatures
and formation pressures. Thus, the salt caverns in North Dakota require horizontal expansion
during their construction to increase the diameter and create more volume for gas storage.

 This study specifically focused on assessing the feasibility of developing a cavern in the
Dunham Salt Interval to store H2 due to this salt exhibiting lower impurities within salt content
and more favorable geotechnical characteristics (Smith and others, 2020). A reservoir simulation
model was developed to investigate the parameters that are involved in the UHS process. The key
design components, including CG, debrining, injection and production, and long-term UHS
predictions, were considered. The model includes three formations: Poe, the overlying caprock;
Dunham, the salt interval for development; and Saude, the underlying seal, all characterized by
ultralow porosity and permeability, making them feasible for salt cavern development.

 A cylindrical geometry was chosen for simulating the cavern because it could closely mimic
the typical geometry of a leached salt cavern in a bedded salt formation (Cyran and Kowalski,
2021). The cavern was modeled at the center of the Dunham Salt Interval, with a salt thickness of
10 ft remaining both above and below the cavern. The modeled cavern had a diameter of 240 ft
and a height of 50 ft. The porosity and permeability of the cavern were set at 99% and 8000 mD,
respectively, to reflect the open container represented by the cavern. Figure 10 illustrates the
detailed grid setting as well as the cross-sectional and aerial views of the cavern in the model. The
reservoir temperature was 203°F at a reference depth of 6830 ft. Since the brine density could
widely vary when creating a cavern, a pressure gradient of 0.52 psi/ft was used to calculate the
reservoir pressure after leaching (Bérest and others, 2020; Khaledi and others, 2016a, b). This
pressure gradient was also used to develop 3D geomechanical models for the Dunham and Pine
Salt Intervals (Smith and others, 2023). The calculated formation pressure ranged from 3548.1 to
3620.9 psi in the model. A four-component (H2, CO2 , N2, and CH4) EOS was integrated into the
reservoir model to calculate the flow dynamics and gas interactions in the H2 storage and recovery
process.

 19

 Figure 10. Illustration of the reservoir simulation model for the Dunham Salt Interval:
 (a) cross-sectional view and (b) aerial view.

 3.2 Dynamic Simulation of UHS

 3.2.1 Simulation of UHS in the Broom Creek Formation

 A series of simulation studies were conducted using Computer Modelling Group Ltd.’s
GEM simulator to assess UHS injection and withdrawal performance. First, a baseline case was
set up with a well at the center of the model to test H2 injectivity into the Broom Creek Formation.
Based on the geologic model, a structural high may be present at this location and the formation
appears to have intervals of higher permeability compared to other locations in the model. These
characteristics make it easier to inject gas into the formation while minimizing the lateral migration
of injected H2. The target injection rate was set at 205 tonnes/day (tpd) (based on average estimated
H2 availability from a potential H2 generation facility in North Dakota, formation properties, and
operational constraints) with a maximum bottomhole pressure (BHP) constraint of 4765 psi, which
ensures the safety of the injection process, i.e., the injection pressure will not fracture the
formation. Based on reviews of existing UHS projects in the U.S. Gulf Coast region (domal salts),
an injection duration and production duration of 7 and 5 months, respectively, were chosen for
simulation. Finally, as part of the iterative simulation process, well placement was varied to
determine optimal storage in the study area.

 20

 Results showed that the injection rate gradually increased with the increasing gas saturation
around the injection well. A total of 42,389 tonnes of H2 was injected into the formation in
7 months. The injection well was then turned into a production well to withdraw the injected H2 at
205 tpd with a minimum BHP constraint of 500 psi. 25,908 tonnes of H2 were produced with
4,443,015 bbl of water in 5 months. 61% of the injected H2 was recovered in this 7-month injection
and 5-month production cycle.

 Different results were observed when applying the same injection–production scheme and
pressure constraints to a flat location with lower-permeability layers. 15,717 tonnes of H2 was
injected into the location in 7 months, and 7659 tonnes of H2 was produced from the same location
in 5 months, indicating that the H2 recovery would be around 49%. Since no H2 gas was present
in the saline formation before H2 injection, a considerable portion of the injected gas became
residual gas that could be difficult to produce back; the H2 recovery was low in the first cycle. The
H2 recovery factor rose with multiple injection and production cycles.

 Observations during this work highlight that permeability is more important for gas
injection, but structure becomes important for long-term recovery. Future studies will investigate
the impact of H2 injection and recovery scenarios constraining the simulation model to determine
impact of structure versus permeability. Figure 11 shows that the H2 recovery factors reached 70%
and 66% for wells completed in domelike and flat locations of the modeled area, respectively, after
10 injection–production cycles.

Figure 11. H2 recovery for wells completed in different locations in the Broom Creek Formation.

 21

 The residual gas saturation for the Broom Creek was 22% from the endpoint saturation
measurements via core flooding experiments conducted as part of the laboratory investigation,
which was integrated into this simulation work. This value is consistent with values in the literature
for residual hydrogen gas saturation in sandstone samples. For instance, Boon and Hajibeygi
(2022) reported a residual hydrogen gas saturation of 24%. With each successive injection–
production cycle, a greater portion of the injected H2 becomes trapped as residual gas due to its
migration through the formation. Therefore, this physical constraint limits the achievable H2
recovery factor, making it difficult to attain high recovery in the saline formation without utilizing
CG.

 3.2.2 Simulation of UHS in the Stadium Field

 The same simulation approach was adopted to investigate UHS performance in the depleted
reservoir. Because this is an active oil field, offset producing wells are in the model and are used
to produce reservoir fluids at a total liquid production rate of 16,000 bbl per day (bpd) to make
more pore volume available for H2 storage in the first cycle. At the onset of simulation, injection
pressure response was tested to make sure the maximum injection BHP did not violate the pressure
constraint under various injection–production scenarios. Based on the test results, to maintain
BHP, H2 was injected into the reservoir at 96 tpd through Well Prod-3 in the center of the field, as
shown in Figure 9. The maximum BHP constraint for H2 injection was 4400 psi, and the minimum
fluid production pressure constraint was 500 psi to maximize H2 injection efficiency while keeping
the reservoir structure safe. Because of the high average permeability (~1500 mD) populated in
the reservoir simulation model (high permeability is due to including natural fractures to history
match fluid flow), H2 can be easily injected into the reservoir at the target rate without exceeding
the maximum BHP constraint. The produced gas rate and composition were monitored to
understand the gas breakthrough behavior and prevent H2 loss from offset wells in the field.
31,984 tonnes of H2 were injected into the reservoir when gas breakthrough was observed in the
offset wells.

 In keeping with the simulations for the Broom Creek Formation, a 7-month injection and
5-month production schedule was used to simulate the long-term H2 storage operations in this
reservoir. The maximum H2 injection rate and BHP were constrained to 96 tpd and 4400 psi,
respectively, for the injection process. The maximum gas production rate and minimum BHP were
set at 145 tpd and 500 psi, respectively, for the production process. Results showed that up to 96%
of the injected H2 could be produced back by 500-psi BHP extraction after 10 cycles. The H2
recovery fell when increasing the minimum producing BHP to 2000 psi, especially in the earlier
cycles, as shown in Figure 12. At the end of the tenth cycle, the offset wells were converted to
water injection wells to displace the remaining H2 from the pore space toward the production well,
where over 98% of the injected H2 could be recovered after 6 months of displacement.

 22

  Figure 12. H2 recovery for the central well in the Stadium Field under two BHP constraints.

 Unlike storage in saline formations, the Stadium Field oil reservoir initially contained HC
gas dissolved in the oil. During the H2 extraction process, HC gas was released from the oil and
occupied pore space, contributing to the formation of residual gas. As a result, the H2 recovery
from the Stadium Field was higher than that from the Broom Creek Formation; however, over each
production cycle, the recovered hydrogen becomes diluted by a relatively small amount of HC gas,
reducing the H2 purity (Figure 13). The comixing is most pronounced during the first production
cycle and diminishes with each subsequent cycle of injection and production.

  Figure 13. Purity of the extracted H2 from the Stadium Field at a BHP constraint of 500 psi.

 23

 3.2.3 Simulation of UHS in the Dunham Salt Interval

 Because salt caverns are voids without an internal rock matrix for support, the designed
operating pressure is more critical compared to other potential UHS types. Two primary design
approaches were reported for gas storage caverns: variable-pressure and constant-pressure systems
(Foh and others, 1979). In a variable-pressure cavern, the internal pressure is determined by the
amount of gas stored. Defining and maintaining the maximum and minimum operating pressures
are critical to prevent structural failure, such as salt fracturing or cavern collapse. The operating
pressure limits, along with the injection and withdrawal rates and overall operational strategy,
impact the duration of each loading cycle, which can range from several hours to several months
(Khaledi and others, 2016a, b). In contrast, a constant-pressure design involves maintaining the
cavern partially filled with water. Pressure stability is achieved via a water column that
hydraulically links the cavern to a surface water reservoir. During gas withdrawal, water is
introduced into the cavern to replace the extracted gas. Conversely, during gas injection, the water
level decreases and returns to the surface reservoir through a vertical well that connects the two
systems (Foh and others, 1979).

 The variable-pressure approach was applied to evaluate the UHS process in the Dunham Salt
Interval. Figure 14 presents the potential operational workflow, highlighting key pressure levels
to illustrate their relative positions and significance within the cavern’s operating cycle. The
geostatic pressure means the hydraulic pressure when the cavern is created after leaching. The
maximum operating pressure (Pmax) in the cavern is limited to prevent formation fractures and gas
leakage, while the minimum pressure (Pmin)is set to prevent salt creep and cavern closure. The
debrining pressure refers to the pressure at which gas (either CG or H2) is injected into the cavern
to displace and remove as much of the residual brine as possible after the leaching and cavern
formation phase.

 Figure 14. Illustration of an operational process for UHS in the Dunham Salt Interval.

 24

 The brine pressure refers to the pressure on the ultimate brine–gas interface after debrining.
Based on the pressure determination method suggested by San and other (2023) and salt cavern
geomechanical properties (Smith and others, 2023), the key operating pressures and ratios were
calculated (Table 4). The maximum operating ratio is the ratio of Pmax to the geostatic pressure (or
overburden pressure) at the top of the cavern. CG ratio is defined as the CG volume (VCG) to the
total gas storage capacity (Vt) of a cavern. The values of the maximum operating ratio (0.60–0.85)
covered the plausible safe operating scenarios to maintain geomechanical stability in this cavern.
The calculation results showed that CG occupies more volume in the cavern when the Pmax
decreased because more CG was needed to keep the cavern stable.

Table 4. Key Operational Pressures and Ratios for UHS in the Dunham Salt Interval
 Maximum Operating Ratio Pmax, psi Pmin, psi CG Ratio, VCG/Vt
 0.60                                   3980             1990                   0.53
 0.70                                   4644             1990                   0.47
 0.75                                   4975             1990                   0.44
 0.80                                   5307             1990                   0.42
 0.85                                   5639             1990                   0.40

 The evaluation assumed that CG was injected into the salt cavern to expel brine and create
space for gas storage after leaching. When the pressure reached the designed debrining pressure,
H2 was injected into the cavern the maximum operating pressure was reached. CH4, CO2, N2, and
H2 were used as CG to perform a sensitivity analysis to compare their debrining performances.
The debrining rate was maintained at 4000 bpd with a constant BHP at 3600 psi. Results showed
that CO2 achieved the shortest debrining time of 33 days, whereas other gases ranged from 50 to
55 days. CO2 is denser than the other injection gases under bottomhole conditions, which may
enhance its ability to displace the brine faster. Using CO2 in the debrining process also resulted in
a larger volume of cumulative injected gas and a higher gas–water ratio on the surface. Simulation
results indicated that gas contamination was inevitable when alternative gases served as CGs.
Consequently, H2 was employed as both the CG and the working gas. The Pmax can impact the gas
storage capacity significantly since H2 is highly compressible. 802 and 585 tonnes of H2 could be
injected into the cavern of the designed size to reach the Pmax of 5639 and 3980 psi, respectively,
in the first cycle. The cavern’s smaller volume compared to the saline formation and the depleted
reservoir resulted in rapid filling of the storage space by H2 when injected at 7.2 tpd. Additionally,
the cavern pressure quickly declined to Pmin during the gas extraction process. The H2 recovery
achieved 94% and 91% at Pmax of 5639 and 3980 psi, respectively, following 10 cycles
(Figure 15). Nearly all the H2 (over 99%) could be recovered if water was injected into the cavern
to displace the gas at the end of the project.

 25

 Figure 15. H2 recovery from the Dunham Salt Interval under different maximum operating
 pressures.

 3.4 Study of Impacting Factors for UHS in the Williston Basin

 This section summarizes important findings about UHS risks in the Williston Basin.
Appendix D looks at how hydrogen interacts with brine and minerals in different types of
reservoirs. Appendix E examines the stability of the Dunham Salt Interval during hydrogen
injection, focusing on possible geomechanical failure conditions. Appendix F discusses the
findings from a literature review on how microbes might cause hydrogen loss and produce
unwanted by-products, highlighting the need for specific assessments and strategies. These studies
assist with understanding chemical, mechanical, and biological risks to support safe UHS in
various geologic settings.

 3.4.1 Geochemical Effects on UHS in Broom Creek and DLM

 Appendix D presents a geochemical investigation into UHS within the Broom Creek (saline
formation) and DLM (representing a depleted oil reservoir). Using PHREEQC-based batch
reaction modeling, the study evaluated abiotic interactions among hydrogen, brine, and reservoir
minerals under formation-specific conditions. PHREEQC is a geochemical modeling software
package developed by the U.S. Geological Survey. Experimental results revealed notable mineral
dissolution (anhydrite, dolomite, calcite), halite precipitation, and changes in brine chemistry,
including increased sodium and chloride levels. Two geochemical modeling approaches were
tested: redox-coupled and redox-decoupled. The redox-decoupled model more accurately reflected
the experimental observations, indicating minimal hydrogen reactivity and suggesting that
observed reactions were largely driven by brine–rock disequilibrium rather than direct hydrogen

 26

effects. The study highlights the importance of refining experimental protocols, such as pre-
equilibrating brine and rock and controlling gas impurities, to improve model calibration and
support reliable field-scale predictions of hydrogen stability and reservoir integrity during UHS.

 3.4.2 Geomechanical Modeling for UHS in the Dunham Salt Interval

 Appendix E describes a geomechanical modeling study focused on evaluating the short-term
stability of UHS in the Dunham Salt Interval, with particular attention to risks of tensile and shear
failures during high-pressure hydrogen injection. Using a modified Barton–Bandis model, the
study assessed fracture behavior and stress evolution in the surrounding salt and caprock
formations under various injection pressures. The base model, built from geologic and mechanical
data of the Poe, Dunham Salt Interval, and Saude Formations, demonstrated that at the
recommended maximum injection pressure of 5639 psi, the cavern remained stable without any
signs of tensile or shear failure. However, increasing the maximum injection pressure to 9000 psi
or higher led to localized shear failure at the cavern wall, although no tensile failure occurred, even
at pressures up to 10,000 psi. The analysis highlights that cavern sidewalls are particularly
susceptible to shear failure due to stress redistribution. The study also found that natural fracture
density plays a key role in tensile failure risk, with closely spaced fractures (e.g., 1-ft intervals)
significantly increasing the likelihood of tensile fracture opening. The report concludes with
recommendations for future laboratory testing to better constrain key geomechanical parameters
and suggests combining long- and short-term stability modeling to more comprehensively evaluate
salt cavern performance.

 3.4.3 Effects of Microbial Interactions on UHS

 Microbial activity poses a potential risk to underground hydrogen storage, with field projects
reporting hydrogen losses ranging from 2% to 4% in different UHS sites (Liebscher and others,
2016; Thaysen and others, 2023). Appendix F provides a comprehensive literature review on
microbial activity relevant to UHS, with a focus on understanding microbial-induced hydrogen
loss and its implications for storage quality. The review identifies key microbial groups, such as
hydrogenotrophic sulfate reducers, methanogens, and acetogens, that can consume hydrogen under
anaerobic subsurface conditions. It examines microbial processes, including sulfate reduction,
methanogenesis, and acetogenesis, highlighting their potential to convert hydrogen into undesired
by-products like hydrogen sulfide or methane. Factors influencing microbial activity, such as
temperature, salinity, pH, and nutrient availability, are discussed in detail, along with strategies to
mitigate microbial hydrogen loss, such as biocide treatment and salinity control. For example,
temperatures above 55°C and salinity above 1.7 M NaCl reduce microbial effects (Thaysen and
others, 2021). Considering these factors during site selection and operation helps reduce risks. The
report also summarizes findings from previous laboratory and field studies, noting that microbial
activity is formation-dependent and may be limited in certain high-salinity or high-temperature
reservoirs. Overall, the review underscores the importance of site-specific microbial risk
assessments and targeted mitigation strategies to ensure the effectiveness and safety of hydrogen
storage in subsurface environments.

 27

 3.5 Engineering Summary and Recommendations for Future Work

 This section summarizes the results of an engineering assessment that includes
geologic/reservoir model selection and updating for three types of formations, advanced fluid
characterization modeling, dynamic reservoir simulation, geochemical and geomechanical
analysis, and sensitivity studies. These activities characterized the performance, risks, and
sensitivity of hydrogen storage systems under a variety of geologic and operational conditions.
The outcomes of this work provide valuable insight into the key parameters for UHS design in the
Williston Basin. Based on the results, the primary conclusions are summarized as follows:

 • Salt caverns are best suited for fast-response, short-term UHS with minimal purity risks
 when using H2 as both CG and working gas. However, geographic limitations may restrict
 their widespread use.

 • Depleted reservoirs may have significant storage capacities and higher recovery factors
 and offer cost-effective H2 storage by leveraging existing infrastructure; however,
 additional efforts are required to manage gas purity and leakage risks.

 • Saline formations may provide significant potential storage capacity. However, saline
 formations have a lower H2 recovery factor without CG use and exhibit higher uncertainty
 because of a lack of geologic characterization and limited operational experience.

 • CG provides necessary pressure to maintain the safe operations of the bedded salt caverns,
 but H2–CG mixing should be carefully considered in the design and operational processes
 because of the relatively smaller thickness of salt caverns in North Dakota compared to
 domal salt caverns in other locations.

 To further advance the understanding and feasibility of UHS in North Dakota, several key
research and development tasks should be pursued:

 • Perform comprehensive techno-economic analyses incorporating market trends,
 regulatory frameworks, and potential tax or other financial incentives to understand the
 financial viability of UHS projects in different geologic formations.

 • Conduct more detailed experiments and simulations to optimize CG selection,
 minimizing hydrogen loss and maximizing storage efficiency.

 • Investigate long-term interactions among hydrogen, CGs, and geologic formations to
 assess risks such as gas migration, mineral reactions, and rock integrity.

 • Establish field-scale demonstration projects in different geologic formations to validate
 modeling predictions and improve operational best practices.

 • Develop cost-effective gas separation and purification processes to improve hydrogen
 recovery rates, especially in consideration of mixed-gas storage.

 28

4.0 BASINWIDE HYDROGEN STORAGE EVALUATION

 Studies have identified saline formations, depleted oil and gas reservoirs, and salt domes or
bedded salt formations as promising candidates for the large-scale storage of hydrogen (Epelle and
others, 2022; Muhammed and others, 2022). Although the Williston Basin in North Dakota
contains these types of geologic formations, none have been employed for UHS. Effective UHS
may take advantage of the diversity of the available subsurface environments to address the
varying technical, economic, and operational challenges associated with large-scale hydrogen
storage.

 In alignment with this objective, a preliminary assessment of hydrogen storage potential
within the Williston Basin was conducted to establish an initial basin-scale database to guide future
research on geologic hydrogen storage in the region. The results from the database and analysis of
the collected data will support subsequent technical evaluations and further long-term
commercialization of hydrogen storage within the region.

 To ensure consistency and continuity with the engineering assessment discussed in the
previous section, the database was compiled and organized according to the three types of
evaluated geologic storage targets: saline formations, depleted oil and gas reservoirs, and salt
caverns. The database incorporates characteristic data relevant to hydrogen storage, including
pressure, temperature, storage reservoir area and thickness, and other relevant geologic and
reservoir data. These data comprised the principal source for estimating hydrogen storage
capacities on the basin scale.

 The SHASTA-HELP (Subsurface Hydrogen Assessment, Storage, and Technology
Acceleration – Hydrogen Estimator for Logistical Planning) tool, a product of DOE’s SHASTA
research collaboration, was selected as the primary method for evaluating potential storage
capacity (Haeri and others, 2023; Lackey and others, 2023). SHASTA-HELP uses a volumetric
approach to estimate the potential to store pure hydrogen or hydrogen mixtures within different
types of subsurface formations or storage environments. The results produced by the tool are
intended for precharacterization or screening-level assessments such as the one performed in this
study, rather than detailed engineering or final design purposes. Given that SHASTA-HELP was
the primary method for evaluating potential storage capacity, the database format was designed to
accommodate the input requirements of this tool and subsequently tailored to address additional
needs. Additional data were also gathered to support the specific evaluation of potential storage
capacity (Section 4.2 –Basinwide Hydrogen Storage Potential) and combined with the results from
the modeling and simulation portion (Section 3.0 – Engineering Assessment of Hydrogen Storage).

 As a secondary method or complement to utilizing SHASTA-HELP, results from simulation
outcomes during the engineering assessment (Section 3.0) were used with volumetric calculations
to estimate hydrogen storage potential and discussed following the SHASTA results for each type
of reservoir in Section 4.2. Although the simulation models are tailored for site-specific
evaluations, they more effectively capture the dynamic behavior of hydrogen during cyclic
injection and withdrawal processes, thereby incorporating geologic and operational complexities
of the storage site. The simulation results are subsequently integrated with volumetric assessments
to extrapolate potential storage capacity from the site scale to basinwide. This integrated approach

 29

facilitates a more detailed and realistic estimation of hydrogen storage potential across extensive
geologic domains. The results from the two methods are compared to evaluate consistency and
support the discussion of their respective strengths, limitations, and implications for large-scale
UHS evaluation.

 In addition to the basinwide UHS assessment, potential H2 generation sources and uses were
identified and evaluated for their potential size and technical readiness. UHS facilities represent
important infrastructure that supports hydrogen energy regionally and nationally. The resilience,
reliability, and diversification that come from large-scale energy storage provide the bridge for
sustainable H2 production and market development.

 4.1 Hydrogen Storage Characterization Database

 4.1.1 Saline Formations

 The Broom Creek Formation is a continuous formation with varying depth and thickness
across much of western North Dakota. Stratigraphically, it is bounded above and below by
relatively impermeable lithologic units, a characteristic that enhances its potential for subsurface
gas storage (Peck and others, 2014). An illustrative example of hydrogen storage in a saline
formation is presented in Figure 16.

publicly available storage permit applications into a storage characterization database that can be
used as representative examples for the estimation of H2 storage potential for the Broom Creek

porosity of the Broom Creek Formation were used for the sites investigated in this fundamental
research study.

 4.1.2 Depleted Oil and Gas Reservoirs

 To represent depleted oil and gas reservoirs, complete data from five out of 10 oil fields that
have contributed to HC production from the DLM structures were compiled. This database
includes data from North Dakota Industrial Commission well files and published papers (North
Dakota Industrial Commission, 2025; Gorecki and others, 2009; Jin and others, 2020). The DLM
reservoirs exhibit a relatively tight rock matrix, characterized by low porosity and low
permeability. However, geological investigations conducted by Knudsen and others (2009) and
Gorecki and others (2009) have demonstrated the potential of the DLM for underground gas
storage, given the topographic structure and natural fractures in these reservoirs contributing to
permeability and fluid flow. Table 6 presents some of the key data required for SHASTA-HELP
from the DLM oil fields that were investigated. Reservoir pressure and temperature data were
unavailable for the other five DLM fields (Dickinson, West Dickinson, Duck Creek, Livestock,
and Subdivision), two of which (Livestock and Subdivision) consist of single producers.

 4.1.3 Salt Domes and Bedded Salt Formations

 Figure 17 illustrates structural characteristics of two main types of salt caverns used for H2
storage: salt domes and bedded salt formations. Salt domes are vertically extensive structures that
can span multiple geologic layers, while bedded salts are generally confined to a single
stratigraphic layer. The study by Smith and others (2023) showed that the presence of laterally
extensive and high-quality bedded salt deposits in North Dakota are promising for UHS
development. Among these, the Dunham and Pine Salt Intervals of the Piper and Spearfish
Formations, respectively, may have considerable potential for cavern development within the
Williston Basin. However, the mechanical properties of the Pine Salt Interval may limit the long-
term operational stability of storage caverns. The Dunham Salt Interval exhibits lower impurities
within salt content and more favorable geotechnical characteristics, rendering it more suitable for
commercial hydrogen storage, which was the reason it was selected for this project.

 Figure 17. Two types of salt cavern gas storage: salt dome and bedded salt formations (Smith
 and others, 2023).

 Parameters for evaluating salt cavern gas storage capacity, compiled in the characterization
database, include temperature, formation thickness, formation area, depth to the top of the cavern,
depth from the cavern center to zero pressure, minimum storage pressure gradient, and CG
fraction. In this study, for the SHASTA-HELP data requirement, multiple equally sized caverns
were assumed to be developed within the Dunham Salt Interval. Based on this assumption, the
relevant input data were compiled; they are presented in Table 7.
