 4.2 Basinwide Hydrogen Storage Potential

 Building upon the findings from the laboratory activities and engineering assessment, the
UHS potential was assessed for select formations in the Williston Basin. The hydrogen potential
storage capacity was estimated for each of these formations using two methodologies: 1)
SHASTA-HELP and 2) engineering simulation results combined with volumetric calculations.
Results estimated for the selected sites in the characterization database were extrapolated for
understanding the overall UHS potential across the entire basin.

 The determination of the CG fraction is a critical step in both methodologies for evaluating
UHS potential. In UHS systems, the total stored gas volume is typically divided into two
categories: working gas and CG. Figure 18 shows an example of these two gases in a saline
formation. Working gas refers to the volume of hydrogen that can be actively cycled in and out of
the storage system during commercial operations. In contrast, CG is the portion that must remain
permanently within the reservoir to maintain pressure, support structural integrity, and ensure
consistent withdrawal performance. This gas is not intended for extraction and is not recovered to
the surface during hydrogen production. However, CG plays an indispensable role in maintaining
the stability of salt caverns, improving hydrogen recovery, and sustaining the pressure regime
required for safe and efficient injection, storage, and withdrawal. The CG fraction varies
significantly with geologic setting and storage technology, with typical requirements ranging from
25% to 75%. Notably, CG can be one of the most cost-intensive components of underground
storage projects (Muhammed and others, 2023). This study assumes that pure hydrogen is injected
and stored, meaning that hydrogen also serves as CG to maintain purity. Because of hydrogen’s
low density and high diffusivity, a relatively higher CG volume is needed to maintain adequate
pressure and reservoir stability. Based on a review of existing UHS projects and practical

 34

 Figure 18. Schematic of UHS using CG in a saline formation (modified from Jin and others,
 2025).

operational considerations, a default CG fraction of 0.5 was adopted across the three geologic
formations evaluated. For saline formations and depleted reservoirs, this value represents a
reasonable minimum. For salt caverns, which generally require less CG for pressure maintenance
because of their favorable sealing characteristics and geometry, the CG fraction can be reduced to
0.4.

 4.2.1 Saline Formations

 Generally, H2 storage is similar in saline formations and depleted HC reservoirs in areas
where saline formations are overlain by impermeable seals (Tarkowski, 2019). However, the CG
requirements in saline formations are typically higher than those in depleted reservoirs, as saline
formations do not contain naturally occurring residual gas as in some depleted reservoirs, which
can partially offset the total CG volume needed (Muhammed and others, 2022; Mouli-Castillo and
others, 2021).

 Another parameter, storage efficiency factor (EF, ranging from 0 to 1), is defined within
SHASTA-HELP as the fraction of the pore space in the reservoir that can be effectively occupied
by gas (Haeri and others, 2023). More specifically, storage efficiency refers to how effectively
pore space is utilized for storage in a geologic formation, such as a saline formation (Bachu, 2015).
This is typically expressed as a percentage of the total pore volume of the reservoir that can be
safely filled and maintained with hydrogen. The basinwide area of the Broom Creek Formation
assessed in this study encompasses approximately 12.4 million acres.

 35

 Figure 19 compares basinwide UHS potential under various storage efficiency scenarios as
evaluated by SHASTA-HELP. The numerical values corresponding to this analysis are provided
in Table 8. The different colors in Figure 19 represent the sites of authorized CO2 storage facilities
within the Williston Basin as described in Section 4.1 – Hydrogen Storage Characterization
Database. To generate a range of H2 storage potential values based on characterization data from
each site, the areal extent of the Broom Creek Formation was used as the reservoir area. At a fixed
EF of 0.05, the total UHS potential exhibits variability across different sites, 1.7–5.5 MMt. An
increase in EF linearly increases H2 storage potential. At an EF of 0.2, the hydrogen storage
potential quadruples, ranging from 7 MMt (based on MAG) to 21.9 MMt (based on SCS1_TBL).
Notably, the total storage potential derived from SHASTA-HELP calculations highly depends on
the reservoir area. This means an expansion in reservoir size proportionally increases UHS
potential.

 Figure 19. Basinwide UHS potential based on SHASTA-HELP assessment of representative
 areas of the Broom Creek Formation (Table 8).

 The engineering assessment of the Broom Creek Formation simulated the same injection
and recovery program at two well locations in the model, one where the top of the formation has
high structural relief (and higher permeability) and one where the structural relief is essentially
zero (with lower permeability). Results of these two simulations were used to provide a range of
storage potential for the Broom Creek Formation. Simulations for the high-relief well indicated
that the amount of hydrogen injected over a 7-month injection reached 42,389 tonnes within a
plume area of 5839 acres (average storage 7.3 tonnes/acre). Approximately 25,908 tonnes of

high estimate for H2 storage in the Broom Creek Formation of North Dakota, approximately 1.5–
9 times the total H2 storage capacity of 399 existing underground gas storage facilities in the United
States (9.8 MMt, Lackey and others, 2023). However, technical considerations like those in other
gas storage projects, including identifying structural closures, surface location constraints, and
potential reservoir pressure buildup among CO2 and H2 storage facilities, are likely to substantially
reduce the practical storage capacity within the area of interest (AOI).

production values were obtained from existing data sources, while the H2 fraction was assumed to
be 1.0 (pure H2) and the CG fraction was set at 0.5.

 The discrepancy between these two methods can be attributed to two main factors. First,
SHASTA-HELP is primarily designed for evaluating depleted gas reservoirs. However, the DLM
fields are oil reservoirs, not gas reservoirs. As a result, SHASTA-HELP underestimated the
potential storage capacity of the DLM. Second, the simulation-based approach accounts for site-
specific reservoir characteristics and operational constraints, providing a more site-specific
estimate of hydrogen storage potential in depleted oil reservoirs. Considering both methodologies
and available data, the estimated UHS potential in the DLM fields ranges from approximately
68,129 tonnes (using SHASTA-HELP) to 187,110 tonnes (extrapolating from the Stadium Field
engineering assessment). For the same DLM field, SHASTA-HELP storage estimates ranged from
19% of the engineering assessment estimates at Hiline to 90% at Patterson Lake.

 4.2.3 Salt Domes and Bedded Salt Formations

 Salt caverns globally represent the most prevalent geologic reservoirs for H2 storage because
of their substantial volume capacity and rapid cycling flexibility. Currently, practical UHS
involving high-concentration hydrogen within salt caverns is operational at only four commercial
sites worldwide, one in the United Kingdom and three within the United States (Sambo and others,
2022). As previously noted, the Dunham Salt Interval was selected for this evaluation. To delineate
the principal distribution of the Dunham Salt Interval, prospectivity maps developed by Smith and
others (2023) were utilized. These maps categorize the Dunham Salt Interval within the regional
AOI according to varying degrees of prospectivity, with details of the mapping illustrated in
Figure 20. This study considers areas outside the AOI but within the Williston Basin unlikely
candidates for salt cavern development. Salt thickness serves as the principal criterion in
prospectivity evaluations: intervals 100 ft thick or greater are classified as highly prospective,
whereas those below 50 ft thick are excluded from consideration as viable prospects. Highly
prospective zones, represented in dark green on the maps, are spatially discrete, typically
encompassing areas with diameters ranging from 1 to 5 miles, and are primarily in the central and
northern portions of the AOI. The total area of the AOI examined for Dunham Salt Interval
prospectivity encompasses approximately 7.5 million acres, which includes approximately 0.4
million acres of high-prospectivity, 0.2 million acres of moderate-prospectivity, and 6.9 million
acres of low-prospectivity regions.

 A CG fraction of 0.4 was applied for the salt cavern storage scenario. The minimum storage
pressure gradient was based on the brine gradient of 0.52 psi/ft, which supports stable cavern
operations within the Dunham Salt Interval. Accordingly, the corresponding minimum operational
pressure derived from this brine gradient is 3553 psi; these parameters were obtained from the salt
cavern report by Smith and others (2023) and were utilized for the salt cavern simulation
evaluation described in Section 3.0 – Engineering Assessment of Hydrogen Storage. The

 39

 Figure 20. Prospectivity map for Dunham Salt Interval (Smith and others, 2023).

parameter “depth from the cavern center to the zero-pressure pressure” was calculated using these
data. Under these assumptions, the working gas mass of H2 in the high-prospectivity area estimated
by SHASTA- HELP reaches up to 2 MMt. The total H2 storage potential for the high-prospectivity
area is estimated to be approximately 3.34 MMt, assuming a CG fraction of 0.4. Similarly, for the
moderate-prospectivity area of the Dunham Salt Interval, SHASTA-HELP estimates a working
gas mass of 0.87 MMt, resulting in a total H2 storage potential of approximately 1.45 MMt. The
total UHS potential for the basinwide assessment is considered as the sum of the high- and
moderate-prospectivity areas, amounting to an estimated maximum of 4.79 MMt, as summarized
in Table 12.

 The Dunham Salt Interval storage potential was also estimated based on project simulation
results using the same combined high- and moderate-prospectivity area and the CG ratio of 0.6
used in Section 3.0. The results suggest that the total basinwide UHS potential is 0.6 MMt when
both high- and moderate-prospectivity areas are combined. The working gas mass accounts for
0.24 MMt, approximately 40% of the total storage potential, as shown in Table 12. The UHS
potentials using the two methods exhibit substantial differences. A significant reason for this
difference may be that SHASTA-HELP is primarily designed for salt dome formations, which

 40

Total                            2.87                4.79                  0.24                 0.6

  are more prevalent globally. In contrast, North Dakota only contains bedded salt formations.
  Consequently, SHASTA-HELP in this context will yield optimistic or overestimated results. The
  SHASTA-HELP manual acknowledges this limit (Haeri and others, 2023), as it specifies that the
  tool is intended for preliminary site characterization or screening-level assessments rather than
  detailed final design. Therefore, more comprehensive evaluations should be complemented by site-
  specific simulation modeling for greater accuracy.

 4.3 Basinwide Hydrogen Source and Market Potential

 Existing and potential hydrogen production in North Dakota is summarized in Table 13,
  showing up to 13.3 MMtpy may be possible. The Great Plains Synfuels Plant (shown in
  Figure 1) is the only H2 production facility in North Dakota at the time of this report. Although the
  facility mostly generates synthetic natural gas (syngas) from coal gasification, it also generates
  ~145 tpd of hydrogen for fertilizer production as well as other chemicals and fuels (Dakota
  Gasification Company, 2025). Atlas Agro’s Heartland Green Fertilizer project involves hydrogen
  production using electrolysis with a design capacity of up to 115 tpd (Heartland Hydrogen Hub,
  LLC, 2025) to make nitrogen fertilizer products for agricultural use. The largest potential for H2
  production in North Dakota is from conversion of natural gas: up to 12.7 MMtpy H2 using
  conventional steam methane reforming (SMR), assuming an industry transition to a H2-based
  economy (U.S. Energy Information Administration, 2025). An evolving future potential is new or
  constrained resources, such as new wind/solar electrical generation: up to 0.5 MMtpy of H2 using
  near-commercial or emerging electrolysis technologies (e.g., proton exchange membrane [PEM]).
  Appendix G details technical information on these technologies.

 Combined fertilizer production values from Great Plains Synfuels Plant and Atlas Agro’s
  Heartland Green Fertilizer project would be about 260 tpd, or ~93,000 tpy, of H2. Another potential
  near-term market is a small percentage (<5%) blended into natural gas pipelines, currently at
  commercial demonstration phase. Blended H2 in natural gas pipelines equates to an upper export
  market of 650,000 tpy of H2 based on current natural gas production rates in North Dakota. A
  future emerging market may be fuel cell transportation. Notably, North Dakota is a long-standing
  major exporter of energy resources (natural gas, crude oil, electricity, ethanol fuel, etc.); therefore,
  a majority of H2 production could be expected to be exported for national or global needs. UHS
  could be used for short-term storage of produced H2 (discussed in Section 4.2 – Basinwide
  Hydrogen Storage Potential).

 41

 4.4 Summary and Recommendations for Future Work

 This study represents the first basinwide assessment of UHS potential in the Williston Basin,
focusing on three types of formations: saline formations, depleted reservoirs, and bedded salt
caverns. Basin-scale datasets were initially created as a foundation for future research, technical
evaluations, and eventual commercialization of hydrogen storage in the region.

 Two methods were used to compare UHS potential and understand uncertainties: the
screening-level SHASTA-HELP evaluation and detailed site-specific simulations in combination
with volumetric calculations. Additionally, potential H2 sources and markets were cataloged.
Based on the results, the primary conclusions are summarized as follows:

 • For the Broom Creek saline formation, estimated basinwide UHS potential ranges widely
 depending on the method used. Using SHASTA-HELP to extrapolate characterization
 data from CO2 storage sites results in estimates of 1.7–21.9 MMt (EF between 0.05 and
 0.2). Extrapolation of simulation results with different structural and reservoir quality
 assumptions results in estimates of 7.4–90.5 MMt, up to 54.6 MMt working gas.

 • For the DLM reservoirs, estimated UHS potential ranges from 0.07 to 0.19 MMt, up to
 0.09 MMt working gas. Results differ compared with the previous method because
 SHASTA-HELP was designed for depleted gas fields, not oil reservoirs.

 • For potential bedded salt caverns in the Dunham Salt Interval, UHS potential ranges from
 0.6 MMt using site-specific modeling to 4.79 MMt (2.87 MMt working gas) using
 SHASTA-HELP, showing that the latter tends to overestimate potential for bedded salts.

 42

 • Across all three types of formations, CG fraction is critical for effectively managing
 storage capacity and should be a focus for optimization.

 • Potential H2 generation in the study area ranges from an existing operational source of
 53,000 tpy to an estimated 13 MMtpy using commercially available, demonstration, or
 pilot-phase technology.

 • Potential current H2 markets include fertilizer production or blended into natural gas
 pipelines for up to 750,000 tpy of H2 in North Dakota; although emerging technologies
 such as fuel cells may increase H2 market demand within the state, North Dakota is
 historically a major exporter of energy production.

 • The results show technical potential for hydrogen generation, storage, and use in the
 Williston Basin but also highlight the need for more site-specific data and modeling
 before commercial UHS development.

 Given the screening-level nature of this evaluation, further research and development are
recommended to refine these potential capacity estimates, address operational uncertainties, and
support commercial-scale UHS deployment in the Williston Basin:

 • High-resolution site characterization: Conduct 3D seismic surveys, well log analysis, and
 core sampling to improve the geologic resolution of targeted saline formations, reservoirs,
 and salt formations.

 • Refined reservoir simulation: Integrate multiphase flow modeling, geochemical
 interactions, and microbial activity assessments to predict long-term hydrogen behavior
 in subsurface conditions. Calibrate models using empirical data from existing gas storage
 facilities and experimental results.

 • CG optimization: Experiment with varying CG fractions through pilot injection tests to
 identify cost-effective strategies while maintaining operational safety.

 • Economics framework: Perform techno-economic assessments to evaluate the cost
 structure of each storage option in local and national hydrogen supply chains.

 • Pilot-scale demonstration projects: Select candidate sites for field-scale pilot testing to
 validate storage performance, recovery factors, and monitoring strategies. Use these
 demonstration results to bridge the gap between feasibility studies and commercial
 deployment.

5.0 CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

 The EERC, with project partners, conducted fundamental research to better understand the
potential for hydrogen storage and recovery in a variety of subsurface geologic formations in the
North Dakota portion of the Williston Basin. The Williston Basin comprises geologic formations

 43

with properties that are likely amenable to H2 storage and recovery, including saline formations,
depleted oil and gas reservoirs, and bedded salt formations. The research included a series of
laboratory experiments, field-scale engineering assessment, and a basinwide evaluation of
hydrogen generation, use, and underground storage (focused on the saline Broom Creek
Formation, the DLM, and the Dunham Salt Interval).

 Two reservoir types, carbonate (Lodgepole) and siliciclastic (Broom Creek) formations,
were found to be technically feasible for H2 storage via comprehensive laboratory studies (i.e.,
porosity, permeability, capillary pressure, and relative permeability data). The bench-scale
investigations of H2 interactions with rock formation and fluid samples generated results indicating
dissolution potential of rock minerals (e.g., increasing TDS). However, additional study would be
required to assess long-term effects of H2–fluid–rock mineral interactions on storage complex
integrity as the batch experiments were relatively short. Additional applied research is also needed
for well integrity beyond literature reviews on materials used to construct the wellbore such as
cement, casing, and elastomers regarding H2 injection and recovery cycling. All characterization
data of reservoir properties provided the input parameters to perform the reservoir simulation for
the engineering assessment of UHS.

 The engineering assessment indicated that while saline formations may provide significant
potential storage capacity, they may have a lower H2 recovery factor without CG use and may
exhibit higher uncertainty because of a lack of sufficient geologic characterization and limited
operational experience compared to the other formations studied. Depleted reservoirs may also
have large storage capacities (depending on their associated historical fluid production and
depletion) and higher recovery factors and could offer cost-effective H2 storage by leveraging
existing infrastructure. One drawback of depleted reservoirs is they may require additional efforts
to manage gas purity and leakage risks. Salt caverns are best suited for fast-response, short-term
UHS with minimal purity risks when using H2 as both CG and working gas. CG provides necessary
pressure to maintain the safe operations of the bedded caverns, but H2–CG mixing should be
carefully considered in the design and operational processes because of the relatively smaller
thickness of salt formations/caverns in North Dakota compared to domal salt formations/caverns
in other locations.

 The basinwide assessment of UHS potential determined varying ranges of hydrogen storage
potential across the selected formations using multiple methods (1.7–90.5 MMt for the Broom
Creek; 0.07–0.19 MMt for the DLM reservoirs; 0.6–4.79 MMt for the Dunham Salt Interval). The
basinwide evaluation also indicated that North Dakota has significant hydrogen generation
potential, with estimates up to a possible 13 MMtpy using commercially available, demonstration,
or pilot-phase technology, suggesting a substantial opportunity for hydrogen market development.

 The findings and results from this project demonstrate the technical feasibility of UHS in the
Williston Basin, highlighting varying storage potential across different varieties of formations in
North Dakota. Based on detailed investigations involving literature reviews, laboratory
experiments, engineering assessment, and basinwide evaluations of UHS, the research
recommends the following to continue pursuing the commercial viability of UHS for the Williston
Basin:

 44

 • Conduct detailed site characterization such as 3D seismic surveys, well log analysis, and
 core sampling to improve the understanding of targeted saline formations, reservoirs, and
 salt formations.

 • Perform comprehensive techno-economic analyses incorporating market trends,
 regulatory frameworks, and potential incentives to understand the financial viability of
 UHS projects in different geologic formations.

 • Conduct more detailed experiments and simulations to optimize CG selection,
 minimizing hydrogen loss and maximizing storage efficiency.

 • Develop cost-effective gas separation and purification processes to improve hydrogen
 recovery rates, especially in mixed-gas storage scenarios.

 • Investigate long-term interactions among hydrogen, well materials, CGs, and geologic
 formations to assess risks such as gas migration, reactions, and rock integrity.

 • Establish pilot- and/or field-scale demonstration projects in different geologic formations
 to validate modeling predictions and improve operational best practices.

 APPENDIX A

LABORATORY TECHNIQUES

 LABORATORY TECHNIQUES

 Laboratory work was conducted to accomplish project goals through 1) assessment of saline
and depleted oil and gas formations for hydrogen storage suitability and 2) characterization and
assessment of the effects of long-term H2 exposure on formation fluids, reservoir and confining
unit rocks, and wellbore materials. Laboratory activities included acquiring, analyzing, and testing
core samples from the targeted H2 storage formations shown in Figure A-1. Samples were obtained
from the North Dakota Geological Survey (NDGS) Wilson M. Laird Core and Sample Library at
the University of North Dakota. Formations identified for sample selection were based on several
criteria: existing data on Williston Basin formations suitable for H2 storage, availability and size
of core for sampling, proximity of wells with available core to existing numerical models, and
preference for wells containing reservoir and confining zone intervals available.

 A series of experimental activities investigated the physical properties of potential storage
sites and the effects of H2 on the geologic matrix and formation fluids. Lab activities included
measurements of the physical properties of caprock, storage reservoir, and underburden
formations; comparison of changes in core properties before and after H2 and brine exposure; and
inspection of geochemical reactions resulting from H2–rock–brine interaction in the samples. In
turn, the reservoir characterization data results of H2 exposure studies provided necessary input
parameters for reservoir-scale simulations. Based on these objectives, three reservoir types were
considered for detailed study: depleted oil and gas reservoirs, with a focus on carbonate reservoirs
which comprise the bulk of North Dakota’s depleted oil and gas reservoirs; siliciclastic (sandstone)
saline formations; and bedded salt formations for engineered cavern development and product
storage. Salt formations in the Williston Basin, such as the Dunham Salt in the Piper Formation,
were previously characterized by Smith et al. (2020), and to avoid redundancy, this project did not
include further laboratory analysis of these formations. Figure A-2 shows the extent of each
formation within the North Dakota portion of the Williston Basin.

 A-1

Figure A-1. Identified formations within the Williston Basin for potential H2 storage.

 A-2

 Figure A-2. Map showing the extent of the Broom Creek Formation and Dunham Salt
 interval of the Piper Formation studied for underground hydrogen storage (UHS) during
 this investigation. The Dickinson Lodgepole fields are shown in blue in the figure and are a
 unique geologic feature known as carbonate mud mounds. The Broom Creek is a
 siliciclastic saline formation, the Lodgepole is a carbonate reservoir currently producing oil
 in the complex known as the Dickinson Lodgepole Mounds and, finally, the Dunham Salt
 is a bedded salt with potential for cavern development for product storage.

Characterization of Properties and Fluid–Rock Interactions

 To evaluate H2 transmissivity and storage potential, measurements of absolute permeability,
effective porosity, interfacial tension (IFT), and contact angle (CA) were conducted under
reservoir conditions. These tests considered variables such as salinity, temperature, pressure, and
bubble size. Sensitivity analyses were performed to assess the impact of these factors on CA values
and the potential for altering in situ wettability of rocks in the presence of H2. The resulting data,
combined with newly generated high-pressure mercury injection (HPMI) test data, informed

 A-3

capillary pressure and relative permeability curves used in reservoir-scale simulations (detail
provided in Section 3.2 of the main report). Finally, vanishing interfacial tension (VIT) tests were
conducted to determine minimum miscibility pressure (MMP) for oil–H2 systems at potential
storage sites.

IFT Tests

 IFT for H2–brine and H2–oil systems was assessed using the pendant drop technique under
reservoir conditions for the Broom Creek and Lodgepole Formations, respectively. Reservoir
wettability and rock–fluid interactions were further evaluated using the sessile drop CA method,
specifically for the Broom Creek rock–brine–H2 and Lodgepole rock–oil–H2 systems.

 For the Broom Creek Formation, IFT measurements were taken by introducing a H2 bubble
into a brine-filled cell maintained at 160°F and 3000 psi. Similarly, Lodgepole Formation IFT was
measured by introducing a H2 bubble into a cell filled with crude oil at 233°F and 3000 psi. The
pendant drop shape and its evolution over time provided insights into IFT dynamics at these
reservoir conditions.

 The results for both formations are presented in Figures A-3 and A-4. Figure A-3 illustrates
the IFT behavior of the H2–brine system for the Broom Creek Formation, displaying the IFT
measurement over time with images of the pendant H2 bubble shape at selected time intervals.
Figure A-4 provides similar information for the H2–oil system in the Lodgepole Formation,
showing the rapid stabilization of IFT in oil as compared to brine.

 The IFT results in Figure A-3 indicate that for the Broom Creek Formation (H2–brine
system), IFT decreased significantly from an initial 61.8 mN/m, reaching 41.3 mN/m by 3 minutes,
40.0 mN/m at 5 minutes, and stabilizing around 35.7 mN/m after approximately 9 minutes. This
trend suggests dynamic interactions between H2 and brine, likely due to diffusion and equilibration
processes at the gas–liquid interface until a stable equilibrium is achieved. The gradual decrease
and eventual stabilization of IFT highlights the impact of hydrogen–brine interactions, which may
promote gas-trapping potential in brine-saturated formations.

 In contrast, the Lodgepole Formation (H2–oil system) demonstrated a relatively stable IFT,
starting at 13.2 mN/m at 0 minutes and quickly reaching equilibrium at approximately 13.1 mN/m
within 3 minutes (Figure A-4). This rapid stabilization indicated minimal dynamic interaction
between H2 and oil, suggesting a lower affinity of H2 for the oil phase. Such stability may imply
that H2 retains higher mobility in oil-bearing formations, potentially affecting gas storage and
retention behavior differently from brine-bearing formations.

 A-4

Figure A-3. IFT stabilization over time for the H2–brine system in the Broom Creek
Formation, with images of the pendant H2 bubble at 0, 3, 5, and +22 minutes, illustrating
H2 bubble shape changes at different time steps.

Figure A-4. IFT stabilization over time for the H2–oil system in the Lodgepole Formation,
with images of the pendant H2 bubble at 0, 3, 5, and +9 minutes, showing minimal bubble
shape change and rapid IFT stabilization.

 A-5

CA Analysis

 CA tests were conducted using the sessile drop technique to investigate wettability and rock–
fluid interactions for both the Broom Creek and Lodgepole Formations. For the Broom Creek
Formation, H2 bubbles were introduced on five different rock surfaces corresponding to distinct
lithofacies—dolostone, sandstone, tight sandstone, mudstone, and anhydrite—all surrounded by a
brine solution. The Lodgepole Formation analysis was performed on a limestone rock sample
surface surrounded by crude oil.

 Figure A-5 presents the CA results for both formations. The Broom Creek samples showed
initially high CA, indicating hydrophobicity, with gradual decreases over time, especially notable
in dolostone and tight sandstone. The Lodgepole limestone exhibited relatively stable CA,
suggesting consistent wettability in the presence of crude oil. These observations highlight the
varying wettability characteristics between formations and lithofacies, which are critical for
optimizing fluid–rock interaction in subsurface operations.

 A-6

HPMI Data Analysis

 To support numerical reservoir simulations for H2 storage in the target formations, a series
of HPMI tests were conducted. These tests provided valuable data on porosity, permeability, rock
type characteristics, pore size, entry pressure, IFT, CA, and pore throat distributions. This
information was used to convert the air–Hg system to the oil–H2 and brine–H2 systems and
generate capillary pressure and relative permeability curves, which are essential inputs for
performing reservoir simulations. Using the results of HPMI data analysis allows for the
construction of charts displaying the overall rock quality index (RQI) for each rock type tested. In
general, RQI charts display the results of the analysis to predict and visualize where the data will
plot in comparison to other samples. On the example plot shown in Figure A-6, RQI is plotted
against normalized porosity. For this example, multiple rock types are displayed and show that, in
general, the sandstone samples plotted in the upper right side of the chart have a high flow zone
indicator and are good hydraulic flow units (HFUs). These sandstone samples have larger pore
throats and elevated porosity and permeability. In contrast, each of the carbonate samples and the
siltstone plot in the lower right corner are interpreted as low-quality reservoir rocks with small
pore throat sizes and low porosity.

 Figure A-6. Example plot showing how reservoir quality can be interpreted using data
 derived from HPMI testing.

 A-7

 The capillary entry pressure values applied in the geologic model to be used in numerical
simulation were determined by deriving a ratio between the RQI of the core sample from HPMI
data and simulation model properties to scale the capillary entry pressure value derived from the
core plug using the formula in Equation 1:

 Lodgepole Formation HPMI Results

 HPMI tests were conducted on 22 samples taken from the Lodgepole Formation. Samples
were sourced from the Knopik 1-11 well (North Dakota Industrial Commission [NDIC] No.
13715) and the Patterson 1-24 well (NDIC No. 13751). Table A-1 provides a summary of porosity
(%) and Swanson permeability (mD), with the calculated RQI, normalized porosity (v/v) and flow
zone indicator (FZI). Plotting the RQI of the selected rock types as a function of normalized
porosity with the addition of the FZI/HFU into the plot highlights the characteristics of the
injection zone. The data showed that the formation may be generally characterized as poor to
intermediate reservoir quality, with low porosity and very low permeability. As shown on the
figure, three distinct trends are seen that demonstrate the samples plot between HFU 4 and the
HFU 5. Samples in the HFU zone may be considered poor reservoir quality, while the remaining
samples shown are considered intermediate in quality (Figure A-7).

 A-8

 Figure A-7. RQI for limestone facies of the Lodgepole Formation. Samples from depths of
 9793.4, 9806, and 9972.3 due to their very low porosity and RQI (see Table A-1). Black ovals
 show three groupings and may demonstrate reservoir quality from poor to intermediate.

 In the absence of an oil sample from the Lodgepole, a substitute oil sample from the Bakken
reservoir was selected and used for the IFT analysis as the Lodgepole is commonly believed to be
sourced from the Bakken Formation. The measured oil density was determined to be 0.774 g/cm3
at a temperature of 160°F. The laboratory oil–H2 capillary pressure was calculated from the air–
Hg capillary pressure using the relationship considering the IFT and CA measured in the laboratory
under reservoir conditions (temperature and pressure) (Table A-2). The IFT of 15.4 mN/m and CA
of 153° were used in the calculations and conversion from the air–Hg system to the oil–H2 system.
These values are direct inputs into the empirical calculation of relative permeability in the absence
of flow through experimental data.

 Figures A-8 and A-9 show the results of capillary entry pressure analysis. Figure A-8 shows
the data as received and plotted using the laboratory-derived air–Hg system, while Figure A-9
shows the interpreted data after converting from air–Hg to an oil–H2 system as is being modeled
in this study. The data presented reiterate the poor to intermediate rock quality as shown in the
RQI assessment. These data generally suggest that incrementally higher pressure will be needed
to access new pore throats (smaller diameter) within the reservoir as injection and subsequent
production of H2 occurs. Stated another way, larger pore throats shown in Figure A-9 suggest that
the samples plotted between injection pressures of 6 and 10 psi will likely dominate flow in this
system.

 It should be noted that these test data and associated interpretations are not always a true
representation of how a reservoir may perform. The rocks tested in this program are not out of the
ordinary when looking at the complexity of carbonate geology. Carbonate samples are typically
lower in porosity and permeability, which will inherently result in the perception of a reduction in
reservoir quality. In this case and as noted previously, the samples selected here are from the
Lodgepole Mound complex in western North Dakota. This collection of oil fields is known to be
a prolific oil producer, even with a lesser quality rock when compared to clastic reservoirs. The
notable difference between the laboratory data discussed in this section and the actual field
performance is the known presence of natural fractures within the reservoir. These fractures greatly
enhance fluid flow and contribute to the overall high oil productivity of this system. Permeability
modifiers are used to account for the natural fracture network improving the total permeability of
the system. This is discussed further in Section 3.0 of the main report.

were identified as comprising the reservoir intervals. Table A-3 shows each rock type, sample
depth, estimated temperature and pressure, and measured values for porosity and permeability.

 The capillary entry pressure values applied in the model were determined by deriving a ratio
 between the RQI of core samples of the modeled region from HPMI data and modeled properties
 to scale the capillary entry pressure value derived from core testing using the formula in
 Equation 1. HPMI data analysis confirmed that porosity, permeability, pore throat size, and pore
 radius vary from one rock type to another. In the Broom Creek Formation, the porosity ranged
 between 2.3% and 24.3% and permeability between 0.000006 and 510 mD. The median pore throat
 radius varied between 0.00537 and 10.1 µm and the pore radius ranged between 0.0292 and
 42.3 µm.

 Sandstone samples demonstrated excellent reservoir characteristics, making it a high-quality
 storage medium. There was a marked increase in cumulative pore space at larger pore throat sizes
 (meso- and macropore range), extending up to 10 µm, contributing to higher permeability and
 better reservoir quality in sandstone. Mudstones and anhydrites predominantly exhibited nano-
 sized pore throats, resulting in lower permeability, which exhibited strong sealing properties,
 effectively serving as barriers. Dolostone and tight sandstone, with their low reservoir quality,
 could function as impermeable baffles within the Broom Creek Formation, restricting fluid flow
 and enhancing the integrity of the formation.

 Table A-5 and Figure A-11 present further data that reinforce each rock type’s observed
 characteristics. Mudstone and anhydrite exhibited high to very high entry pressures, confirming
 their effectiveness as upper and lower seals, respectively. Tight sandstone and dolostone showed

average entry pressures, reinforcing information that points to effective baffles within the
formation. In contrast, sandstone displayed very low entry pressures for both air–Hg and brine–H2
systems, attributed to its large pore throat radius. The variations in pore throat and pore sizes
significantly influenced the petrophysical properties and, consequently, the entry pressures across
different rock types.

 A-15

Capillary Pressure and Relative Permeability Analysis

 Laboratory coreflooding tests on rock samples were performed, and relative permeability
curves were determined for different H2–water systems. These core-scale experiments provide an
understanding of flow behavior of H2 in storage formations as well as an assessment of the sealing
ability of the caprock. With the combination of measurements obtained for IFT, CA from the H2–
water system, and HPMI measurements, a model for capillary pressure was derived for the water
saturation range for the H2–water system. The developed dataset of petrophysical properties and
rock–fluid interactions was applied to reservoir-scale simulation for the development of subsurface
H2 storage (Section 3.2 of the main report, Dynamic Simulations of UHS, provides further detail).

Relative Permeability and Capillary Pressure Curves

 The endpoint saturation points and HPMI data for both the Lodgepole and Broom Creek
samples supported the generation of relative permeability and capillary pressure curves for the
rock types represented, which are essential for the reservoir simulation process (Section 3.0 of
main report, Engineering Assessment of Hydrogen Storage, provides further detail) (Figures A-12
and A-13).

 Figure A-12. Relative permeability for the limestone facies of the Lodgepole Formation.

 A-16

 Figure A-13. Relative permeability for the rock types of the Broom Creek Formation.

 Endpoint Saturation Measurement and Relative Permeability Evaluation

 Gas–liquid relative permeability was evaluated for both the Broom Creek and Lodgepole
Formations through a series of flow-through tests conducted at reservoir conditions. Because of
the high flammability of H2 and the elevated temperature and pressure conditions of the
experiments, safety considerations necessitated the use of a proxy gas. While nitrogen and methane
are commonly used as proxies for H2 in the literature, helium was chosen in this study because of
its similar molecular size and inert nature (Rezaei et al., 2022; Al-Yaseri et al., 2022; Thaysen et
al., 2023). Initially, the rock samples were fully saturated with liquid. Helium was then injected to
determine irreducible liquid saturation. This was followed by reinjecting the liquid to assess
residual gas saturation. Saturation profiles were derived by integrating data from effluent volumes,
mass balance, and measurements of pressure and flow rate.

 Table A-6 presents the properties of the selected rock samples from the Broom Creek and
Lodgepole Formations, highlighting variations in porosity and permeability between the two
formations. Table A-7 outlines the endpoint results, illustrating the fluid displacement behavior.
In the Broom Creek sample, helium injection lowered liquid saturation from 100% to 44%,
indicating significant gas penetration. After the second brine injection, residual gas saturation
reached 22%, signifying the portion of gas retained within the rock. For the Lodgepole sample,
helium injection reduced liquid saturation to 37%, with residual gas saturation of 23% following
reinjection with oil. These experimental results provided essential input data for generating relative
permeability curves, aiding in the characterization of fluid flow properties under reservoir
conditions.

MMP Testing

 H2 and crude oil miscibility behavior was evaluated at a Lodgepole Formation reservoir
temperature of 233°F using the capillary rise/VIT method described in Hawthorne et al. (2016).
Three capillary tubes with internal diameters of 1.5, 1.12, and 0.68 mm were placed inside a visual
cell partially filled with crude oil. H2 was injected into the cell incrementally, and the H2–oil
meniscus was observed at each pressure step. As pressure increased, the fluids approached the
miscibility point, leading to a reduction in the meniscus level and a decrease in crude oil capillary
height.

 Capillary heights were recorded at each pressure step, and the data were plotted and
extrapolated to determine MMP. The analysis revealed that at 233°F, the MMP between H2 and
crude oil was approximately 16,355 psi, as shown in Figure A-14.

 APPENDIX B

LABORATORY INVESTIGATION –
 MINERALOGY

 LABORATORY INVESTIGATION – MINERALOGY

 Laboratory work for mineralogical composition via powder X-ray diffraction (XRD), X-ray
fluorescence (XRF), thin-section analysis, and scanning electron microscopy (SEM) was
conducted to accomplish project goals through 1) assessment of saline and depleted oil and gas
formations for hydrogen storage suitability and 2) characterization and assessment of the effects
of long-term H2 exposure on reservoir and confining unit rocks. Laboratory activities included
acquiring, analyzing, and testing core samples from the targeted H2 storage formations. Samples
were obtained from the North Dakota Geological Survey (NDGS) Wilson M. Laird Core & Sample
Library at the University of North Dakota. Formations identified for sample selection were based
on several criteria: existing data on Williston Basin formations suitable for H2 storage, availability
and size of core for sampling, proximity of wells with available core to existing numerical models,
and preference for wells containing reservoir and confining zone intervals available.

 A series of experimental activities investigated the physical properties of samples from
potential storage sites and the effects of H2 on the rock matrix. Lab activities included thin-section
analysis; XRD and XRF measurements of the physical properties of caprock, storage reservoir,
and underburden formations; and comparison of changes in core properties before and after H2 and
brine exposure via SEM. In turn, the reservoir characterization data results of H2 exposure studies
provided necessary input parameters for reservoir-scale simulations and thus insight regarding
concerns over H2 injectivity. Based on these objectives, three reservoir types were considered for
detailed study: depleted oil and gas reservoirs, with a focus on carbonate reservoirs, which the bulk
of North Dakota’s depleted oil and gas reservoirs are; siliciclastic (sandstone) saline formations;
and bedded salt formations for engineered cavern development and product storage. Salt
formations in the Williston Basin, such as the Dunham Salt in the Piper Formation, were previously
characterized by Smith et al. (2020). The Broom Creek Formation was selected as a representative
siliciclastic reservoir because extensive data sets (XRD, XRF, and thin sections) were available for
reference (Sorensen et al. [2009] and Pothana et al. [2024]). Broom Creek Formation samples that
were previously acquired from RTE 10 (NDIC# 37229) were selected for this study to represent
facies observed in Broom Creek Formation cores to perform geochemical batch reaction tests to
determine the potential for geochemical reactivity during H2 storage. To avoid redundancy in
mineralogic studies, this project only included SEM work performed on the Broom Creek samples
that were exposed to geochemical batch reactions and did not include further laboratory analysis
of the salt formations.

Lodgepole Formation at Knopik 1-11 Well and Patterson 1-24 Well

 Twenty-two samples from the Lodgepole Formation (six samples from the Knopik 1-11 well
and 16 samples from the Patterson 1-24 well) were analyzed by XRD and XRF.

 Bulk composition analysis of six finely ground, homogenized core samples from the
Lodgepole Formation at the Knopik 1-11 well show carbonate as the dominant mineral (98%,
mostly calcite). Minor amounts of clay (0.9%), quartz (0.2%), sulfate (0.4%), halide/sulfide
(0.2%), and oxide/hydroxide (0.3%) make up the rest of the mineralogy (Figure B-1a). The major
mineral constituents of the Lodgepole Formation at the Knopik 1-11 well are shown in Table B-1.

These results align with the average elemental composition obtained by XRF, which shows Ca as
the dominant element (Figure B-1b). The higher content of “others” in the XRF bar chart (~40%)
in Figure B-1b is due to the presence of carbonates in the sample, which is not determinable by
XRF.

 XRF analysis of the Lodgepole Formation at the Knopik 1-11 well shows a high percentage
of CaO (up to 59%), indicating the presence of calcite as the dominant mineral (Figure B-2a and
B-2b). A thin-section micrograph shows the rock matrix mainly comprised of crinoid fragments
along with brachiopod/bivalve shell fragments, bryozoans, ostracods, and other bioclasts
(Figure B-3). The observed total porosity ranges from 2.04% to 9.50%, with an average of 4.48%
(Figure B-2b) with inter- and intraparticle-type porosity. Figure B-2 shows changes in mineralogy
as a function of depth next to the core sample porosity and permeability data. Clay content is
significantly low, averaging 0.9% (Table B-1).

at the Knopik 1-11 well as a function of depth by a) XRF and b) XRD in comparison to core
sample total porosity (%) and permeability (mD).

 Bulk composition analysis of 16 finely ground, homogenized core samples from the
Lodgepole Formation at the Patterson 1-24 well also shows carbonate as the dominant mineral

observed at Knopik 1-11 well. Minor amounts of feldspar (0.5%), sulfate (0.2%), halide/sulfide

mineral constituents of the Lodgepole Formation at the Patterson 1-24 well are shown in

shows Ca as the dominant element followed by Si. The higher content of “others” in the XRF bar
chart (~35%) in Figure B-4b is due to the presence of carbonates in the sample, which is not
determinable by XRF.

 Samples 1 and 2 (Table B-3) from the RTE 10 well were both from the Opeche Formation
  and experienced visible alterations. Sample 1 (Figure B-5) had extensive dissolution of anhydrite

  disappeared due to the loss of the connecting cement. The staircase structure, indicated by the red
  arrow in the right image of Sample 1 in Figure B-5, can be observed in the remaining anhydrite
  cement. The total dissolution of anhydrite cement is also documented in this sample.

 Sample 2 (Figure B-5) primarily consisted of anhydrite, with small crystals of dolomite and
  a few quartz grains. Significant dissolution was observed in this sample after exposure to H2–brine,
  and the ion-milled area was hardly recognizable in the post-exposure sample, as shown in the right

  shapes.

  resulting in an open pore space (right image of Figure B-5). The staircase structure, indicated by
  the red arrow in the right image, can be observed in the remaining anhydrite cement.

 Sample 4 (Figure B-5) also showed considerable dissolution of anhydrite cement after
  exposure, leading to an open pore space. The red arrows in the right image indicate the staircase
  structure, which is visible in the remaining anhydrite cement.

 Sample 5 (Figure B-5) primarily consists of quartz, feldspar, and dolomite, which acted as
  the cementing agent. The exposure of this sample to hydrogen and brine resulted in substantial
  dissolution of dolomitic cement. This dissolution created pore space, which was subsequently
  filled by extensive precipitation of halite (NaCl; red arrows in right image of Figure B-5).

 Sample 6 (Figure B-5) was mainly composed of dolomite and some quartz. The primary
outcome of the H2–brine reaction in this sample was dissolution, resulting in the formation of edge
pits with triangular and pyramidal shapes (as indicated by the red arrows in the image on the right
of Figure B-5). Additionally, some precipitation of halite (NaCl) was documented in this sample.

 Samples 7 and 8 (Table B-3) were limestone samples from the Knopik 1-11 well,
representing the Lodgepole Formation. The dissolution footprints in the limestone of Sample 7
(Figure B-5) are visible across the ion-milled area in this sample as the edge pit structure that
developed in triangular/pyramidal shapes. Furthermore, extensive precipitation of halite cubes and
anhydrite needle crystals was observed as pore-filling minerals (red arrows in right image).

 In Sample 8 (Figure B-5), the dissolution was observed as the development of edge pit
structures in triangular or pyramidal shapes as well as partial or complete calcite crystal
dissolution. Additionally, extensive precipitation of halite cubes and anhydrite needle crystals (red
arrows in right image) was noted as pore-filling minerals.

 ii

 INVESTIGATION OF HYDROGEN EFFECTS ON WELL MATERIALS

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

 Ensuring wellbore integrity is crucial for safe underground hydrogen storage (UHS), and
well integrity can be compromised by the potential effects of hydrogen (H2) on materials used to
construct the wellbore such as cement, casing, and elastomers. Significant research has been
conducted and published investigating such effects; however, a thorough review on the topic is not
publicly available that compiles existing research and assesses any research gaps to help advance
toward improving the mitigation of potential H2-induced impacts to wellbore materials during
UHS operations.

 The Energy & Environmental Research Center (EERC), in partnership with the U.S.
Department of Energy (DOE), is conducting fundamental research to better understand the
potential for H2 storage and recovery in a variety of subsurface geologic formations located in the
Williston Basin of western North Dakota. As part of this study, a literature review was conducted
to review existing research investigating potential H2 effects on well materials (cement, casing,
and elastomers) and assess research gaps, with the primary goal of making recommendations for
future work.

 Results of the literature review suggest the following could improve the mitigation of
potential H2-induced impacts to wellbore cement, elastomers, and casing materials during UHS
operations:

 • Integration of sensor technology for real-time monitoring of cement integrity in UHS
 could be beneficial to trace the development of any potential voids or fractures within the
 cement seal. Given that H2–cement reactions are very slow, it is critical to conduct
 experiments for extended periods to understand the long-term impact, particularly to
 simulate multiple H2 injection/withdrawal cycles. Therefore, it is essential to conduct
 comprehensive rock mechanics and rheological tests on different cement class types at
 reservoir temperature and pressure conditions in a controlled environment (to minimize
 leaching and reactions unrelated to H2 exposure) in a laboratory-scale setting before
 initiating field trials.

 • Developing new materials that are more resistant to H2-induced corrosion is also critical.
 For example, high-strength steels, alloys, and coatings could be designed to resist H2
 permeation. In addition, more effective corrosion inhibitors to reduce the rate of corrosion
 could be developed.

 • In addition to developing new elastomeric materials, a system that can control the gas
 decompression rate in UHS target conditions could be developed for better durability of
 the UHS system. The risk of decompression increases with significant changes in
 temperature. Therefore, in order to further maintain wellbore integrity, any elastomer
 assembly used should be resistant to the risk of decompression to ensure longevity.

 iii

• Wellbore casing investigations involving in situ H2 charging under stress are necessary.
  Most of the H2-aided metal casing corrosion laboratory work is performed on H2-
  precharged specimens that are only a simulation of a real-world scenario. However, the
  charging process during commercial operations is not consistent, and the conditions under
  which precharging is performed are generally not indicative of high-pressure operations.

• Investigating the effects of different environmental factors such as temperature, pressure,
  pH, and the presence of other gases on H2-induced corrosion should be considered to
  develop more accurate corrosion models and prevention strategies. Developing predictive
  models that can estimate the probability of H2-induced corrosion occurring in different
  scenarios is critical to significantly improve safety and reduce the economic and
  environmental impacts of potential failures.

 iv

 INVESTIGATION OF HYDROGEN EFFECTS ON WELL MATERIALS

BACKGROUND

 The Energy & Environmental Research Center (EERC), in partnership with the U.S.
Department of Energy (DOE), is conducting fundamental research to better understand the
potential for hydrogen (H2) storage and recovery in a variety of subsurface geologic formations
located in the Williston Basin of western North Dakota. The Williston Basin comprises geologic
formations with properties that are likely amenable to H2 storage and recovery, including depleted
oil and gas reservoirs, bedded salt formations, and saline formations. The goal of the “Williston
Basin Resource Study for Commercial-Scale Subsurface Hydrogen Storage” project is to support
the future commercialization of H2 generation, storage, and use by assessing the potential for high-
volume, secure, subsurface H2 storage with concomitant high recovery from geologic complexes
of the North Dakota portion of the Williston Basin.

 As part of the overall “Williston Basin Resource Study for Commercial-Scale Subsurface
Hydrogen Storage” project, under Subtask 2.4 – Effect of Hydrogen on Wellbore Cement and
Casing Materials, a literature review was conducted. The main goal and objectives of this literature
survey were to review existing research investigating potential H2 effects on well materials and
mechanisms that can be responsible for loss of wellbore integrity, make recommendations for
future work by assessing any research gaps, and provide strategies for maintaining wellbore
integrity, specifically examining:

 1. The effects of H2 on wellbore cement as one of the main hydraulic barriers that plays a
 key containment role in supporting steel casings and blocking undesired fluid flow
 between the wellbore and the formation from the fluid column to the target depth.

 2. The effects of H2 on elastomers as high polymeric organic networks capable of absorbing
 large deformations reversibly and possibly important components of seal and barrier
 elements for underground H2 storage (UHS) applications.

 3. The effects of H2 on casing materials since low- and high-alloy steels typically used in
 well casings are susceptible to corrosion, H2 embrittlement, H2 blisters, H2-induced
 cracking, and sulfide stress cracking in the presence of H2 or other gases formed
 underground.

APPROACH

 The objective of this project is to show that UHS could be an effective net-zero emission
strategy for the Williston Basin in North Dakota. UHS offers the advantages of safety and vast
storage capacity, often exceeding orders of magnitude compared to surface storage methods
(Panfilov, 2016; Sambo and others, 2022; Zivar and others, 2021). Wells constructed for UHS need
to withstand injection/production phases and contaminants such as carbon dioxide (CO2),
hydrogen sulfide (H2S), and water that may accompany H2 gas (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022).
Materials used to construct wells include cement, elastomers, and metal casings (Robisson and

 1

others, 2013; Kwatia and others, 2017; Ugarte and Salehi, 2022), and the integrity of a well
depends on the compatibility and durability of these materials (Goodman and others, 2022).
Depleted oil and gas wells may be considered for conversion to storage applications but are
subjected to more constraints compared to new wells as the depleted wells were constructed for
another purpose and require evaluation to ensure proper well integrity (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022).
Despite the immense potential benefits of UHS as a future energy solution, the performance and
permanence of the UHS unit can have inherent risks and uncertainties associated with wellbore
integrity because of constant geochemical interactions of wellbore cement, elastomers, and casings
with the stored gas and its by-products. As such, storage or withdrawal of H2 may be jeopardized
depending on the severity of cement, elastomer, and casing degradation and well functionality for
injection. Therefore, understanding how H2 reacts with these materials is critical to assessing H2-
induced well degradation for a UHS project to address the sustainability of the present H2 energy
market and possible future needs.

 The general approach and methodology of the literature review conducted highlights:

 1. Wellbore cement investigation of the interaction between molecular H2 and various
 cements by characterizing samples using different physical and chemical tests.

 2. Potential microbial effects on wellbore cement. Molecular H2 is considered one of the
 most important and energetically viable electron donors for microbial respiration in the
 subsurface (Gregory and others, 2019).

 3. Mitigation of potential cement degradation. Studies indicate that some cements may be
 less prone to H2-induced degradation despite being compositionally similar.

 4. Wellbore elastomer investigation to examine elastomers when exposed to varied gas
 mixtures and pure gases at different conditions and their resulting physiomechanical
 properties.

 5. Potential chemical degradation of wellbore elastomers to examine chemical degradation
 that occurs when elastomers encounter a chemically corrosive material such as H2S under
 UHS conditions.

 6. Mitigation of potential elastomer degradation within wellbores. Studies indicate a
 balanced material in terms of durability and gas barrier property is needed for the seals in
 a high-pressure H2 environment.

 7. Wellbore casing investigation to explore the effect of H2 permeation in different types of
 high-strength carbon steels and examine the sensitivity of the steel to H2 embrittlement.

 8. Potential for microbially induced corrosion (MIC). MIC was examined as another
 concern for the deterioration of metal structures in subsurface environments.

 9. Mitigation of potential wellbore corrosion. The studies investigated indicate that low-
 strength carbon steels are more suitable than high-strength steels for H2 storage
 applications.

 2

 This literature review supports project goals by providing strategies for maintaining wellbore
integrity as an important component of risk management and potential liability in an underground
storage complex, where the well is the primary pathway between the surface and the subsurface.
While uncommon, experience from underground gas storage operations suggests that the most risk
to the integrity of the H2 storage complex is leakage through wells (Goodman and others, 2022).
Multiple mechanisms can be responsible for loss of wellbore integrity including fluid migration
through existing leakage pathways, casing microbial corrosion, H2 blistering and H2
embrittlement, cement degradation, and elastomer failure (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). H2 is an
electron donor that can create efficient reactions chemically or biologically. Because of a lower
molecular weight, density, and viscosity, H2 gas has a higher potential for leakage compared to
methane (CH4) and other fluids such as CO2, nitrogen (N2), and water (H2O) which increase its
mobility in the subsurface. H2 also has a higher diffusivity than CH4, which increases the risk of
diffusion into well materials (Goodman and others, 2022).

EFFECTS OF HYDROGEN ON WELLBORE CEMENT

 Various safety barriers are employed in well construction to minimize potential leakage and
safe underground storage of gases. Cement is one of the main hydraulic barriers that plays a key
containment role in supporting steel casings and blocking undesired fluid flow between the
wellbore and the formation from the fluid column to the targeted depth (Kiran and others, 2017).
Different types of cement are used for this purpose (usually Classes A–H cement according to the
classification outlined in American Petroleum Institute [API], 2019), depending on specific
subsurface pressure and temperature conditions and/or resistance against chemical interactions
(Corina and others, 2023). Despite its role in integrity and durability for long-term UHS, analysis
of effects of H2 on the mechanical properties of well cement is largely lacking. This section
presents a review of the main studies that investigate the potential effects of H2 on the mechanical
properties of well cement and the associated reactions for improved UHS planning (summarized
in Table 1). Most H2–cement interaction studies focus on changes in chemistry, mineralogy, elastic
properties, and fluid flow properties such as porosity and permeability to determine the mechanical
strength of cement after H2 exposure.

 Wellbore Cement Investigation

 Recently, Ugarte and others (2024) investigated the interaction between molecular H2 and
Class H cement by characterizing samples using different physical and chemical tests. Class H
cement is an ordinary Portland cement (PC) with a typical Blaine fineness of 1600 cm2/g and is
used by nearly 80% of drilling companies across the United States. The typical compounds of a
Class H cement are 50% tricalcium silicate (Ca3SiO5), 30% dicalcium silicate (Ca2SiO4), 5%
tricalcium aluminate (Ca3Al2O6), and 12% tetracalcium aluminoferrite (Ca4Al2Fe2O10) (Nelson,
1990). The cement samples were cast in the form of cylinders and incubated for 7, 28, 84, and
168 days. Two different environments consisting of distilled water and water saturated with
electrolysis-generated molecular H2 with a concentration of approximately 2 ppm were tested. The
samples were characterized using unconfined compressive strength (UCS), porosity and
permeability (PP), nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR), Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy
(FTIR), acoustic wave velocities, and scanning electron microscopy coupled with energy-
