 dispersive x-ray spectroscopy (SEM–EDS). Results showed samples exposed to molecular H2
 exhibited a greater UCS in both the short- and long-term exposures compared to the same samples

 4

exposed to distilled water only. Lower porosity was found in H2-exposed samples, with the highest
decrease of 6.1% in the longest curing time. Permeability was proportionally impacted, showing a
decrease of 4.2% in the longest curing time. NMR distributions showed a reduction of larger pores
and an increase of smaller pores in the H2-exposed samples with a higher Young’s modulus, shear
modulus, and Poisson’s ratio. The highest percentage differences observed were 4.7%, 5.0%, and
1.8%, respectively. Compositional analysis demonstrated that the presence of H2 increased the
amount of Ca(OH)2 and Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O (a calcium trisulfoaluminate hydrate)
which was confirmed by the presence of crystalline needle-shaped structure under SEM. This
experimental study demonstrated that the presence of molecular H2 could increase the formation
of hydrated products such as Ca(OH)2 and Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O, causing a shift in the
porosity distribution and increasing the compressive strength of Class H cement, which is
beneficial for well integrity. However, it is also known that the formation of secondary
Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O can lead to expansion, internal stress, and cement degradation when
the cement is exposed to overheating (80°–90°C) (Collepardi, 2003). As such, further research
considering temperature and pressure as variables is needed to simulate the subsurface conditions
and present the data more accurately.

 The changes in the properties of neat Class H cement after exposure to H2 under geological
storage conditions for different time periods were reported by Fernández and others (2023).
Cement slurry was prepared according to API Recommended Practice (RP) 10B-2 specifications
(American Petroleum Institute, 2013), using a water-to-cement ratio of 0.38 followed by placing
the solid cement sample in a core holder with H2 exposure at 1500 psi for 7 and 14 days at 120°F
(49°C). Interestingly, analysis of PP before and after H2 exposure showed a slight increase in
porosity by ~2%, with a significant increase in permeability after both 7 days (~60%) and 14 days
(175%) of exposure. However, the cause of this opposing relationship between PP was not well
understood. A decrease in Young’s modulus and Poisson’s ratio indicated a decrease in elastic
properties and increased deformation at both exposure times. Overall, the property most affected
by H2 exposure was cement permeability, followed by the elastic properties. It was also noted that
the length of the exposure period is proportional to the increase in PP, a critical factor to consider
while using Class H cement. It was proposed that the change in cement properties is the reaction
of H2 with C–S–H following the reaction, 𝐶𝐶−𝑆𝑆−𝐻𝐻 + 2𝐻𝐻+ ↔ 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶2+ + 𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑥𝑥𝑂𝑂𝑂𝑂𝑥𝑥. C–S–H is a
semiamorphous, gel-like material formed during cement hydration and known to be the principal
binding agent of cement (Cao and others, 2013; Carey, 2013). In contrast to Ugarte and others
(2024), this study reported that the dissolution of C–S–H groups into calcium ions and amorphous
silica could change the cement pore structures, resulting in an increase in permeability and the loss
of wellbore mechanical properties.

 Al-Hadrami and others (2022) investigated the effects of H2 on Class H cement’s rheological
and mechanical properties by exposing wet cement to H2 prior to molding and curing. Cement
samples were prepared according to the procedure recommended in API RP 10B-2, using an
OFITE Model 20 programmable constant-speed blender. H2 was added to the cement slurry
samples for 20 s, 2 min, and 4 min during the mixing process followed by molding and curing at
120℉ (49°C) for 24 hr. Compressive strength tests, density tests, rheological tests, and computed
tomography (CT) scans showed an inverse relationship between the compressive strength of the
cement samples and the time exposed to H2. Similarly, direct correlations between rheological
properties (plastic viscosity and yield point) and exposure period were observed. It was proposed
that the reductive dissolution of redox-sensitive minerals such as hematite present in the cement

 5

(derived from clays and shale as raw materials) by H2 following the reaction, Fe2O3 + 4H+ + H2
→ 3H2O + 2Fe2+, is the major cause. Furthermore, H2 exposure to wet cement trapped H2 bubbles,
affecting rheological properties, mechanical strength, and stability of the Class H cement. Overall,
the study concluded that H2 exposure to wet cement is detrimental to Class H cement’s mechanical
strength, affecting the long-term secure operation of UHS.

 A similar observation was reported when a dry cement sample was exposed to a mixture of
H2 and natural gas (NG) (Shi and others, 2020). A commercially acquired dry cement sample
(cement type not mentioned) was incubated for 3 months with a H2–NG mixture at ~26.2 MPa
pressure at a constant temperature of 353.15 K (80°C). Results indicated relatively small changes
in porosity, which increased by only 0.2%, and mineralogy due to H2 exposure. The permeability
of the cement sample increased significantly by 62.5%, representing a potential impact on the
sealing effectiveness of the cement, similar to the observation by Fernández and others (2023).
However, the causes for this behavior are not entirely clear. It has been reported that the dissolution
of pore throats may cause significant changes in permeability with small corresponding changes
in porosity (Shi and others, 2020). X-ray diffraction (XRD) analysis showed an increase in the
Ca(OH)2 and CaCO3 content after H2 exposure. It must be noted that only a dry cement sample
was tested in this work. To substantiate a trend with respect to cement impact, additional testing
conducted under relevant environmental conditions, for example, with fluids, is required.
Nonetheless, this study provides some helpful insights to predict the behavior of wellbore cement
in a UHS condition.

 Class G cement, which is finer (Blaine fineness of 2450 cm2/g) than Class H cement, is
another cement type that is commonly used as a support material in UHS systems. There exists a
handful of studies related to the effects of H2 on Class G cement. Two Class G cement core plugs
were prepared by mixing cement with 44% distilled water and dried at 33°C for 30 days. The
sample was then exposed to H2 for 125 days at 1400 psi and 75°C (Al-Yaseri and others, 2023).
XRD, x-ray fluorescence (XRF), CT scan, and dynamic elastic properties analyses indicated that
H2 exposure had a limited impact on the Class G cement integrity under the tested experimental
conditions. The XRD analysis indicated only a minor alteration in mineralogy such as alite
(Ca3SiO5) (increase from 53.7% to 54.4%) and brownmillerite (Ca2[AlFe]2O5) (increase from
7.2% to 10.3%) with some CaCO3 growth that was attributed to the cement hydration process.
Interestingly, an increase in cement density with H2/brine injection was observed, suggesting the
precipitation of high-density minerals in pore spaces. As such, the total CT porosity reduced from
7.9% to 7.2% (total reduction of 8.86%) in Sample 1 and 8.3% to 7.6% (total reduction of 8.43%)
in Sample 2. The injection of H2 had only a minor effect on the Poisson’s ratios, as the values
slightly increased from 0.223 to 0.23 in Sample 1 (total increase of 3.1%) and
0.217 to 0.229 in Sample 2 (total increase of 5.5%). Similarly, the dynamic elastic modulus
increased from 13.91 to 14.82 (total increase of 6.6%) in Sample 1 and 13.45 to 15.04 (total
increase of 11.8%) in Sample 2. Overall, the results indicated low deformation potential of Class G
cement when subjected to H2 exposure at experimental conditions, results similar to what Ugarte
and others (2024) found with Class H cement.

 A modeling effort using geochemical thermodynamics calculation was also carried out to
evaluate H2-induced reactions to Class G cement when H2 is costored with CH4 (Jacquemet and
others, 2020). This effort showed that the two primary hydration products, C–S–H and calcium
hydroxide (Ca[OH]2[s]), are non-redox-sensitive minerals, and hence their volume fraction

 6

remained unchanged after reaction with H2. In contrast, redox-sensitive minor cement minerals of
Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O and hematite encountered full reductive dissolution with H2. The
produced sulfides from Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O reduction and the ferrous iron from hematite
(Fe2O3) reduction combined to precipitate FeS, and the remaining ferric iron in hematite combined
with the ferrous iron to precipitate ferric–ferrous iron oxide Fe3O4. The precipitation of FeS and
Fe3O4 was estimated to reduce the cement matrix volume after reaching full thermodynamic
equilibrium, affecting cement integrity. However, considering the low amount of Fe2O3 in the
cement matrix and the temperature range of UHS (less than 100°C), the potential reduction of
Fe2O3 to Fe3O4 should not impact cement resistance (Iorio and others, 2022). Another
thermodynamic batch modeling effort by Zeng and others (2022) used Class G/H cement to
simulate cement degradation in the presence of H2 and formation brine. The simulation was run at
an infinite time scale at reservoir temperature (95°C) and pressure (10 MPa). Results showed that
the cement hydration process transformed the major cement compositions to C–S–H (55.53%),
Ca(OH)2 (27.89%), and C3AH6 (13.27%) with small fractions of FeS (1.66%) and Fe3O4 (1.65%).
A higher proportion of stable C–S–H and Ca(OH)2 phases indicated the increased stability and
mechanical strength of the Class G cement after H2/brine exposure.

 The trend toward safe usage of the materials in UHS projects, particularly for high-pressure
and high-temperature (HPHT) environments, requires higher accuracy in modeling and test
environments formulated for the appropriate environmental conditions. Experimental data on the
effects of H2 exposure on the mechanical properties of cement using specifically designed
autoclaves to simulate UHS conditions have been reported in several studies. Corina and others
(2023) contained the neat Class G cement samples inside a polypropylene tube and submersed in
a brine mixture with a density of approximately 1.15 g/cm3. The brine mixture was saturated with
Ca(OH)2 to prevent cement leaching. H2 was pumped into the autoclaves to 200 bar and incubated
at 80°C for 8 weeks. For comparison, several samples were exposed to inert gas (N2) at the same
pressure and temperature conditions, while another sample set was kept unexposed. Changes in
mechanical properties (Young’s modulus, Poisson’s ratio, and ultimate strength) were monitored
using UCS tests and confined cyclic loading tests. Changes in cement mineralogy were analyzed
by XRD. From the UCS tests, it was observed that the average ultimate strength of H2-exposed
samples was reduced by 5–6 MPa compared to the N2-exposed and unreacted samples, both after
1- and 2-month exposure. Similarly, the average Young’s modulus after 1- and 2-month exposure
was 4.6 and 5.7 GPa, smaller by 2.8 and 0.7 GPa compared to the N2-exposed samples. Similarly,
the average Poisson’s ratio of both N2- and H2-exposed samples ranged between 0.24 and
0.4, higher than those of the unexposed samples (0.17). For cyclic tests, the cement sample was
confined to 8 MPa. A pore pressure of 1 MPa was applied and kept constant during the test. At the
initial stage, the sample was axially loaded to reach a differential stress of 15 MPa. Afterward, the
axial stress was cyclically changed to 4 (unloading) and 15 MPa (loading) 12 times. General
observations showed a sharp increase in static elastic modulus from the initial loading to the first
cycle followed by a gradual increase until Cycle 12. With H2 exposure, the static modulus was
8.6 GPa during initial loading, increasing to 9.8 GPa after 12 cycles before decreasing to 6.9 GPa
after final unloading. The increase in elastic modulus was likely due to sample compaction and
porosity reduction, resulting in a stiffer material. The change in dynamic modulus and Poisson’s
ratio followed the same trend but with a much smaller variation. The ultimate strength of the H2-
exposed sample was 53.6 MPa, slightly smaller than the N2-exposed (55.1 MPa) and unreacted
(55.4 MPa) samples. XRD analysis of the unreacted, N2- and H2-exposed samples showed alite,
brownmillerite, Ca(OH)2, katoite, and hibschite. Overall, it was concluded that the effects of H2

 7

exposure and cyclic loading of H2 on mechanical properties and mineralogical changes of Class G
cement were limited under the tested conditions. Nothing observed in the mechanical properties
of cement can significantly affect UHS integrity under HPHT conditions.

 Boersheim and others (2019) also implemented a specially designed autoclave to evaluate
the PP of Class G cement plugs with added H2 for 4 weeks at 50 bar and 100°C in a saline synthetic
brine (total dissolved solids [TDS] 352.23 g/L). An overall increase in permeability and a reduction
in porosity (by ~10%–19%) was observed postexposure, similar to observations reported by
Fernández and others (2023) and Shi and others (2020). Severe salt efflorescence in the cement
cores was observed and considered the main culprit for the changes in PP of the cement plugs. It
also indicated that the magnitude of salt precipitation in the cores depends on the salinity of the
fluids. After exposure, the pH of the in situ fluid increased from 5 to 11 and the concentration of
calcium increased, both suggesting cement leaching during H2 exposure. Although the high-pH
conditions combined with H2S can potentially form the mineral Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O, the
mechanical effect of Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O on the cement’s properties is yet to be
determined (Kutchko and others, 2011). This study showed that the exposure of cement to H2 under
reservoir conditions may increase permeability but decrease the porosity of cement materials.
Without any mechanical strength tests, this study suggested no significant impact on wellbore
integrity or cement seal during UHS. Similar results were reported by a recent H2–cement
interaction study using an autoclave at 150 bar and 90°C (Cracolici and others, 2023). The
hardened Class G cement samples were immersed in synthetic formation water and exposed to H2
for 8 weeks. UCS, gas permeability, and electrical conductivity were analyzed to evaluate the
cement integrity after H2 exposure. Although H2 exposure reduced the compressive strength of the
sample by 5.4%, mechanical strength tests were in the acceptable range that indicated the minor
effect of H2 on the chemical and physical characteristics of the Class G cement. Using similar test
conditions and sample type, Iorio and others (2022) also discovered that H2 does not alter the
chemical and physical properties of Class G cement samples.

 A different experimental approach using disaggregated and crushed cement specimens
(<355 µm) was performed by Aftab and others (2023). This study was based on the compositional
changes of the brine after H2 exposure. Three different cement types (Portland cement without
lignosulfonate retarder [PC], Portland cement with a retarder [PCR], and Portland cement that was
set under atmospheric conditions [PCA]) were used. For PC and PCR cements, a slurry was
created by mixing a preprepared cement paste with water with a cement:water ratio of
2.5:1 and cured for 28 days in a humidity chamber. The slurry for PCA was prepared with a
cement:water ratio of 3:1 and air-dried for 36 hr. The chosen conditions were 3000 psi, 80°C,
and 35 and 250 ppt salinity, reflecting the average conditions of 138 depleted gas fields in the
United Kingdom, North Sea. The disaggregated cement was exposed to gas (H2, N2, or air) and
brine for 15.2 and 30.4 days. A range of analytical techniques was employed to evaluate the
materials used in the experiments, including XRD, plane polarized light (PPL) optical microscopy,
porosity measurements, and inductively coupled plasma optical emission spectroscopy (ICP–
OES). The study showed very low alteration (<1%) in the elemental concentrations in the brine
solution under ambient, N2-exposed, or H2-exposed samples, suggesting the occurrence of no
significant geochemical reactions between H2 and cements regardless of the wellbore cement type.
This study also suggested a negligible impact of H2 on the integrity of the wellbore cements during
UHS. It is worth noting that it is possible that chloride ions from the dissolved salts accelerate a

 8

hydration reaction mechanism leading to a reduction in the contact area between cement grains
and water; therefore, chemical reactions are less likely to occur in this system (Okhovat and others,
2020).

 The project Underground Sun Storage (Renewables and Gas Austria AG, 2017) in Austria
reported an increase in cement’s gas permeability by a small factor of 1.2–1.7 after being exposed
to H2 for 2 to 14 months at 40°C and 70 bars. The final gas permeability of these samples was also
measured after exposure to CH4 and N2 and found to be in the same order as those exposed to H2.
This was attributed to the leaching of cement and reaction with CO2 in the mixing water. The
cement reacts with CO2 through a process called carbonation when CO2 is present, which
transforms CO2 into solid calcium carbonate (CaCO3) particles that become bound with the
cement. Accordingly, the final permeability remained in the same order as the initial permeability.
The phase identification using XRD on the H2-exposed sample indeed showed a reduction in
Ca(OH)2 and an increase in vaterite (CaCO3 polymorph) content after H2 exposure, contributing
to a reduction in cement permeability during H2 exposure.

 Potential Microbial Effects on Wellbore Cement

 Because of its low reduction potential, molecular H2 is considered one of the most important
and energetically viable electron donors for microbial respiration in the subsurface (Gregory and
others, 2019). The main microbial H2-utilizing terminal electron-accepting processes expected and
identified to occur in underground gas storage sites are methanogenesis, sulfate reduction, and
acetogenesis. These processes have been observed to occur at temperatures as high as 90°C and at
salinities as high as 30% (Vreeland and others, 1998; Ivanova and others, 2007). Thus H2 can be
consumed to generate CH4 (via methanogenesis) or acetate (via acetogenesis) in the presence of
CO2/HCO3− or H2S in the presence of sulfate (via sulfate reduction) (Gregory, and others, 2019),
negatively influencing the quality of the stored H2. Although the main impact of microbes on H2
storage is the permanent loss of H2 due to the conversion of H2 into products including CH4 and
H2S, the formation of toxic and corrosive H2S gas by the reaction, 4H2 + SO42- + H+ → HS- +
4H2O, via active bacterial sulfate reduction (BSR) can cause dissolution of cement materials. BSR
is a very efficient process in which a small quantity of sulfate can produce a significant amount of
H2S. These microorganisms can be present in the cement sheath surface or within interstitial space
(Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). In addition to the disadvantages of producing H2S, water generation
can increase water saturation, which results in less saturation of H2 (less space for H2 storage) as
well as the dissolution of water-soluble phases in the cement.

 Another scenario to consider is the presence of chemolithotrophic sulfur-oxidizing bacteria
of the genus thiobacillus which are the most aggressive biological agents that promote microbial-
induced degradation of cementitious materials (Sand and Bock, 1988). These bacteria obtain
energy through the oxidation of reduced inorganic sulfur sources such as H2S producing sulfuric
acid. Cement samples were inoculated with thiobacilli and incubated for 9 months in a test
chamber with a humidity >95% at 30°C. H2S at a concentration of 15 mg/m3 was generated,
causing severe corrosion of the concrete material. Fungi are another candidate that can participate
in biodeteriorations of cementitious materials. These multicellular organisms are known to be
crucial hidden players of microbiomes in anoxic environments. During their metabolism, these
organisms generate organic acids such as gluconic, citric, and oxalic acids that can induce the
dissolution of cement-based materials (Makul and others, 2022). Perfettini and others (1990)

 9

evaluated the Ca(OH)2 cement degradation by two fungal strains after 11 months of incubation
and discovered an increase in the cement porosity and a loss of bending strength. Despite the
important role of microbes in cement degradation, studies on H2-related microbial effects on
cement materials under subsurface conditions are very limited.

 In addition to biological reactions, chemical reactions of H2 with dissolved sulfur species or
sulfur-bearing minerals (e.g., pyrite) may also occur in subsurface environments (Reitenbach and
others, 2015). Besides its direct impact on mineral dissolution, PP, and mechanical properties,
these reactions also lead to the formation of H2S. In addition to negatively affecting the quality of
the stored H2 gas, H2S can also modify the redox potential and lower the pH of pore waters (Truche
and others, 2013), triggering further fluid–rock reactions, e.g., dissolution of Ca(OH)2(s), C–S–H,
and ferrite (Lecolier and others, 2006). H2S can also compromise the infrastructure because of its
corrosive, flammable, and toxic nature (Wei and others, 2017).

 Mitigation of Potential Cement Degradation

 Studies indicate that Class G cement is less prone to H2-induced degradation despite being
compositionally similar to Class H cement. However, reducing the water content as a potential
approach to mitigate the problem should be considered, i.e., the water:cement ratio of Class H
(38%) is lower than that of Class G (44%). In addition, Class H has a coarser grind than Class G,
which gives it better water-retarding properties for cementing deep wells. As such Class H cement
is recommended over other cement classes.

EFFECTS OF H2 ON ELASTOMERS

 According to American Society for Testing and Materials (ASTM) Standard D1566,
elastomers are high polymeric organic networks capable of absorbing large deformations
reversibly. Therefore, elastomers are important components of seal and barrier elements such as
packers, blowout preventers (BOPs), and liner hangers (Ahmed and others, 2020, 2019) for UHS
applications. Packers are used for zonal isolation in completion operations, the BOP is a large
valve situated at the top of the well and used to shut in the well during well control events, and the
liner hanger is used to suspend a partial casing string beneath that above (Shafiee and others, 2022;
Ahmed and others, 2020, 2019). The most common elastomer materials used in the oil and gas
industry are classified into seven groups: nitrile butadiene rubber (NBR), ethylene propylene diene
monomer (EPDM), hydrogenated nitrile butadiene rubber (HNBR), fluoroelastomers (FKM),
perfluoroelastomers (FEPM), perfluorocarbon elastomers (FFKM), and vinyl–methyl–
polysiloxane (VMQ) (Tetteh, 2023). The chemical and mechanical properties of each of these
elastomers depend on the type of monomer, molecular weight, number, and type of crosslinks.
However, regardless of the type, elastomers are always susceptible to physical and chemical
damage under UHS conditions (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). They undergo degradation when
exposed to high gas pressures, chemicals, heat, ultraviolet (UV) radiation, gases, and ozone. In
this section, elastomer degradation because of H2 exposure is discussed (summarized in Table 2).

 Wellbore Elastomer Investigation

 A recent study by Tetteh (2023) used three general purpose elastomers that are widely used
in the oil and gas industry: EPDM, FKM, and NBR. The elastomers were exposed to varied gas
mixtures and pure gases of H2, CH4, and CO2 at different conditions via autoclave aging
experiments, and their physiomechanical properties were examined. EPDM is a synthetic
elastomer well known for its excellent weather and ozone resistance. FKM is a fluoroelastomer
known for its ability to withstand extreme temperatures and for its fluid resistance. NBR is a highly
flexible and durable elastomer that offers high tensile strength and abrasion resistance. The aging
period ranged from 1 to 7 days, while temperatures varied between 77° (25°) and 158°F (70°C)
and pressure was maintained at 3 MPa. The trend of degradation in elastomers was highest with
H2 exposure, more so in NBR (i.e., a 3% and 3.4% reduction in hardness after aging in 100% H2
for 1 and 7 days, respectively, at 25°C.). The effects of pure H2 and H2–CH4 mixtures on the
mechanical properties of elastomers were found to be similar. EPDM showed increased hardness
and compressional resistance when aged in 100% H2 and H2–CH4 mix environments at high
temperatures and poor compressional resistance at low temperatures. EPDM also maintained its
thermal resistance properties in gaseous H2 environments. The most significant elastomer

 11

degradation in gaseous H2 environments was seen in NBR. FKM remained thermally stable in
gaseous H2 environments, although its compressional resistance decreased at elevated
temperatures.

 Simmons and others (2021) studied the impacts of high-pressure H2 exposure to NBR.
Compression set tests were performed before H2 exposure and 30 minutes after exposure to H2 gas
up to 90 MPa for 22 hr at 110°C. Compression set is the amount of permanent deformation a
material experiences after compression for a specific time and temperature. A nearly 40% increase
in compression set was observed after H2 exposure, which can significantly reduce sealing
performance and results in a loss of stored H2. The NBR elastomer showed a 72%–85% increase
in volume. Transmission electron microscopy showed an increase in particles in the polymer after
exposure to a pressure of 28 MPa, indicating an initiation of void formation in NBR over time.

 Shi and others (2020) investigated the impacts of H2–CH4 blends on elastomers using three
different types of polymeric materials commonly used in wellhead seals – one made from
polytetrafluoroethylene (Teflon™) and two others from a proprietary elastomer mixture. The
samples were incubated with an H2:CH4 gas ratio of 13:87 at 26.2 MPa and 353.15 K (80°C). After
incubation for 3 months, the diameter of an elastomer aged in a H2-laden gas mixture increased by
approximately 14.3% compared to a 3.05% increase for elastomers aged in pure CH4. H2 under
high pressure can permeate and oversaturate the elastomers. If the surrounding pressure suddenly
decreases, the rapid release of gas can result in the development of cracks exceeding the
elastomer’s yield tensile strength and generate internal cracking and blistering within the material
(Patel and others, 2019; Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). It must be noted that the risk of decompression
increases at high temperatures or very low temperatures when elastomer becomes brittle (Patel and
others, 2019; Koga and others, 2011). However, this study only covered the change in dimensions
of the elastomers, not the change in mechanical strength. This study lacks the data needed for the
evaluation of the long-term robustness of the elastomers under operational conditions.

 Yamabe and Nishimura (2012) investigated rapid gas decompression failures in NBR on
exposure to high-pressure H2 gas. When elastomers are under high pressure, gas permeates into
the material. When the pressure is suddenly reduced, the permeated gas cannot escape, causing
violent expansion and eventual failure of the material. The authors examined the relationship
between H2 content, mechanical properties of the material, and extent of blister damage in the
explosive decompression failure process. Further investigations were also conducted to determine
chemical deterioration within the NBR. Experiments were carried out with a durability tester in
which NBR O-rings were exposed to H2 gas at varying conditions. Some samples were compressed
at ratios ranging from 8% to 30% during experiments where they were repeatedly exposed to H2
at pressures up to 90 MPa and temperatures ranging from –60° to 100°C. Such compression
produced cracking parallel to the compressional force applied after decompression because of
stress generated at the center of the sample, whereas cracks observed in uncompressed samples
were ringlike and distributed randomly. Furthermore, at high decompression rates, cracks appear
on the surface of the elastomer. SEM images obtained for O-rings exposed repeatedly to H2 at
90 MPa indicated that fatigue cracks started from within the elastomer and not on the surface. It
was also determined that decompression at high pressures for samples exposed to H2 creates small-
scale fractures within the elastomers due to bubble formation. Although these bubbles rarely
affected the tensile properties of the elastomers, they seemed to contribute to the initiation of

 12

blisters. The same authors studied the influence of fillers such as carbon black and silica on H2
penetration properties and blister fracture using EPDM and NBR at a pressure of 10 MPa and a
temperature of 30°C, with exposure time varied from 2 to 24 hr (Yamabe and Nishimura, 2009).
Irrespective of the presence and type of fillers, H2 penetrated the material in molecular form, and
more so upon the addition of carbon black as a filler. Blister damage was more severe in the
materials that did not contain added fillers, and the degree of blister damage decreased with a
decrease in H2 content and an increase in tensile properties. In contrast, no blister formation was
observed in the silica-containing composites. Fujiwara and others (2015) showed that the
mechanical properties of the NBR elastomer can be weakened after cyclical pressure depending
on the type of fillers added to the material when exposed to 30°C and 90 MPa for 12 hr. In contrast
to Yamabe and Nishimura (2009), only the silica-filled NBR was significantly affected (elastic
modulus approximately halved) as the number of exposure cycles increased. Notably, this effect
increased as the silica content and the number of cyclic high-pressure exposures increased, but no
effects were observed for the carbon black-filled NBR samples nor the unfilled ones. However, it
must be noted that there was a significant pressure difference between these two experiments.

 Koga and others (2011) investigated the effects of high-pressure H2 on the initiation of
blisters and subsequent blister damage in three major elastomer types used in H2 equipment:
EPDM, VMQ, and HNBR O-rings at 100°C and 90 MPa for 110 min. VMQ is a silicone-based
elastomer known for its extreme high-temperature resistance, low-temperature flexibility, and
good resistance to UV radiation, oxygen, and ozone, and HNBR is a hydrogenated NBR that
possesses better chemical and mechanical resistance. Results showed that the amount of gas
leakage under cyclic pressure is higher than under constant pressure because of surface and inner
crack initiation. The crack damage of VMQ was the least despite it having lowest tensile strength,
followed by HNBR and EPDM (Figure 1).

 Figure 1. Optical micrographs of O-rings after H2 exposure: a) EPDM, b) HNBR, and c) VMQ.

 Since VMQ has a diffusivity one order higher than EPDM and HNBR, H2 molecules diffuse
faster after decompression. In addition, the crack damage was inversely proportional to the
decompression time and the tensile strength of the materials decreased with increased temperature.
The same authors exposed EPDM and VMQ O-rings to high-pressure H2, helium (He), and N2
gases at 10 MPa at 25°C followed by rapid decompression for 0.3 s. The blister fracture occurred
in EPDM but not in VMQ. The blister size increased in the following sequence: N2 > H2 > He.

 13

When the cross section of the VMQ O-rings was observed under an optical microscope before and
after exposure, no cracks were found regardless of the gas species (Figure 2).

 Figure 2. Blister initiation behavior of EPDM and VMQ with high-pressure gas decompression
 at 10 MPa and 25°C. White arrows in EPDM show blisters on the surface.

 Jaravel and others (2011) investigated parameters affecting the occurrence of cavities in
elastomer vinyltrimetoxysilane (VTMS) exposed to a pressurized H2 environment at pressures
ranging from 0.1 to 27 MPa in a temperature-regulated chamber. VTMS is known to enhance the
properties of elastomers and sealants by promoting adhesion, improving mechanical strength, and
increasing thermal stability. After gas saturation, depressurization was conducted at pressures from
1 to ~90 MPa. Two different tests were conducted to detect the effects of loading factors. The first
involved variable decompression rates with fixed saturation pressure and a second with fixed
decompression rate with variable saturation pressures. The authors concluded that explosive
decompression in rubbers could be prevented when elastomeric samples are pressurized at low
pressures and decompressed at slow depressurization rates, as depicted in Figure 3. In contrast to
the lower decompression pressure sample shown in Figure 3a, many cavities with a star-shaped
development were observed in the higher decompression pressure sample shown in Figure 3b.
However, it is worth noting that rapid gas decompression failures are not typical in large-scale
underground gas storage conditions because of relatively lower injection and withdrawal
pressures.

 According to White and others (2019), maximum withdrawal pressures at underground gas
storage stations are usually less than 7 MPa, with injection pressures typically within the range of
20–28 MPa. Considering these conditions, Yamabe and Nishimura (2009) exposed the two
elastomers, EPDM and NBR, filled with carbon black and silica to a gaseous H2 environment at
pressures less than 10 MPa and a temperature of 30°C for 2 to 24 hr to investigate H2 permeation
properties and damage due to blister formation. The authors observed that H2 could cause blisters
in elastomers filled with carbon black, as this filler can lower the diffusivity of the materials,
whereas silica had no influence on the material’s diffusivity. Overall, the degree of blister damage
in the composites decreased with a decrease in H2 content and an increase in tensile properties
(indicative of the elastic modulus and fracture strength).

 14

 Figure 3. Examples of damaged morphology with a) H2 saturation pressure = 9 MPa and
 decompression pressure = 0.9 MPa and b) H2 saturation pressure = 6.5 MPa and
 decompression pressure = 9 MPa.

 Potential Chemical Degradation of Wellbore Elastomers

 Another potential issue under UHS conditions is chemical degradation, which occurs when
elastomers come in contact with a chemically corrosive material such as H2S. As discussed in the
previous section, under UHS conditions, H2S is produced both chemically and biologically. First,
it causes erosion of the elastomer surface and spreads diffusively into the elastomer internals. At
this point, H2S reacts with the polymeric structure and crosslinks of the elastomer, which may
result in their breakage (Patel and others, 2019; Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). Chemical degradation
of elastomers also depends on the temperatures to which the rubber materials are exposed, the
duration of exposure, and the polymeric structure of the rubber material (Richter and Blobner,
2017; Shakiba and others, 2021). The potential chemical degradation of elastomers in gaseous
environments via a reaction between the elastomers and gas molecules has been studied by several
authors. Cong and others (2013) exposed HNBR elastomers to H2S gas at 1000 psi and 100°C
(212°F). HNBR is a hydrogenated NBR that possesses better chemical and mechanical resistance.
The authors used NMR with infrared and x-ray photoelectron spectroscopy to analyze the samples.
They observed a deterioration in tensile strength, ultimate elongation, and hardness of the
elastomers. In an aqueous solution, H2S dissociates into H+ and HS- ions. H+ causes hydrolysis of
the C≡N group in HNBR while HS- attacks the C=O group, producing two new functional groups:
C=S and C―C=S. In contrast, during homolysis, H2S can alter into H• and HS• radicals that react
with the polymer chain of elastomer-forming macromolecule radicals which again react with other
radicals. This chain reaction causes the breakdown of double and triple bonds in the polymer,
eventually yielding saturated C–S–C bonds and notably altering the physical properties of the
elastomer (Cong and others, 2013; Patel and others, 2019; Salehi and others, 2022). Fernández and
Castaño (2016) conducted autoclave tests on NBR samples in the presence of H2S and CO2 at a
total pressure of 1400 psi and a temperature of 95°C (200°F) for a period of 168 hr. A permanent
deformation as a function of H2S concentration was observed. It was concluded that the NBR
evaluated should not be used in environments where H2S concentrations are very high (over
2000 ppm) because of a decrease in its mechanical properties. For example, yield strength
decreased from 16 to 12.8 MPa, and elongation at break decreased from 684% to 388% with no

 15

H2S and with 5000 ppm, respectively. The NBR studied, however, may be used at low
concentrations of H2S while considering the exposure time and operation temperature.

 Mitigation of Potential Elastomer Degradation Within Wellbores

 A balanced material in terms of durability and gas barrier property is needed for the seals in
a high-pressure H2 environment. Studies show decompression rate has a strong influence on
durability, i.e., when the decompression rate was slower, durability was considerably improved.
Therefore, a system that can control the decompression rate needs to be developed. Studies show
that the carbon-filled or silica-filled elastomers perform better than the unfilled ones in high-
pressure H2 environments, with silica-filled ones being the better performers.

EFFECTS OF H2 ON CASING MATERIALS

 Wells used for UHS projects have been designed and constructed to operate with
hydrocarbons and could present issues while handling H2., for instance, low- and high-alloy steels
typically used in well casings are susceptible to corrosion, H2 embrittlement (HE), H2 blisters, H2-
induced cracking (HIC), and sulfide stress cracking (SSC) in the presence of H2 or other gases that
might be formed underground, such as H2S (Reitenbach and others, 2015; Wallace and others,
2021). Corrosion is a material degradation that refers to an electrochemical process involving
electron release due to metal dissolution at an anodic site and subsequent electronic transfer to
cathodic sites where oxygenated water is reduced to hydroxyl ions (OH−) (Ohaeri and others,
2018). The important reduction-oxidation (redox) processes involved in corrosion are represented
by two half-cell and overall reactions below in Equations 1–5 (Ohaeri and others, 2018).

 Anodic reaction:

 Fe → Fe2+ + 2e− (iron dissolution) [Eq. 1]

 Cathodic reactions:

 2H+ + 2e → H2 (hydrogen reduction) [Eq. 2]

 O2 + 2H2O + 4e− → 4OH− (oxygen reduction in neutral and alkalis media) [Eq. 3]

 O2 + 4H+ + 4e− → 2H2O (oixygen reduction in acidic media) [Eq. 4]

 Overall corrosion reaction:

                                       Fe + 2H+ → Fe2+ + H2                                 [Eq. 5]

 The two electrodes (cathode and anode) are often located on the surface, and an electrolyte
is necessary for any corrosion process to proceed. The electrolytes complete the electric circuit in
a corrosion cell by providing a transport medium for ions. The atomic H2 released during the
corrosion process (Equations 1–5) will either recombine to form H2 molecules or migrate into steel

 16

microstructure defects (Ohaeri and others, 2018). Literature reports that H2, once adsorbed on the
steel surface, can permeate through surfaces at grain boundaries and dislocations, causing HE that
leads to fatigue and failure of steel casings (Revie, 2008; Ghasemi, 2011; Gou and others, 2023).
H2 blistering occurs when atomic H2 is formed on the metal surface by dissociative chemical
sorption that can accumulate beneath the metal surface. The accumulated H2 thus causes pressure
to rise, producing plastic deformation in metals. HIC is the crack that results from the entry of H2
into the steel surface (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). SSC is a form of HE of metal by atomic H2
produced by acid corrosion on the metal surface (Iannuzzi, 2011). This section will review studies
on the potential effects of H2 on mechanical properties of the metal casing for better UHS planning
(summarized in Table 3).

 Wellbore Casing Investigation

 A recent study by Du and others (2023) explored the effect of H2 permeation in three types
of high-strength carbon steels, P110SS, Q125, and Q140, in an acidic environment via
electrochemical H2 permeation testing, minimum tensile strength (MTS) testing, and fracture

 18

morphology analysis. As the H2 filling current increased, so did the system’s temperature,
producing a pH decrease and increase in H2 diffusion coefficient, thus the sensitivity of the steel
to HE. The yield and tensile strengths of Q140 and Q125 were reduced by approximately 5%,
while the yield and tensile strengths of P110SS were reduced by only approximately 1.7%,
indicating that the higher the strength of the alloy, the more sensitive it is to H2 damage. It has been
reported that low to intermediate mild-carbon steels and their welds with a typical strength of less
than 800 MPa are indeed more resistant to HE then high-strength medium- and high-carbon steels
with carbon content greater than 0.3% (Reitenbach and others, 2015). As such, high-strength
medium- and high-carbon steels have limited applications in H2 service because of poor toughness
and weldability. Thus it is suggested that when higher-strength steels (rather than medium- and
high-carbon steels) are required, the low-alloy steels should be used in quenched and tempered
conditions as they have better toughness (Reitenbach and others, 2015). Boersheim and others
(2019) placed low-strength N80 (MTS of 689 MPa), K55 (MTS of 665 MPa), and J55 (MTS of
517 MPa) milled steel samples (commonly used in a variety of well environments) in a high-
salinity brine (TDS 352.23 g/L) exposed to H2 at 100°C and 100 bar (10 MPa) for a period of
4 weeks. The potential H2 damage of the samples was evaluated by determining the failure
mechanism. Visual inspection of the fragmented samples found no HE. Moreover, stress–strain
analysis before and after H2 exposure showed no sign of HE in any of the three samples, nor was
ultimate yield strength reduced. All three samples showed an extension in elasticity, possibly
indicative of strain hardening; nevertheless, low-strength steels remain more suitable for H2 service
applications. The effect of internal H2 and the displacement rate on fracture toughness behavior of
two structural steels, S355 (ultimate tensile strength of 540 MPa) and H8 (ultimate tensile strength
of 860 MPa), via H2 precharging in a high-pressure gaseous environment at 19.5 MPa and 450°C
for 21 hr was reported by Álvarez and others (2019). Permeation experiments were also conducted
to obtain the H2 diffusion coefficients of the steels. It was observed that the lower the displacement
rate (deformation) and the higher the steel yield strength, the stronger the reduction in fracture
toughness due to the presence of internal H2. A relatively high displacement rate of 0.1 mm/min
was sufficient to obtain the maximum embrittlement situation for the low-strength S355 steel (HEI
≅ 60%). However, in the case of the stronger H8 steel, it was necessary to decrease the
displacement rate one hundred times, to 0.001 mm/min, to obtain maximum embrittlement under
internal H2 (HEI ≅ 90%). The highly stressed, hard-tempered martensite microstructure of the H8
steel showed H2 diffusivity 15 times lower than that of S355 steel; however, crack growth began
rapidly when the displacement was 0.001 mm/min.

 A related recent study by Wang and others (2023) investigated the HE behavior of X80
pipeline steel in a simulated NG environment containing 0%–20% H2 through H2 permeation and
slow strain rate tensile (SSRT) tests. X80 steel is a microalloyed, low-carbon steel used for pipeline
applications because of its high strength, acceptable fracture roughness, and good weldability
(Mandal and others, 2021). The permeability characteristics, mechanical properties, and fracture
morphologies were systematically analyzed. The embrittlement mechanism showed that the
subsurface H2 concentration, ductility loss, and HE susceptibility increased significantly as the H2-
blending ratio increased from 10% to 20% (Figure 4).

 19

  Figure 4. Whole fracture morphologies of X80 steel under different H2-blending ratios: a) 0%,
  b) 1%, c) 5%, d) 10%, e) 15%, and f) 20%.

 HE index increased by 17 times over the condition with a 1% blending ratio. Moreover, a
positive correlation between subsurface H2 concentration and HE index was revealed. Combining
the in situ high-pressure gaseous H2 permeation with the high-pressure SSRT and fracture analysis
results, a 10%–15% H2-blending ratio was determined to be the critical value for the safe use of
X80 steel in H2-blended NG pipelines. However, it must be noted that the experiment was
conducted in a mixed NG, nitrogen, and H2 environment. Nevertheless, this study could help
predict the behavior of low-carbon, low-alloy, high-strength steel when H2 is at a higher
proportion, e.g., UHS systems.

 Corrosion-resistant alloys (CRAs), such as 13Cr, commonly used in the oil industry for
severe corrosion conditions, exhibit martensitic structure and provide better resistance against H2S
or CO2, but they are susceptible to the effects of H2. Steels having an austenitic structure (face-
centered cubic [FCC] lattice) can have a lower or higher stability, depending on their composition.
These austenitic structures can prevent H2 diffusion much better than the martensitic (body-
centered tetragonal lattice) or ferritic (body-centered cubic lattice) phases, which act as a path for
H2 atoms in the structure. San Marchi and others (2008) used Type 316 austenitic SS to examine
variations in H2-assisted fracture due to alloy composition and microstructure (annealed versus
strain-hardened) tensile property measurement. Type 316SS is an environmentally compatible and
fracture-resistance structural material commonly used for high-pressure applications, including H2
gas service. The internal H2 content (average 136 wppm [weight parts per million]) was developed
by thermal precharging in H2 gas, designed to produce a uniform and high concentration of H2 in
the test specimens. Observations from seven different types of 316SS showed that the high Ni
content (>12%) in Type 316SS alloys offers greater resistance to H2-assisted fracture. Furthermore,
the internal H2 charging resulted in reductions of ductility for both annealed and strain-hardened
microstructures. The reduction of area was reported for all seven alloys tested with internal H2,

 20

losing ductility of 12% to 43%. The lowest ductility values were attributed to the alloys with the
lowest Ni content. Increased ductility of the alloys with Ni content by H2 precharging indicated
that more stable alloys are also more resistant to H2-assisted fracture. Charged H2 also induces the
transformation of austinite to martensite for austenitic SS (Głowacka and others, 2005). The effect
of electrochemical charging of H2 on the structure of a lean duplex SS (LDX 2101) was examined
by both time-of-flight secondary ion mass spectrometry (TOF–SIMS) and electron backscatter
diffraction (Sobol and others, 2017). The results show a phase transformation of austenite to
mainly a defect-rich, brittle body-centered cubic (BCC) and hexagonal close-packed (HCP) phase.
The phase transformation was observed to occur in H2-rich regions in the austenite steel.

 In addition to the mechanical stress and H2 atom concentration, metallurgical
microstructures also remarkably affect HIC susceptibility (Kagay and others, 2016; Dwivedi and
Vishwakarma, 2018; Gou and others, 2023). For example, pipeline welds contain a unique
microstructure and can become effective H2 traps which, when combined with a high stress
concentration, can initiate H2-induced cracks (Kagay and others, 2016). It was found that the
cracks could originate from inclusions at the welded joint of an X100 (yield and tensile strengths
of 695 MPa and 830 MPa, respectively) pipeline steel in H2S environments. This caused HICs in
both the base metal and the weld, which are usually initiated from the Al–Ca–Si–O inclusions
propagating in a typical stepwise pattern with the transgranular mode (Gan and others, 2018).
X100 pipeline steel possesses a superior combination of strength, ductility, and corrosion
resistance (Wang and others, 2021). The testing was conducted in a H2S-saturated solution at
ambient temperature and pressure. Electrochemical cyclic H2 permeation testing was also
conducted to obtain the H2 diffusion coefficient and reversible/irreversible H2 concentrations. The
irreversible H2 concentration in the welded joint was almost 100 times higher than that of the base
metal, the primary factor leading to higher HIC susceptibility (Figure 5).

 Figure 5. SEM images of the propagation path of HIC cracks in base metal and the weld metal
 of X100 pipeline steel.

 HIC and corrosion behavior of friction stir-welded (FSW) X70 pipeline steel was performed
by Giarola and others (2021) in H2S-saturated solution (0.5% acetic acid [wt] + 5.0% NaCl [wt]).

 21

X70 is a high-strength steel that offers a high ratio of strength and toughness (Hashemi, 2011).
FSW is a solid-state joining technique that takes place at lower process temperatures, which
minimizes the thermal degradation and solidification-related defects that occur in traditional fusion
welding methods (Kilic and others, 2023). The HIC parameters in the welded regions were higher
than base metal values, implying the welded regions are highly susceptible to HIC. When
examining the path and crack propagation, irregular inclusions such as MnS, CaO, and AlO were
detected, considered irreversible traps where H2 atoms are mainly trapped by the interfaces
between the inclusions and the steel matrix and initiate microcracks under H2-charging conditions
(Du and others, 2015). Gou and others (2023) also studied the HIC of a welded X80 pipeline steel
using impact and SSRT combined with molecular dynamics modeling. The data showed the
highest HIC susceptibility at the fine-grained heat-affected zone (FGHAZ) and CGHAZ of the
steel, more so in the CGHAZ, displaying an obvious intergranular brittle fracture upon
electrochemical H2 charging. The HAZ is more prone to HIC because of the presence of extensive
H2 traps and hard phases. In addition, coarse grains contained in HAZ also affect the local HIC
susceptibility. For example, it was previously found that at the X100 steel weld, the average grain
size of HAZ was 10–15 µm, while the average grain size of base steel was 5–10 µm (Zhang and
Cheng, 2010). Indeed, SEM investigations of CGHAZ of the welded X80 steel after H2 charging
showed a macroscopic granular form. The presence of cracks on the fracture surface implies that
the fracture toughness of CGHAZ drops sharply after H2 charging.

 The corrosion behavior of SS is often correlated to the protective nature of the passive oxide
film that forms on the surface. Passive film, no more than a few nanometers thick, plays an
important role in the corrosion performance of metal, especially SS (Li and others, 2021), and the
contributing elements for corrosion protection are Cr, Ni, and Mo (Wang and others, 2018). H2
permeation usually facilitates the corrosion of SS remarkably. Yang and others (1999) used
secondary ion mass spectrometry (SIMS) to analyze the passive film of type
310SS formed at 0.7 V. Results showed lower ratios of Cr2O3:Fe, Cr(OH)3:Fe, NiO:Fe, and FeO:Fe
in the passive film of H2-charged samples compared to the uncharged samples, indicating the
susceptibility of 310SS to pitting is strongly influenced by H2 present in the specimens while
inverting the p-type passive film to n-type. The H2 effect on semiconductivity and compositions
of passive films formed on ferrite and austenite phases in a 2507 duplex SS were investigated by
Guo and others (2017) using current-sensing atomic force microscopy and x-ray photoelectron
spectroscopy (XPS). It was demonstrated that H2 significantly increased the conductivity of
passive film because of the increase of the OH−:O2− ratio. The passive film on austenite had higher
conductivity than that on ferrite after H2 charging because of more H2 in austenite. The presence
of H2 caused an inversion of the conductivity type of passive film from p-type to n-type, attributed
to the chemical composition change increasing pitting susceptibility.

 The corrosion behavior of a nickel-free, HNSS containing 0.96 wt% N2 with H2 charging
was investigated via electrochemical measurements and XPS surface analysis (Qiao and others,
2021). The most compelling result was the increased pitting potential (generally corresponding to
less corrosion) that resulted from the enrichment of elemental nitrogen (N) in the passive film and
the replacement of Ni with Mn in H2-charged HNSS. However, the protection of the passive film
on HNSS was damaged by H2 charging, evidenced by the increased carrier densities (by a factor
of 10), depleted atomic percentage of total elemental Cr and Cr2O3 (from 4.90% to 3.06% and
65.4% to 46.2%, respectively), and decreased O2−:OH− ratio (from 2.18 to 0.31) in the passive

 22

film, while the content of H2O increased drastically. Such a response could be attributed to the
reactions of permeated H2 with O2− and OH− forming H2O. It was proposed that the elemental N
in the passive film and a significant increase of the passive current density can neutralize the
localized acidification in the pit by forming NH3 or NH4+, suppressing the propagation from
metastable to stable pitting. The XRD patterns of the H2-charged and uncharged HNSS are shown
in Figure 6. The characteristic peaks correspond to the γ (austinite) and δ (ferritic) phases and the
peak intensity of the γ phase is higher than that of the δ phase, indicating the γ phase was dominant.
In addition, no peak relating to the other phases, such as precipitates, was identified.

 Figure 6. XRD patterns of H2-charged and uncharged HNSS.

 The relationship between H2 and N2 on the passivity and pitting susceptibility was
investigated with alloys 316L, 316LN, and 316LNH containing different nitrogen contents of
0.015, 0.198, and 0.556 wt% N (Ningshen and others, 2006). The study revealed that
electrochemically precharged H2 significantly reduced the pitting resistance of alloys containing
0.015 and 0.198 wt% nitrogen contents. In the alloy with the highest nitrogen content (0.556 wt%
N), an increase in the passive film current density with H2 was observed without affecting
breakdown potential (surface potential at which the surface’s passive film breaks down). Results
indicated that precharged H2 deteriorated the passive film stability and pitting corrosion resistance
in these alloys, and the increase in nitrogen content of the alloy offsets the deleterious effect of
precharged H2. Luo and others (2017) found that H2 reduces the contents of Cr2O3 and N (NH3
dissociates into NH2) in the passive film of 2205 duplex SS and reduces the thickness of the passive
film. The passive film with low N content weakens the ability to reject corrosive anions (such as
Cl−), making the passive film more vulnerable to attack (Ningshen and others, 2006).

 23

 Experiences in a high-temperature (350°C bottomhole) geothermal Icelandic well showed
that HE can occur in conditions similar to underground gas storage (Karlsdottir and others, 2012).
During monitoring and discharge testing of a new high-temperature geothermal well in the Krafla
geothermal area in Iceland, it was discovered that the K55 carbon steel liner in the bottom of the
well was fractured and severely corroded. SEM–EDS examination of the parts from the steel liner
and tensile strength testing of the material revealed that the liner was severely corroded and parts
of it at an approximately 1600-m depth had experienced environmentally assisted cracking in the
form of HE.

 It is also noteworthy that H2 purity and pressure play a significant role in the HE effects of
steels (Barthélémy, 2007). It is generally recognized that the greater the purity of H2, the more
pronounced the embrittling effect since the impurities most frequently encountered in H2, such as
oxygen and water vapor, have an inhibiting effect on embrittlement (Barthélémy, 2007). Sulfur
dioxide (SO2) also has an inhibiting effect (Barthélémy, 2007). Other impurities such as CH4 and
N2 do not seem to have any appreciable effect. By contrast, some other impurities such as CO2,
and particularly H2S, have an accelerating effect on HE (Barthélémy, 2011). San Marchi and
Somerday (2008) found that O2, SO2, and carbon monoxide (CO) lowered the fatigue crack growth
rate for X42 steel at 6.9 MPa H2 pressure. Furthermore, a mixture containing H2 and CH4 had
lower fracture toughness than pure H2, suggesting slight embrittlement by NG. The same authors
presented data showing the inhibiting effects of O2 and CO on fatigue crack growth for low-alloy
Cr–Mo steels at 1.1 MPa. It is also recognized that HE increases when the partial pressure of H2
increases. Studies using AISI 321 steel have shown a maximum embrittlement effect of H2 attained
between 20 and 100 bar (Barthélémy, 2007). However, steels can be very sensitive to HE even at
very low pressure (a few bars), which can give rise to a very pronounced embrittling effect. In tests
performed by Gutierrez-Solana and Elices (1982) on a typical Spanish pipeline carbon steel similar
to X42, tensile tests on double-notched specimens showed a significant reduction in area losses at
H2 pressures near 7 MPa.

 Potential for Microbially Induced Corrosion

 MIC has been another concern for the deterioration of metal structures in subsurface
environments. The annual cost of MIC in the United States is staggering, surpassing
US$2.7 trillion per year, making it a significant economic burden (Xu and others, 2023). The
principal corrosion mechanism in subsurface systems usually involves bacteria known as SRB,
similar to that discussed in the cement degradation section. A variety of SRB-influenced corrosion
mechanisms have been proposed. SRBs in the presence of H2 can perform sulfate reduction. Thus
generated sulfide reacts with iron, producing iron sulfide that initiates metal corrosion, as depicted
in Figure 7 (Hang, 2003; Dinh and others, 2004).

 24

 Figure 7. Schematic diagram of iron metal corrosion by SRB.

 The biocatalytic cathodic sulfate reduction (BCSR) theory was proposed by Gu and others
(2009). According to the BCSR theory, two MIC processes were proposed. Type I, extracellular
electron transfer (EET), refers to the transfer of electrons from the metal surface to the cytoplasm
of bacteria directly or by electron carriers, which are referred to as direct electron transfer (DET)
and mediated electron transfer (MET), respectively. Type II, metabolite-MIC (M-MIC), refers to
the corrosion caused by secreted metabolites. Accelerated corrosion rates in the presence of SRB
have been reported previously. A comparative study of electrochemical corrosion of 316LSS was
conducted in both a biotic SRB culturing medium and a sulfide-containing sterile solution.
Electrochemical behavior and corrosion products were investigated thoroughly by cyclic
voltammetry and various surface analysis techniques, including confocal laser scanning
microscopy (CLSM), SEM–EDS, and atomic force microscopy (AFM) (Chen and others, 2017).
Results demonstrated that sulfides generated from the SRB Desulfomicrobium metabolism resulted
in a heterogeneous iron sulfide film on the steel surface. Corrosion occurred at locations where
sulfides accumulated, increasing the surface roughness of the steel. MIC tests were carried out
using Type 304SS coupons covered with the Desulfovibrio vulgaris (ATCC 7757) biofilm (Zhang
and others, 2015). It was found that both riboflavin and flavin adenine dinucleotide (FAD), two
common electron mediators that enhance electron transfer, accelerated pitting corrosion and
weight loss on the coupons when 10 ppm (w/w) of either was added to the culture medium in 7-
day anaerobic lab tests. Similarly, the SRB-induced corrosion test of 2205 duplex SS (DSS) was
investigated via electrochemical measurements, surface analysis by SEM, XPS, and focused ion
beam–transmission electron microscopy (FIB–TEM) (Cui and others, 2020). After 28 days of
immersion, the corrosion rate of coupons in the SRB-inoculated medium was 10 times higher than
that of coupons in the sterile medium. Corrosion products were absent from the top part of the
biofilm where acidic −COOH was present, which indicated corrosion was not caused by

 25

metabolites. SRB clung to the matrix and mutually dissolved, which may have favored the electron
transfer mechanism. The observed accelerated corrosion was partially attributed to breakage of the
passive film thereby causing continuing damage and corrosion pits. The same authors studied
SRB–X80 steel interaction and discovered more corrosion pits in the SRB-inoculated sample than
in the sterile sample (Cui and others, 2021). Carbon starvation tests showed coupons immersed in
0% and 10% carbon source media exhibited far more aggressive corrosion than those in 100%
carbon. Both metabolite and EET were reported as corrosion mechanisms in this study, while EET
was the predominant mechanism in the carbon source reduced media.

 Mitigation of Potential Wellbore Corrosion

 The studies investigated indicate that low-strength carbon steels are more suitable than high-
strength steels for H2 storage applications. These studies also indicate that the use of HNSS can
potentially offset the deleterious effect of H2 to some extent. N element in the passive film can
neutralize the localized acidification in the pit by forming NH3 or NH4+, suppressing the
propagation from metastable to stable pitting. Furthermore, high Ni content (>12%) can also
increase H2-induced fracture resistance. It must also be noted that most of the H2-aided corrosion
laboratory work is performed on H2-precharged specimens that are only a simulation of the real
scenario.

SUMMARY OF FINDINGS

 The main goal and objectives of this literature survey were to review the existing research
investigating potential H2 effects on well materials, make recommendations for future work by
assessing any research gaps, provide strategies for maintaining wellbore integrity, and establish a
good understanding of mechanisms that can be responsible for loss of wellbore integrity, including
fluid migration through existing leakage pathways, casing microbial corrosion, H2 blistering and
HE, cement degradation, and elastomer failure.

 There are several mitigation strategies that can be derived from laboratory-based research
associated with potential H2-induced failures in well construction such as well cement, elastomers,
and casing materials. For example, cycling H2 through the well using Class H cement is
recommended over other cement classes. Carbon- or silica-filled elastomers perform better than
the unfilled ones in high-pressure H2 environments, with silica-filled ones being the better
performers. Studies also indicate that low-strength carbon steels are more suitable than high-
strength steels for H2 storage applications.

 Cement exposure to H2 may or may not affect its mechanical strength significantly,
regardless of the applied geochemical conditions and cement type. The studies investigated have
shown that cement exposure to H2 generates minerals such as Ca(OH)2(s) and
Ca6(Al[OH]6)2·(SO4)2·26H2O that can precipitate within larger pore spaces, causing an overall
increase in the cement’s mechanical strength and resultant well integrity. However, it also appears
that these minerals can lead to expansion, internal stress, and degradation of cement materials.
Another factor that can contribute to cement degradation is primarily associated with redox-
sensitive minerals as cement components. Although the proportion of these redox-sensitive

 26

minerals within the cement slurry is relatively small, H2-induced reductive dissolution can still
weaken the cement’s strength over the long term, potentially jeopardizing wellbore integrity. Given
that Class G and H cement types are the most commonly used in the oil and gas industry at high
temperature and pressure conditions, it is worth noting that the water:cement ratio of Class H
cement (38%) is lower than that of Class G cement (44%) (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). A recent
numerical simulation study of H2 diffusion in cement sheaths also showed that cement with a low
water:cement ratio is desirable for H2 storage wells (Dudun and others, 2023). While the
precipitation of Ca(OH)2(s) and C–S–H compounds upon cement exposure to H2 has been reported
to fill the open pore spaces and impact well integrity, the adverse effect of chemically or
biologically produced H2S leading to the deterioration of cement materials cannot be ignored.

 Elastomers used as sealing assemblies in UHS operations are potentially prone to failure
regardless of the elastomer type because of several factors, namely stresses from pressure cycling
associated with injection and production operations, pressure and temperature conditions, and
chemical degradation. Among the common elastomer materials used in the oil and gas industry,
NBR has better mechanical properties than FKM but is limited by its low chemical resistance.
However, relative to NBR, HNBR has improved temperature and chemical resistance in corrosive
H2S and CO2 environments, can operate under high-pressure conditions, and has a lower risk of
failure under decompression (Ugarte and Salehi, 2022). Studies show that carbon- and silica-filled
elastomers outperform their unfilled counterparts in high-pressure H2 environments, with silica-
filled elastomers performing best. Industries have been exploring alternatives such as metal-to-
metal seals, particularly for applications in harsh chemical environments and extreme temperature
and pressure conditions. However, cost, lack of ductility, and elasticity have been a major concern
with these metal seals. As such, elastomer is still widely used and the preferred seal material
primarily because of lower cost, resilience, and its ability to seal against irregular and dynamic
surfaces (Tu and Cheng, 2016).

 H2-induced metal corrosion is a challenging issue. The interactions between metal casing
and H2 could deteriorate mechanical strength over time, which may produce significant impacts
both operationally and environmentally. Studies indicate that HNSS can potentially offset the
deleterious effects of H2 to some extent. Furthermore, high Ni content (>12%) can also increase
H2-induced fracture resistance. It must also be noted that most of the H2-aided corrosion laboratory
work is performed on H2-precharged specimens that are only a simulation of the real scenario. In
a laboratory-controlled environment, the H2-charging process is rapid and consistent and may be
modified by changing the charging time or current. However, the charging process is not consistent
in practice and the conditions under which precharging occurs are generally not relevant to service
in high-pressure H2 gas. Therefore, it is very important to consider the possible subsurface
geochemical conditions, casing flow conditions, stress level, and specific material composition
(Shehata and El-Shamy, 2023). In addition to improving the structure of steel components, some
advanced methods have been found to reduce H2 damage. Chen and others (2018) found that
graphene coating can effectively reduce H2 damage. Compared with the α-Fe2O3 film without the
graphene coating, the work function increased under both compression and tensile strain, showing
that the strained surface layer of the graphene coating has better corrosion resistance, even on
deformed steels.

 27

RECOMMENDATIONS FOR CONTINUED RESEARCH

 Despite the wide range of analytical methods used to evaluate wellbore integrity, several
research gaps still exist. The following suggestions are presented to advance research toward
improved mitigation of potential H2-induced impacts to wellbore cement, elastomers, and casing
materials during UHS operations:

 • Conducting comprehensive rock mechanics and rheological tests on different cement
 class types at reservoir temperature and pressure conditions in a controlled environment
 (to minimize leaching and reactions unrelated to H2 exposure) in a laboratory-scale
 setting before initiating field trials is essential. Given that H2–cement reactions are very
 slow, it is critical to conduct experiments for extended periods to understand the long-
 term impact, particularly to simulate multiple injection/withdrawal cycles of H2.
 Therefore, integration of sensor technology for real-time monitoring of cement integrity
 in UHS could be beneficial to trace the development of any potential voids or fractures
 within the cement seal.

 • To further maintain wellbore integrity, the elastomer assembly used must be resistant to
 the risk of decompression to ensure longevity. The risk of decompression increases with
 significant changes in temperature. Therefore, in addition to developing new elastomeric
 materials, a system that can control the gas decompression rate in UHS target conditions
 could be developed for better durability of the UHS system.

 • Wellbore casing is another integral component that could be susceptible to H2-induced
 impacts. Most of the H2-aided metal casing corrosion laboratory work is performed on
 H2-precharged specimens that are only a simulation of a real-world scenario. However,
 the charging process during commercial operations is not consistent and the conditions
 under which precharging is performed are generally not indicative of high-pressure
 operations. H2 diffusion and buildup inside the steel are significantly affected by stress
 conditions. Therefore, investigations involving in situ H2 charging under stress are
 necessary.

 • Developing new materials that are more resistant to H2-induced corrosion is critical. For
 example, high-strength steels, alloys, and coatings could be designed to resist H2
 permeation. In addition, more effective corrosion inhibitors to reduce the rate of corrosion
 could be developed.

 • Investigating the effects of different environmental factors such as temperature, pressure,
 pH, and the presence of other gases on H2-induced corrosion should be considered to
 develop more accurate corrosion models and prevention strategies. Developing predictive
 models that can estimate the probability of H2-induced corrosion occurring in different
 scenarios is critical to significantly improve safety and reduce the economic and
 environmental impact of potential failures.

 APPENDIX D

GEOCHEMICAL EFFECTS ON UNDERGROUND
 H2 STORAGE IN THE BROOM CREEK
  FORMATION AND DICKINSON LODGEPOLE
 MOUNDS

 GEOCHEMICAL EFFECTS ON UNDERGROUND H2 STORAGE IN THE
 BROOM CREEK FORMATION AND DICKINSON LODGEPOLE MOUNDS

 The storage of hydrogen in geologic formations involves geochemical interactions between
rocks and fluids, which are crucial for understanding and optimizing underground hydrogen
storage (UHS). Geochemical reactions within these environments can involve stored hydrogen,
cushion gas, reservoir fluids, and minerals, leading to various processes that may influence storage
efficiency and safety (Hemme and van Berk, 2018; Bo et al., 2021; Saeed et al., 2023). These
reactions can be classified into abiotic (nonmicrobial) and biotic (microbial) categories, with
specific parameters affecting the biogeochemical processes.

 Abiotic reactions primarily involve mineral, redox, and aqueous processes. For instance,
mineral dissolution and precipitation can modify the porosity and permeability of reservoir rocks,
thereby influencing storage capacity and flow behavior. These alterations are significant as they
can impact the reservoir’s ability to efficiently store and release hydrogen. Additionally, changes
in the geomechanical properties of the rock may occur, potentially compromising the overall
integrity of the reservoir or caprock (Aluah et al., 2024). Understanding these processes through
geochemical modeling enables prediction of reservoir property changes and supports the
optimization of storage operations.

 Biotic reactions, in contrast, involve microbial activity that can substantially affect the
storage environment. Key biotic processes include methanogenesis, acetogenesis, and sulfate
reduction, which convert hydrogen into methane, acetate, and hydrogen sulfide, respectively
(Thaysen et al., 2021). These reactions are facilitated by microorganisms such as methanogens,
acetogens, and sulfate-reducing bacteria. They can lead to hydrogen consumption and the
production of other gases, thereby influencing the purity and volume of stored hydrogen. The
activity of these microorganisms depends on various factors, including temperature, salinity, and
nutrient or reactant availability (Thaysen et al., 2021).

 This study examines potential geochemical reactions, with a focus on abiotic interactions
between hydrogen and minerals and fluids in storage reservoir rocks. The goal is to understand
geochemical impacts and capture experimental observations. The research evaluates how these
reactions affect mineralogy, porosity, permeability, and potential hydrogen losses. By analyzing
two geologic formations, Broom Creek and Lodgepole, the study aims to provide comprehensive
insights into the geochemical processes governing hydrogen storage in these formations. Each
formation presents unique conditions, such as water chemistry, mineral composition, porosity, and
permeability, that influence these interactions. The methodology employed in this study is detailed
in the subsequent section.

Methodology and Data

 This study employed a static, nontransport model developed with PHREEQC Version 3
(Parkhurst and Appelo, 2013), utilizing a Python script as a pre- and postprocessor to simulate
both equilibrium and kinetic reactions relevant to UHS within the two potential geologic
formations. PHREEQC is a geochemical modeling software package developed by the U.S.

 D-1

Geological Survey. The equilibrium reactions include aqueous interactions within the formation
brine and the solubility of H2 in brine. These reactions are modeled to represent the chemical
processes occurring within the brine. However, mineral reactions are simulated kinetically to
account for their time-dependent nature, allowing for a dynamic, time-kinetic analysis of the
system over the simulation period. The model utilizes a single-cell approach that concentrates on
the geochemical effects of hydrogen storage, including hydrogen loss and alterations in brine, rock
mineralogy, porosity, and permeability following geochemical reactions. This method aids in
understanding how chemical reactions influence the physical properties of the reservoir.

 Mineral dissolution and precipitation reactions lead to changes in mineral phases within the
reservoir, subsequently impacting the overall porosity and permeability of the rock. Changes in
porosity as a result of rock dissolution and precipitation are determined using solid and pore
volume calculations ([Eq. D-1). The porosity of the rock (∅) can be calculated as follows:
 𝑉𝑉𝑝𝑝
                                          ∅ = 𝑉𝑉 +𝑉𝑉                                          [Eq. D-1]
 𝑠𝑠 𝑝𝑝

where 𝑉𝑉𝑝𝑝 is the pore volume (liter) and 𝑉𝑉𝑠𝑠 is the solid (rock) volume (liter). The changes in porosity
arise as a result of the rock dissolution and precipitation due to mineral reactions as modeled in
PHREEQC. The porosity change affects the reservoir’s storage capacity.

 To enhance the robustness of the model, the simulation duration was aligned with that of the
batch reaction experiments conducted as part of the project.

 Incorporating Python facilitated input management and enabled seamless interaction
between equilibrium and kinetic reactions within the PHREEQC framework, resulting in a
streamlined workflow and comprehensive analysis. This methodology ensures that significant
changes in reservoir properties caused by geochemical reactions are accurately captured.

 The role of the H2 redox reaction in UHS remains not fully understood (Zhan et al., 2021,
Saeed and Jadhawar, 2024). Consequently, PHREEQC models for each sample, Broom Creek and
Lodgepole, were executed with both coupled and decoupled H2 redox processes to better elucidate
the impact of H2 redox reactions. The H2 redox reaction can be described as shown in [Eq. D-2
(Parkhurst and Appelo, 2013):

                                    2 𝐻𝐻 + + 2 𝑒𝑒 − ↔ 𝐻𝐻2                                     [Eq. D-2]

 In developing the geochemical models, several assumptions were made to ensure consistent
and realistic simulations of UHS. Baseline conditions were established using the initial reservoir
pressure and temperature for each formation. Both initial and final pH values from the experiments
were incorporated as inputs to enhance prediction accuracy. The mineralogical composition of
each formation was averaged to simplify the model while preserving key geologic properties. All
minerals were modeled kinetically to reflect their time-dependent reactions, except for ankerite,
chamosite, gypsum, halite, hydrophilite, magnetite, magnesite, orthoclase, sylvite, and dawsonite,
due to the absence of kinetic parameters in the literature. Minor minerals, accounting for less than
0.5 wt%, were largely disregarded to focus on the predominant geochemical interactions.

 D-2

 The input data needed for geochemical modeling include water chemistry, rock mineralogy,
underground conditions, and porosity. The water chemistry data used for geochemical modeling
in this work for each formation are shown in Table D-1. The rock mineralogical composition for
each formation is shown in Table , and the porosity and conditions data for the three formations
are shown in Table .
