Results and Analysis

 The experimental study was designed to explore the interactions between hydrogen, rock
minerals, and brine under simulated subsurface conditions. The objective was to assess the
geochemical effects of hydrogen on two different rock types, sandstone and carbonate, in the
presence of brine, which serves as the reactive fluid medium. Core samples from both the Broom
Creek and Lodgepole were prepared and divided into smaller segments for testing. Each sample
was placed in a reactor, and brine was introduced into the reactor chambers first to saturate the
rock pores followed by hydrogen injection to create a reactive environment up to 3000 psi. The
system was maintained at a constant pressure of 3000 psi and monitored for 30 days at controlled
temperatures, as shown in Table . In both samples, the pH value decreased after the batch reaction
experiments, as shown in Figure D-1.

  Figure D-1. Brine pH values before and after the batch reaction experiments for the Broom
  Creek and Lodgepole samples.

 D-4

 The Broom Creek sandstone samples exhibited significant geochemical reactions during the
experiments. Notably, anhydrite and dolomite underwent substantial dissolution. This process
weakened the cementing agents that bind the sandstone grains, potentially affecting the rock’s
structural integrity. An unexpected finding was the precipitation of halite (sodium chloride), a
mineral not anticipated under the experimental conditions. Furthermore, the brine displayed
increased concentrations of sodium (Na) and chloride (Cl) despite no apparent external sodium
source. This suggests possible experimental artifacts, such as evaporation effects or incomplete
control of initial conditions. Postreaction gas analysis revealed a 97% concentration of hydrogen
and traces of nitrogen, likely resulting from impurities rather than reaction products.

 The Lodgepole samples exhibited a different pattern of geochemical interactions. Calcite,
the primary mineral in carbonate rocks, underwent dissolution, leading to the creation of new pore
spaces. However, these pores were subsequently partially filled by the precipitation of anhydrite
and halite. A similar observation in terms of the simultaneous halite precipitation and Na/Cl brine
concentration increase was made in the Lodgepole experiments. Another similar finding was the
presence of N2 traces in the retrieved gas after the experiments. This nitrogen contamination was
likely due to impurities introduced during the hydrogen injection process, either from the gas
cylinder or the injection lines.

 These experimental findings highlight the complexity and uncertainty in hydrogen–brine–
rock interactions, revealing both expected dissolution reactions and unexpected precipitation
phenomena. The inconsistencies, such as halite precipitation and nitrogen contamination, indicate
the need for a more refined experimental design to isolate and better understand these processes.

 The brine composition results in Figure D-2 show a comparison of the PHREEQC models,
redox-coupled and decoupled, with the experimental results of the Broom Creek test. Analysis of
the results shows a good agreement between the models and the experimental results. The redox-
decoupled model aligns more closely with experimental observations, particularly for calcium
(0.0532 mol/kg) and magnesium (0.0321 mol/kg) concentrations, suggesting limited hydrogen
redox activity under the tested conditions. However, neither model accurately predicts the
unexpected increase in sodium (2.72 mol/kg experimentally versus 2.21 and 2.44 mol/kg in the
coupled and decoupled models, respectively) and chloride concentrations observed experimentally.
Overall, while the decoupled model provides a more accurate representation of experimental trends,
both models provide satisfactory results to predict brine composition, with further refinement
required to match the experimental values.

 D-5

 Figure D-2. Comparison of experimental and modeling results for the Broom Creek water
 elemental composition after the batch reaction experiment.

 Figure D-3 presents a comparison of experimental results with the redox-coupled and
decoupled models for the Lodgepole Formation. It reveals some discrepancies in the predicted
geochemical behavior of the carbonate sample. For calcium, the experimental concentration
(0.264 mol/kg) falls between the predictions of the coupled model (0.399 mol/kg) and the
decoupled model (0.234 mol/kg), with the latter being closer to the experimental value. Chloride
concentrations, critical for understanding halite behavior, are underestimated by both models (8.25
and 8.20 mol/kg for the coupled and decoupled models, respectively) compared to the
experimental value of 9.65 mol/kg. Sodium concentrations follow a similar pattern, with the
experimental value (5.75 mol/kg) exceeding both model predictions (4.44 mol/kg for the coupled
model and 4.69 mol/kg for the decoupled model), indicating limitations in simulating the potential
water vaporization observed in the experiments. Sulfate concentrations are underestimated by the
decoupled model (0.00215 mol/kg) compared to the experimental value of 0.012 mol/kg, which
might indicate a potential need to refine how anhydrite dissolution and precipitation are modeled.
Moreover, the redox-coupled model significantly overestimates the carbon elements (0.151 mol/kg)
compared to the experimental value (0.0024 mol/kg), while the decoupled model underpredicts
the value, i.e., less than 1E-5 mol/kg. Overall, the decoupled model provides a closer
approximation to experimental observations for key elements, but neither model fully captures the
brine compositions observed in the experiment for the Lodgepole Formation.

 D-6

 Figure D-3. Comparison of experimental and modeling results for the Lodgepole water
 elemental composition after the batch reaction experiment.

 The mineralogical changes observed experimentally in the Broom Creek sample for
anhydrite, dolomite, and halite were compared with the modeling results and are detailed in
Table D-2. Although XRD measurements, notable observations from the experiments were used
to evaluate the geochemical activity trends predicted by the developed models. For anhydrite, the
experimental results showed significant dissolution, which the redox-coupled model overpredicted
as complete dissolution (−100%), while the redox-decoupled model estimated a more realistic 23%
reduction. Similarly, for dolomite, the experimental data indicated substantial dissolution, with the
coupled model again predicting complete dissolution (−100%) and the decoupled model
estimating 96%, aligning better with observations. These results suggest that the decoupled model
might provide a closer representation of carbonate mineral behavior under the experimental
conditions. However, the high dissolution rates in both models indicate that more accurate kinetic
reaction parameters are needed for the carbonate minerals.

 Halite precipitation, observed experimentally, presents a unique inconsistency, as both
models predicted complete dissolution (−100%), failing to replicate the precipitation. This
discrepancy may be attributed to an uncontrolled experimental environment, potentially involving
water evaporation that concentrated dissolved ions and promoted halite reprecipitation. Such
evaporation effects would not be accounted for in the PHREEQC models. These observations
emphasize the need for tighter control over environmental factors, such as humidity and brine
volume, to reduce uncertainties. Overall, the redox-decoupled model performed slightly better in
predicting the observed mineralogy changes, especially the anhydrite precipitation phenomena,
than the redox-coupled model.

 The changes in porosity (Figure D-4a) further illustrate the impact of mineralogical
alterations predicted by the models. However, there are no experimental measurements to confirm
the model-predicted values. Both the redox-coupled and decoupled models predict similar
decreases in porosity, with the coupled model showing a reduction of 3.1% and the decoupled
model a slightly smaller reduction of 3.0%. These minimal decreases suggest that pore space is
being reduced due to mineral precipitation, particularly involving illite, pyrite, and orthoclase in
the redox-coupled model and chlorite, microcline, and orthoclase in the redox-decoupled model.
These findings demonstrate that, despite differences in the dissolution and precipitation patterns
for specific minerals, both models predict a comparable net effect on reservoir porosity,
emphasizing the need to validate these predictions against experimental results. Porosity
measurements post-batch reaction experiments are recommended to ensure reliability.

 Figure D-4. Model-predicted porosity changes before the H2–brine–rock interactions in the
 a) Broom Creek and b) Lodgepole.

 The experimental observations and modeling results of the mineralogy changes in the
Lodgepole sample are detailed in Table D-3. Experimentally, anhydrite showed significant
precipitation compared to the redox-coupled model, which predicted complete dissolution
(−100%). The redox-decoupled model, however, aligned with the observed trend by predicting
substantial anhydrite precipitation (2657%), suggesting it more accurately represents the chemical
conditions leading to anhydrite supersaturation and precipitation.

 Calcite exhibited further variability between experimental and modeling results.
Experimental data indicated minor calcite dissolution, whereas the redox-coupled model predicted
complete calcite dissolution (−100%), overestimating its reactivity. Conversely, the redox-
decoupled model predicted 7% calcite precipitation, which does not match the observed
dissolution but suggests that the decoupled model stabilizes calcite under the given conditions. For
dolomite, both models predicted complete dissolution (−100%), while the experiment did not
reveal any changes. Lastly, halite precipitation observed experimentally was well represented by
both models, with the redox-coupled model showing considerable precipitation and the decoupled
model slightly less but still significant halite accumulation. These outcomes emphasize the better
alignment of the decoupled model with experimental precipitation trends, although inconsistencies
in calcite behavior highlight areas for further refinement.

 The porosity changes predicted by the redox-coupled and decoupled models show
contrasting potential impacts on reservoir properties between the Broom Creek and Lodgepole
(Figure D-4). In the redox-coupled model, porosity decreases significantly by 3.0%. These
reductions reflect the model’s prediction of widespread mineral dissolution followed by
precipitation, leading to pore-filling. Conversely, the redox-decoupled model predicts minimal
changes, with porosity increasing slightly by 0.2%. This suggests that the decoupled model
assumes more balance between the precipitation and dissolution activity in the rock matrix.
Porosity measurements post-batch reaction experiments should be conducted in future tests to
confirm the porosity model predictions.

 In the Broom Creek modeling results (Figure D-5), the redox-coupled model predicted that
H2 constitutes the majority of the gas phase at 87%, with notable contributions from CH4 (8%),
H2O (2%), and H2S (3%). However, these gases were not observed experimentally, indicating that
the redox-coupled model may overestimate the extent of hydrogen’s chemical reactivity in the
system. Conversely, the redox-decoupled model predicts an inert behavior for hydrogen, with
99.7% of the gas phase composed of H2 and only 0.3% H2O. This reflects an assumption of
minimal redox activity, aligning more closely with experimental observations that did not show
the production of gas by-products such as H2S and CH4. These results suggest that the decoupled
model provides a more realistic representation of gas behavior in subsurface hydrogen storage
scenarios, assuming external conditions remain stable and H2 redox reactions are limited.

 D-11

  Figure D-5. Average gas composition for the Broom Creek test as predicted by the a) H2
  redox-coupled model and b) H2 redox-decoupled model.

 The gas composition model predictions (Figure D-6) for the Lodgepole Formation are
compared between the redox-coupled and decoupled models to evaluate their assumptions
regarding H2 reactivity and stability. In the redox-coupled model, H2 is predicted to dominate the
gas phase at 98%, with minor contributions from CH4 (1%) and H2O (1%). This contrasts with the
experimental observations which showed no trace of gases other than H2 and N2 as an impurity.

 Conversely, the redox-decoupled model predicts even greater inertness, with H2 comprising
99% of the gas phase and the remaining 1% as H2O. This reflects a scenario with limited redox
activity, where hydrogen remains chemically stable and unchanged. The absence of CH4, H2S, and
CO2 in the decoupled model supports its assumption of minimal reactivity, aligning with
experimental measurements that suggest limited gas-phase reactions in hydrogen–brine–rock
systems.

Discussion and Recommendations

 The experimental observations from the Broom Creek and Lodgepole tests indicated notable
interactions between hydrogen, brine, and rock under subsurface conditions. In the Broom Creek
sandstone, significant dissolution of anhydrite and dolomite was observed, weakening cementation
and potentially compromising rock integrity. Unexpected precipitation of halite and an increase in
sodium and chloride concentrations were noted, indicating experimental artifacts or unaccounted
mechanisms (e.g., water vaporization). Similarly, in the Lodgepole carbonate, calcite dissolution
created new pore spaces later filled by anhydrite and halite precipitation. Both experiments
revealed traces of nitrogen in the gas phase, attributed to impurities during hydrogen injection.

 D-12

 Figure D-6. Average gas composition for the Lodgepole test as predicted by the a) H2 redox-
 coupled model and b) H2 redox-decoupled model.

 The redox-decoupled model provides a closer approximation to experimental observations
compared to the redox-coupled model. For brine chemistry, the decoupled model aligns more
closely with experimental concentrations of calcium, magnesium, and other major ions, suggesting
limited hydrogen redox activity under the tested conditions. While discrepancies, such as
underestimated sodium and chloride concentrations (potentially due to water vaporization), persist,
the decoupled model’s assumptions of minimal reactivity appear more representative of the
observed experimental trends. Similarly, in terms of rock mineralogy, the decoupled model better
reflects the partial dissolution trends of anhydrite and dolomite, and it aligns more closely with
observed mineral precipitation, such as halite, which was not predicted by the coupled model.

 The decoupled model supports the theory that the observed reactions in the experiments are
primarily caused by brine–rock disequilibrium rather than hydrogen reactions. This interpretation
is supported by the experimental setup, where the brine and rock were not equilibrated before
testing, creating a chemically reactive system independent of hydrogen’s role. The model’s better
alignment with mineralogical changes, including partial dissolution of anhydrite and dolomite and
halite precipitation, further supports this theory. While anhydrite dissolution in the presence of H2
has been reported in previous experimental studies (Flesch et al., 2018), other studies have
maintained limited or no reactivity between the gas and the mineral (Fatah et al., 2024; Ebere and
Jabbari, 2024).

 Gas composition results further support the redox-decoupled model’s accuracy.
Experimentally, the gas phase remained largely composed of hydrogen with no significant by-
products such as methane, hydrogen sulfide, or carbon dioxide. The decoupled model mirrors this
inert behavior, predicting 99% H2 with minimal water vapor, whereas the coupled model
overestimates reactivity, predicting methane and hydrogen sulfide that were not observed
experimentally. These findings support minimal abiotic redox assumptions in modeling hydrogen
storage systems, where the chemical stability of hydrogen is critical for long-term storage
feasibility.

 D-13

Conclusions and Recommendations

 The combination of experimental and modeling approaches provided valuable insights into
hydrogen–brine–rock interactions. The following are the main conclusions from this work:

 1. Batch reaction experimental results revealed interactions within the H2–brine–rock
 system, including mineral dissolution and precipitation with unexpected observation of
 simultaneous halite precipitation and an increase in Na/Cl concentrations in the brine in
 both tests.

 2. The redox-decoupled model aligned better with experimental data, indicating minimal
 hydrogen reactivity, while the redox-coupled model overestimated mineral dissolution
 and by-product gas generation such as H2S and CH4.

 3. Observed discrepancies highlighted the need to equilibrate the system, suggesting some
 reactions stemmed from brine–rock disequilibrium rather than direct hydrogen effects.

 4. Future experiments including pre-equilibrating samples, controlling impurities,
 conducting post-exposure XRD and porosity measurements, and improving gas and water
 mass measurements are crucial to calibrate geochemical models for H2–rock–fluid
 reactions in UHS. Additionally, repeating experiments with nitrogen instead of hydrogen
 would help isolate the specific effects of hydrogen on geochemical processes.

 To improve the reliability of future experiments based on the findings of this study, the
following recommendations are proposed:

 1. Pre-equilibrate brine and rock samples before the initial XRD and brine chemistry
 analysis to minimize reactions driven by disequilibrium and isolate the role of hydrogen
 more effectively.

 2. Control for impurities in the hydrogen injection process to avoid unintended influences
 on the observed geochemical reactions.

 3. Enhance precision in gas and water mass measurements to improve data quality and
 reduce uncertainties.

 4. Conduct experiments with additional controls, such as monitoring variables like humidity,
 brine volume, and other external factors that could contribute to unexpected outcomes.

 5. Perform XRD measurement after the batch reaction experiments to properly characterize
 the mineralogy changes in the rock samples.

 6. Measure porosity and permeability after exposure to understand the impact of
 geochemical reactions on the petrophysical properties of the rocks.

 D-14

 7. Use N₂ instead of H2 in parallel experiments to assess whether the observed reactions are
 gas-specific or primarily influenced by broader geochemical system dynamics.

 Implementing these recommendations will improve the robustness of the experimental setup,
allowing a more precise assessment of hydrogen’s reactivity in subsurface environments. These
improvements will also enhance the predictive accuracy of the geochemical models which can
consequently be upscaled to field-scale models.

 APPENDIX E

 GEOMECHANICAL MODELING FOR
UNDERGROUND H2 STORAGE IN THE DUNHAM
 SALT CAVERN

 GEOMECHANICAL MODELING FOR UNDERGROUND H2 STORAGE IN THE
 DUNHAM SALT CAVERN

 The stability of the rocks surrounding salt caverns is a critical factor in hydrogen storage.
The cavern should withstand repeated cycles of hydrogen injection and withdrawal over many
years without significant creep deformation or volumetric shrinkage, and the rocks should not fail
or fracture during high-pressure hydrogen injection cycles due to stress changes (Munson et al.,
1998; Naderi et al., 2024). This activity focused on short-term stability analysis during high-
pressure hydrogen injection periods. A modified Barton–Bandis (B-B) model was utilized to
examine factors leading to tensile fracture opening and shear failure in cavern walls and caprocks
using Computer Modelling Group Ltd.’s (CMG’s) GEM simulator (Computer Modelling Group
Ltd., 2021).

 Underground rocks are composed of grains that form the rock’s framework and porous
media filled with formation fluids. Overlying and surrounding rocks exert compressive forces from
both vertical and horizontal directions on all rock fragments, resulting in compressive stress within
each piece. The total stress experienced by a rock fragment is divided into two components:

 1. Effective stress refers to the component of stress transmitted through the solid framework
 of rocks. It is considered positive when the stress is compressive.

 2. Pore pressure refers to the fluid pressure within the porous media of rocks, opposing the
 total stress exerted on the formation.

 Based on Terzaghi’s effective stress principle (Terzaghi, 1923), injecting hydrogen into the
cavern results in increased pore pressure within the surrounding rocks and a reduction in effective
stress. This change can elevate the risk of shear and tensile failures in geologic formations.

Tensile Failure Identification

 During hydrogen injection, tensile fractures may develop if rock tensile strength is
insufficient to resist internal stress. Continuous injection can reduce the effective stress to zero and
convert it into negative (tensile) stress. When the magnitude of this negative effective stress
surpasses the tensile strength of the rock, tensile fractures are likely to occur.

 The B-B model is a constitutive model that describes the behavior of joints, such as fractures
and faults, under shear or tensile stress (Bandis et al., 1983; Zhao et al., 2020). In this study, a
modified version of the B-B model is used to simulate the opening and closing of fractures at all
possible locations in the model due to tensile failure. This process is enabled by using a dual-
permeability system consisting of matrix and fracture grids. The stresses and strains are computed
in the matrix grids. The opening and closing of fractures are represented by the changing fracture
permeability in the fracture grids based on the stresses calculated in these matrix grid blocks (Tran
et al., 2010; Mohamed et al., 2020; Badree and Alexander 2021; Fan et al., 2022; Gillioz et al.,
2024).

 E-1

 The model assumes that natural fractures are initially assigned a very low permeability and
are distributed throughout all matrix blocks, except for the cavern zone. This assumption mimics
the initial no-fracture condition and allows for the simulation of fracture opening or closing due to
stress changes at all potential locations. The stresses within the matrix grid blocks control the
opening and closing of the fractures by changing the fracture permeability, while the matrix
permeability remains unaffected.

 The process of fracture opening and closing in the modified B-B model is described below
and shown in Figure .

 • Point A: Initially the fractures are completely closed with an extremely low permeability.

 • Path AB: As injection continues, the pore pressure in the matrix increases, reducing the
 normal effective stress acting on the fracture walls (𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 ), which is important for fluid flow
 in the corresponding fracture. Along Path AB, fracture permeability remains extremely
 low, and the behavior is reversible.

 • Path BC: As the 𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 decreases until it reaches the fracture opening stress (𝐹𝐹𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟 ), tensile
 failure occurs, and the fracture instantly increases to its maximum permeability (𝐾𝐾ℎ𝑓𝑓 ).
 The absolute value of 𝐹𝐹𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟 is equivalent to the tensile strength of the material.

 • Path DCE: After fracture permeability reaches 𝐾𝐾ℎ𝑓𝑓 , as long as 𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 is less than 0, fracture
 permeability maintains opening with the maximum permeability ( 𝐾𝐾ℎ𝑓𝑓 ). Path CE
 represents the process of reducing pore pressure and increasing 𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 .

 • Path EFG: As 𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 reaches 0 via the CE process, fracture permeability instantly reduces to
 the fracture closure permeability (𝐾𝐾𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐 ). As 𝜎𝜎′𝑛𝑛 continues to increase through the FG
 process, fracture continues to close with decreasing fracture permeability; the current
 fracture permeability is calculated using Equation E-1.

 𝑒𝑒 4
 𝐾𝐾𝑓𝑓 = 𝐾𝐾𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐 � 𝑒𝑒𝑛𝑛� ≥ 𝐾𝐾𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟 [Eq. E-1]
 0

where 𝑒𝑒𝑛𝑛 and 𝑒𝑒0 are the initial and current fracture apertures. 𝑒𝑒𝑛𝑛 is stress-dependent on the
geomechanical modeling process.

 E-2

 Figure E-1. Modified B-B model describing fracture opening and closing due to changing
 effective normal stress.

Shear Failure Identification

 Shear failure can be triggered during the hydrogen storage process due to the change in
pressure and stress state. The Mohr–Coulomb failure criterion was commonly used in previous
studies to identify potential shear failure at the surrounding rocks of the salt cavern during gas
storage (Böttcher et al., 2017; Li et al., 2020b).

 Figure E-2 schematically describes the Mohr–Coulomb failure criterion. 𝑂𝑂𝑂𝑂 and 𝜑𝜑 are two
critical parameters representing the cohesion and friction angle of the rocks. These two parameters
also define the Mohr–Coulomb failure line 𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷 that describes the shear strength of the rocks. The
Mohr circle is determined by the minimum and maximum principal effective stresses 𝜎𝜎′3 and 𝜎𝜎′1 .
As hydrogen is injected into the cavern, the pore pressure increases, causing a reduction of both
𝜎𝜎′3 and 𝜎𝜎′1 . The Mohr circle changes its size and moves to the left. If the Mohr circle touches or
intersects the failure line 𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷, shear failure occurs.

 E-3

 Figure E-2. Schematic description of the Mohr–Coulomb failure criterion.

The value of 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶/𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 is critical to shear failure, as shown in Equations E-2 through E-5.

                                                     (𝜎𝜎′1 −𝜎𝜎′3 )
                                            𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 =                                            [Eq. E-2]
 2

 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 = 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 ∙ 𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐 [Eq. E-3]

The location of point C is determined by:

                                                     (𝜎𝜎′1 +𝜎𝜎′3 )
                                            𝑂𝑂𝑂𝑂 =                                            [Eq. E-4]
 2

The shear safety factor (SF) is defined as:

 SF = 1 − (𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 𝑜𝑜𝑜𝑜 (1, 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶/𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶)) [Eq. E-5]

 If 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶/𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 exceeds 1, the SF turns into zero. When SF is greater than 0, the rocks remain
elastic and are safe from shear failure. However, when SF is zero or near zero, the rocks have
either failed or are close to failure.

Base Model Setup

 The base model consists of three layers: the Poe and Dunham Salt Members of the Piper
Formation and the Saude Member of the Spearfish Formation. A cylindrically shaped cavern is
located at the center of the Dunham Salt layer. The base model was built by expanding the flow

 E-4

model in horizontal directions to ensure that the near-cavern stresses were not significantly
affected by model boundaries. The length, width, and height of the base model were 624, 624, and
150 ft, with 39, 39, and 22 grids in the X, Y, and Z directions, respectively. The Poe, Dunham Salt,
and Saude Members correspond to the layers 1 to 4, 5 to 18, and 19 to 22, respectively, in the Z
direction. A cylindrically shaped cavern was located at layers 7 to 16 in the Z direction, as shown
in Figure E-3. Initially, sealed natural fractures were assigned to all the blocks of the base model,
with fracture spacing of 10 ft in the X, Y, and Z directions via the dual-permeability method. The
permeability of all the fractures was set to 1×10-8 mD. The purpose of adding these natural
fractures was to allow for the simulation of tensile fracture opening and closing using the modified
B-B model.

 Figure E-3. Three-dimensional (3D) view of the geomechanical model with a cylindrically
 shaped cavern at the center. Z direction Layers 1 to 10 were cut to visualize the cavern
 wall.

 Geomechanical modeling was coupled with fluid flow modeling during the simulation
process. At the cavern zone, the grids for geomechanical calculation were nulled but flow
calculation was performed for gas injection. Geomechanical properties including Young’s
modulus, Poisson’s ratio, tensile strength, cohesion, and friction angle were assigned to the base
model. Most of the geomechanical properties were obtained from the study by Smith et al. (2023),
as shown in Table . However, the cohesion and friction angle of the Dunham Salt was unknown.
Liang et al. (2007) performed laboratory experiments to evaluate the geomechanical properties of
rock salt, which was mainly composed of halite. The cohesion and friction angle of 595 psi and
31° were obtained and were used in the base model of this project. The distribution of Young’s
modulus in the base model, as an example of geomechanical property distribution, is shown in
Figure E-4.

 E-6

 Figure E-5. 1D MEM profile for calibration and interpretation of pore pressure and stresses
 displaced in equivalent mud weight.

 The effective stresses in the minimum horizontal, maximum horizontal, and vertical
directions (𝜎𝜎′ℎ𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 , 𝜎𝜎′𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻 , 𝜎𝜎′𝑣𝑣 ) were calculated using Equation E-6 and were used to initialize
the geomechanical model:

 𝜎𝜎 ′ = 𝜎𝜎 − 𝛼𝛼𝑃𝑃𝑝𝑝 [Eq. E-6]

where 𝜎𝜎, 𝜎𝜎′, 𝛼𝛼 , and 𝑃𝑃𝑝𝑝 represent total stresses, effective stresses, Biot’s coefficient, and pore
pressure, respectively. Biot’s coefficient 𝛼𝛼 is an uncertainty parameter ranging from 0 to 1. With
a greater Biot’s coefficient (𝛼𝛼) value, the effective stresses (𝜎𝜎′) are more sensitive to the change in
pore pressure (𝑃𝑃𝑝𝑝 ); tensile or shear failure due to hydrogen injection is more likely to occur. The
Biot’s coefficients of the Poe, Dunham Salt, and Saude layers were not tested. To represent the
worst-case scenario, the base model used 𝛼𝛼 values of 0.5 for the Dunham and 0.9 for other layers.
along depth is shown in Figure E-6. The Dunham Salt layers (depth ranging from approximately
6830 to 6900 ft) exhibit higher effective stresses than other layers, as shown in Figure E-6b. Since
the salt layer has lower 𝛼𝛼, the effective stresses of the salt rocks were less weakened by pore
pressure; a greater portion of the total stresses was carried by the skeleton of the salt rocks.

 E-7

 Figure E-6. Distribution of a) total stresses (𝜎𝜎ℎ𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 , 𝜎𝜎𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻 , 𝜎𝜎𝑣𝑣 ) and pore pressure (Pp) and
 b) effective stresses (𝜎𝜎′ℎ𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 , 𝜎𝜎′𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻 , 𝜎𝜎′𝑣𝑣 ) along depth for initialization of the
 geomechanical model.

 The base model simulated 731 days of debrining and hydrogen injection to evaluate the risks
of potential tensile and shear failures. Since the cavern was initially filled with brine, debrining
was required before hydrogen injection. The base model started simulation with 56 days of
debrining by injecting methane with a maximum injection pressure of 3600 psi and producing
brine with a maximum production rate of 4000 bpd. The bottomhole pressure (BHP) was
maintained at 3600 psi during the process. Then, the gas injector and producer were closed. After
96 days of no-operation period, continuous hydrogen injection was performed for 579 days with a
maximum injection pressure (Pmax) of 5639 psi and a maximum injection rate of 1 MMscf/d, as
shown in Figure E-7. The model applied the same Pmax to identify potential tensile and shear
failures.

 E-8

 Figure E-7. Change of BHP in the base model.

 Hydrogen injection results in an increase in pore pressure and a decrease in minimum
effective stress at the sidewalls and roof area surrounding the cavern zone, as shown in
Figures E-8 and E-9. The reduction in effective stresses due to increasing pore pressure leads to
potential risks of tensile and shear failures. However, tensile failure was not detected in the model.
The minimum effective stress of the whole model was above 0, as shown in Figure E-9, suggesting
the whole model was under compressive stress conditions and no tensile failure existed. The
fracture permeability of the whole model remained 1e-8 mD, as shown in Figure E-10, validating
that no tensile fractures were open during hydrogen injection.

 Figure E-8. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of pore
 pressure distribution for the base model: a) at initial reservoir condition on 1/1/2000 and
 b) after hydrogen injection on Day 731.

 E-9

 Figure E-9. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of
 minimum effective stress distribution for the base model: a) at initial reservoir condition on
 Day 1 and b) after hydrogen injection on Day 731.

 Figure E-10. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of
 fracture permeability distribution throughout the simulation period for the base model.

 Shear was not detected in the model either by the end of the hydrogen injection. The Shear
Safety Factor (SF) was above 0.5 in the whole model, as shown in Figure E-11, suggesting that
the salt cavern would be safe from shear failure during hydrogen injection. Figure E-11 also shows
that the cavern wall exhibited lower SF after hydrogen injection, suggesting that hydrogen
injection increased the risk of shear failure at the cavern wall. The sidewall presents a higher risk
of shear failure. This is due to different responses of minimum and maximum effective stresses at
the cavern sidewalls and other locations, causing the Mohr circle of the cavern sidewalls to reach
the Mohr–Coulomb failure line faster compared to that of other locations. Previous studies also
support that the cavern sidewalls present the risk of shear failure during gas storage (Li et al.,
2020b; Wang et al., 2023; Naderi et al., 2024).

 E-10

 Figure E-11. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of SF
 distribution for the base model: a) at initial reservoir condition on Day 1 and b) after
 hydrogen injection on Day 731.

Simulation Results and Discussions

 The base model suggests that the salt cavern was stable and free from tensile and shear
failures during hydrogen injection based on reservoir and operational conditions (Pmax of 5639 psi)
suggested by the study (Smith et al., 2023). Five additional cases were designed to investigate the
effect of increasing Pmax on the risks of tensile fracture opening and shear failure. The Pmax was
changed to 6000, 7000, 8000, 9000, and 10,000 psi, respectively, for the five additional cases.
Other model setups were the same as the base model.

 The results suggest that with larger Pmax, greater pore pressure increase was observed
surrounding the cavern sidewalls, roof, and caprocks after hydrogen injection, as shown in
Figure E-12. Increasing pore pressure at the surrounding rocks presents increasing risks of tensile
and shear failures.

 E-11

 Figure E-12. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of pore
 pressure distribution by the end of hydrogen injection on Day 731 for the cases with Pmax of
     a) 5639 psi, b) 6000 psi, c) 7000 psi, d) 8000 psi, e) 9000 psi, and f) 10,000 psi.

 The risk of shear failure with changing Pmax was evaluated for the six cases. With increasing
Pmax, the cavern sidewalls exhibited lower SF, indicating a higher risk of shear failure, as shown
in Figure E-13. The figure also shows that the highest risk of shear failure occurred at the block
(12, 20, 6) compared to other blocks. The block (12, 20, 6) was monitored for SF development for
the six cases, as shown in Figure E-14. The SF of the block (12, 20, 6) dropped to 0 when Pmax
reached or was above 9000 psi, suggesting the presence of shear failure.

 The modeling results suggest that despite increasing Pmax led to a higher risk of shear failure,
shear failure was not observed when BHP is under 8000 psi. When Pmax reached or exceeded
9000 psi, shear failure was only observed at a thin shell of the cavern wall, as shown in Figure E-
13e and f. It should be noted that the modeling results captured the location of shear failure, but
the initiation and propagation of the shear-induced fractures were not modeled. It is possible that
shear-induced fractures could maintain stability for a long time if the friction of the shear fractures
increases during the shearing process (Li et al., 2020b).

 E-12

 Figure E-13. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of SF
 distribution by the end of hydrogen injection for the cases with Pmax of a) 5639 psi,
   b) 6000 psi, c) 7000 psi, d) 8000 psi, e) 9000 psi, f) 10,000 psi.

Figure E-14. Development of SF at the block (12, 20, 6) for the cases with Pmax of 5639, 6000,
7000, 8000, 9000, and 10,000 psi.

 E-13

 Minimum effective stress (𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 ) was compared for the six cases with different Pmax, as
shown in Figure E-15. The results suggest that with increasing Pmax, 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 surrounding the cavern
walls and roof and at the bottom layer of the caprock showed a significant reduction by the end of
hydrogen injection. The Dunham Salt layers initially exhibited higher 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 and lower Biot’s
coefficient than the Poe and Saude layers before hydrogen injection. Despite greater pore pressure
increase occurring at the Dunham Salt layers surrounding the cavern zone than the caprock layers
after hydrogen injection, lower 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 was observed at the bottom layer of the caprocks than the
Dunham Salt layers, indicating the risk of tensile fracture opening is higher at the caprocks than
the salt layers.

 Figure E-15. 3D cross-sectional view (Slab of I range from 1 to 20, J from 1 to 20) of 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚
 distribution by the end of hydrogen injection on Day 731 for the five cases with Pmax of
    a) 5639 psi, b) 6000 psi, c) 7000 psi, d) 8000 psi, e) 9000 psi, and f) 10,000 psi. The
 selected block (20, 20, 4) was monitored for minimum effective stress development.

 Block (20, 20, 4), which was located at the center of the caprock bottom layer, was monitored
for the development of 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 during the simulation period for the six cases, as shown in
Figure E-16. The results suggest that increasing Pmax resulted in a decrease in 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 at the block
(20, 20, 4). As the Pmax increased to 10,000 psi, the 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 at the block (20, 20, 4) dropped to
–838 psi. The absolute value of it was less than the tensile strength of the Poe layers (1011 psi),
implying that no tensile fractures opened at this block. The fracture permeability distribution by
the end of hydrogen injection was the same all six cases, further confirming that no tensile fracture
opening occurred when Pmax increased up to 10,000 psi.

 E-14

 Figure E-16. Development of 𝜎𝜎′𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 at the block (20, 20, 4) for the cases with Pmax of 5639,
    6000, 7000, 8000, 9000, and 10,000 psi.

 The geomechanical properties including cohesion and friction angle of the Dunham Salt used
in the models were obtained based on the triaxial test for rock salt conducted by Liang et al. (2007),
which was widely cited. However, the cohesion and friction values of the Dunham Salt are
unknown. Another uncertainty is that the Dunham Salt layer contains bedding layers with various
cohesions and friction angles. If the bedding layers present lower cohesion and friction angle, the
overall shear strength of the rock is lower, and a higher possibility of shear failure is expected to
occur. From the laboratory aspect, more triaxial tests are recommended to be carried out for both
rock salt and bedding layer components of the Dunham Salt rocks to provide the ranges of cohesion
and friction angle for the formation.

 The Biot’s coefficients of the Poe, Dunham, and Saude members were assumed based on the
maximum values for rock salt and mudstone obtained from the available literature. Previous
studies suggest that the Biot’s coefficient for the rock salt is smaller than other sedimentary rocks,
with the range of 0 to 0.5 (Cosenza et al., 1999; Alkan et al., 2007; Zhang et al., 2020; Grgic et al.,
2022). The Poe and Saude layers are mainly composed of mudstone/siltstone with embedded
anhydrite. Different studies provided Biot’s coefficient values for mudstone, siltstone, or shale,
with a range of 0.2 to 0.9 (Suarez-Rivera and Fjaer, 2013; Zhou et al., 2017; Li et al., 2020a). The
rocks with a higher Biot’s coefficient present a greater reduction in effective stresses when pore
pressure is increasing due to hydrogen injection.

Conclusions and Recommendations

 A geomechanical model was developed to assess the stability of salt rocks and caprocks,
focusing on tensile and shear failure mechanisms, in accordance with the recommendations from
the study Smith et al. (2023). The results indicate that increasing injection pressure elevates the

 E-15

risk of both tensile and shear failures. Shear failure is likely when the injection pressure reaches
or exceeds 9000 psi, while no tensile failure was detected at pressures up to 10,000 psi. Under the
maximum injection pressure recommended in the Smith et al. (2023) study, no rock failures would
happen to underground hydrogen storage in the Dunham Salt Cavern.

 Since the geomechanical modeling for this project was preliminary and some properties used
in the current models exhibited uncertainties, detailed sensitivity studies are recommended to
evaluate the effect of changing cohesion and friction angle on the shear stability of the cavern
surrounding rocks. Additional laboratory measurements are recommended to accurately determine
the Biot’s coefficients for the Poe, Dunham, and Saude Members. These measurements will enable
the refinement of the geomechanical model by incorporating the measured Biot’s coefficients for
the three zones.

 The current models employed CMG to assess the short-term stability of salt caverns,
focusing on tensile and shear stresses. The Smith et al. (2023) study utilized FLAC3D software to
analyze long-term creep deformation and volumetric shrinkage. Each modeling approach
addressed a specific aspect of salt cavern stability. Future research could integrate both CMG and
FLAC3D within a comprehensive model to evaluate both short- and long-term stability of salt
caverns simultaneously.

 E-18

 APPENDIX F

EFFECTS OF MICROBIAL INTERACTIONS ON
 UNDERGROUND H2 STORAGE

  EFFECTS OF MICROBIAL INTERACTIONS ON UNDERGROUND H2 STORAGE

 The role of microbial activity in the viability of underground hydrogen storage (UHS) is
increasingly acknowledged, especially as it could lead to hydrogen loss. To improve the
effectiveness of UHS, it is essential to understand the mechanisms of microbial interactions and
their effects on hydrogen storage systems. This involves studying how microbes interact with
hydrogen, identifying the microbial communities capable of thriving in UHS environments and
analyzing the environmental conditions that promote their growth underground. A review of
existing literature on the topic was conducted as part of the project to understand the interaction of
microbes with hydrogen and potential microbial hydrogen consumption.

Microbial Reactions During UHS

 Geochemical reactions during UHS can be categorized into abiotic and biotic reactions.
Abiotic reactions involve interactions between hydrogen and various elements, such as minerals,
formation brine, and CO2, one of the commonly studied cushion gases in UHS, which have a
certain impact on storage integrity and stability (Appendix D to the main report provides more
details). Biotic reactions, or microbial activities, are recognized as one of the main causes of
hydrogen loss in underground reservoirs. Several hydrogen storage test sites have reported
microbial hydrogen consumption, including projects in Lobodice, Czech Republic; Lehen, Austria;
and Ketzin, Germany. The Lobodice project reported the most significant hydrogen losses by
microbial hydrogen consumption from a hydrogen-rich town gas storage site, with levels
decreasing from 54% to 37%. The losses at other sites were more moderate, ranging from 2% to
4% of the injected hydrogen (Liebscher et al., 2016; Thaysen et al., 2023). These reported
hydrogen losses by microbial activities demonstrate the importance of understanding the
mechanisms and impact of reactions between hydrogen and microbes Thaysen et al. (2021) ranked
biotic hydrogen-consuming processes based on their free energy yield and hydrogen threshold
measurements. Among these reactions, three are considered highly relevant for hydrogen storage
with the highest H2 threshold and lowest free energy yield during the microbial process:
hydrogenotrophic methanogenesis, homo-acetogenesis, and hydrogenotrophic sulfate reduction.

 Methanogenesis is a category of anaerobic processes involving methanogens that generate
methane from substrates like CO2. A specific example is hydrogenotrophic methanogenesis, which
consumes hydrogen to produce methane and water from CO2. This pathway offers relatively low-
yield energy production and, in some cases, serves as a source of cellular carbon.
Hydrogenotrophic methanogenesis follows the overall reaction shown in Equation F-1 (Zeikus,
1977; Getabalew et al., 2020; Thaysen et al., 2023):

                                    CO2 + 4H2 → CH4 + 2H2 O                                 [Eq. F-1]

 Acetogenesis is a biological process where acetate is produced from carbon dioxide and an
electron donor to provide energy and potentially cellular carbon for homoacetogens.
Homoacetogenesis, which can be a major microbial activity during UHS, is a specific type of
acetogenesis where certain bacteria produce acetate (acetic acid) as their primary metabolic end
product. They achieve this by reducing CO2 using H2 as an electron donor. Some well-known

 F-1

homoacetogenic bacteria include Clostridium aceticum, Acetobacterium woodii, and Moorella
thermoacetica. The biochemical process of homoacetogenesis involves reducing carbon dioxide to
acetate through the Wood–Ljungdahl pathway (Saady, 2013; Ye et al., 2014; Karekar et al., 2022;
Thaysen et al., 2023). The overall chemical reaction is provided in Equation F-2.

                                    4H2 + 2CO2 → CH3 COOH + 2H2                             [Eq. F-2]

 This pathway has two main phases (Hidalgo-Ulloa et al., 2022): 1) CO2 reduction: Carbon
dioxide is reduced to carbon monoxide (CO) and a methyl group and 2) acetyl-CoA formation:
The carbon monoxide and methyl group combine with coenzyme A to form acetyl-CoA, which is
then converted to acetate.

 Hydrogenotrophic sulfate reduction is a biochemical process that occurs in anaerobic
environments where sulfate acts as the terminal electron acceptor. This process is carried out by
several genera of sulfate-reducing bacteria (SRB), including Desulfovibrio, Desulfobacter, and
Desulfotomaculum. These bacteria are commonly found in various anaerobic settings such as
marine sediments, where they play an important role in the sulfur cycle (Cord-Ruwisch et al.,
1987). In hydrogenotrophic sulfate reduction, hydrogen molecules donate electrons that are used
to reduce sulfate to sulfide. Sulfur reduction produces energy for the organisms and cysteine amino
acid. Strategically inserted sulfur atoms also serve to thermally stabilize proteins of thermophilic
bacteria. The overall chemical reaction is summarized by Equation F-3 (Hidalgo-Ulloa et al., 2022;
Thaysen et al., 2023):

                                    4H2 + SO2−   +    −
                                            4 + H → HS + 2H2 O                              [Eq. F-3]

 This reaction involves several key intermediate steps (Hidalgo-Ulloa et al., 2022):

 • Activation of sulfate: Sulfate (SO42-) is initially activated by ATP sulfurylase to form
 adenosine 5'-phosphosulfate (APS).

 • Reduction to sulfite: APS is then reduced to sulfite (SO32-).

 • Reduction to sulfide: Finally, sulfite is further reduced to sulfide (H2S) through a series
 of enzymatic reactions, with hydrogen serving as the electron donor.

 Besides the three highly relevant hydrogen-consuming processes, there are also two
metabolic pathways of potential relevance for hydrogen storage: iron (III) reduction and sulfur
reduction. Iron reduction depends on the availability of iron oxides and iron-bearing minerals,
which are prevalent in most sediments but less common in carbon-rich oil fields. The overall
chemical reaction is provided in Equation F-4.

                            𝐻𝐻2 + 2𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹 + 4𝐻𝐻 + → 2𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹 2+ + 4𝐻𝐻2 𝑂𝑂                  [Eq. F-4]

 Sulfur reduction likely utilizes the same substrates as hydrogenotrophic sulfate reduction,
and the microorganisms involved in this process are collectively referred to as sulfur species-

 F-2

reducing microorganisms (SSRM). They perform sulfur species reduction (SSR). Equation F-5 is
the overall chemical reaction for sulfur reduction:

                                       𝐻𝐻2 + 𝑆𝑆 → 𝐻𝐻𝑆𝑆 − + 𝐻𝐻 +                            [Eq. F-5]

 Other reactions of hydrogen with electron acceptors, including oxygen (O2), nitrate (NO3-),
halogenated compounds, fumarate, and manganese oxides (MnO2/MnO42-), are considered less
relevant for hydrogen storage because of their restricted presence, arising from their high reactivity
and short lifetimes in the highly reducing environment of hydrogen storage. In terms of free energy,
the order of increasing competitiveness for microbial hydrogen utilization pathways is as follows:
acetogenesis < sulfur reduction < methanogenesis < sulfate reduction < iron reduction < aerobic
hydrogen oxidation < denitrification. (Thaysen et al., 2021; Dopffel et al., 2024).

Effects of Microorganisms on UHS

 H2 Consumption and Gas Mixture Change

 The primary concern with microorganisms in UHS relates to the loss of H2, which is
metabolized by microorganisms underground. This loss alters the composition of the stored gas.
As previously mentioned, hydrogenotrophic methanogens utilize H2 to reduce CO2 to methane and
water, thereby increasing CH4 levels. The introduction of methane can alter the physical properties
of the stored gas, such as density and pressure characteristics, potentially complicating the
management of UHS. Also, the presence of anhydrite and other inorganic sulfate minerals allows
SRB and H2 to initiate microbial processes such as sulfate and sulfur reduction, producing toxic
and corrosive H2S gas. Even a slight increase in H2S levels can adversely affect the quality of
hydrogen, including its material integrity and safety, particularly for the health of personnel at
hydrogen storage facilities (Thaysen et al., 2023). While homoacetogenesis does not directly
produce gas, it results in the formation of acetic acid from H2. Acetic acid, being a weak organic
acid, can lower the pH of its environment. While SSRMs prefer a pH above 7, other hydrogen-
utilizing organisms, such as methanogens and homoacetogens, prefer more acidic environments
with a pH less than 7. Acidification can inhibit their growth, subsequently affecting microbial
reactions (Van Kessel and Russell, 1996; Dopffel et al., 2021). Additionally, if conditions are
optimal, acetic acid can be utilized by methanogens in two ways. One method is acetoclastic
methanogenesis, which converts acetic acid directly into methane and carbon dioxide. The other
is hydrogenotrophic methanogenesis, where acetic acid is first converted to hydrogen and carbon
dioxide by other bacteria, and then methanogens reduce the CO2 to CH4 and water (Zhu et al.,
2020).

 Side Effects on Reservoirs and Facilities

 Besides directly affecting the stored gas, microbial reactions can also impact reservoirs and
facilities in the UHS site. These side effects on reservoirs and facilities are usually caused by by-
products or metabolites produced by microbial reactions and by biofilms formed by
microorganisms in the subsurface. Not only can these reactions and their products individually
affect the properties of reservoirs and equipment, but they can also combine to exacerbate certain
issues, leading to more severe problems.

 F-3

 Microbial activities can significantly enhance the dissolution of minerals. Various microbes
produce metabolites like organic acids, sulfides, and chelating agents that promote mineral
dissolution (Uroz et al., 2009; Gadd, 2010; Banfield and Nealson, 2018). For instance, acetic acid
produced during homoacetogenesis can lower the pH of the environment, facilitating mineral
dissolution (Van Kessel and Russell, 1996; Dopffel et al., 2021). SRB generate H2S, which can
react with metal ions to form soluble metal sulfides (Banfield and Nealson, 2018). Additionally,
microbial activity can trigger redox reactions that change the oxidation state of minerals, leading
to their dissolution. For example, iron-reducing bacteria (IRB) convert ferric iron (Fe3+) to the
more soluble ferrous iron (Fe2+) (Bazylinski and Frankel, 2004). Certain minerals in geologic
reservoirs are especially prone to microbial-induced dissolution. Carbonates are highly reactive in
acidic conditions; silicates are susceptible to enhanced weathering by acid-producing bacteria,
which releases essential cations into the solution (Hiebert and Bennett, 1992; Bennett et al., 2001);
and sulfides can be oxidized by sulfur-oxidizing bacteria, producing sulfuric acid that further
promotes mineral dissolution (Southam and Saunders, 2005).

 Following the dissolution of minerals by microbial activities is microbial-induced mineral
precipitation (MIMP). As microbes dissolve minerals, they release essential nutrients like
phosphorus, potassium, and magnesium into the environment. These nutrients can then be
absorbed by microbes and other organisms and used in metabolic processes that lead to mineral
precipitation (Schulz and Schulz, 2005; Benzerara et al., 2006; Banfield and Nealson, 2018). Both
dissolution and precipitation are integral to the cycling of elements such as carbon, sulfur, and iron,
a process known as biogeochemical cycling (Maier, 2015). Using iron cycling as an example, in
iron-rich environments, IRB dissolve iron minerals by reducing Fe (III) to Fe (II). This process
releases Fe (II) into the environment, which can then be oxidized by iron-oxidizing bacteria (IOB)
to precipitate new iron minerals such as ferrihydrite (Weber et al., 2006). These processes of
dissolution and precipitation have significant effects on porosity—mineral dissolution tends to
increase porosity, while precipitation tends to reduce both the porosity and permeability of the
reservoir (Chagneau et al., 2015; Dopffel et al., 2021).

 Permeability can be influenced not only by MIMP but also by biofilms. Biofilms are
complex communities of microorganisms that adhere to various surfaces (like rock or pipelines)
and are embedded in a self-produced extracellular matrix. This matrix protects the microorganisms
from environmental stressors, enhancing their survival and resilience by reducing physical and
chemical stresses (Watnick and Kolter, 2000; Tolker-Nielsen, 2015). The extracellular polymeric
substances (EPS) in biofilms, which include polysaccharides, proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids,
are essential for the stability of biofilms (Tourney and Ngwenya, 2014; Dong et al., 2020). The
formation of biofilms on rock surfaces and within pore spaces leads to a reduction in permeability.
Additionally, biofilms, combined with MIMP, can severely clog the pore spaces in rocks or
pipelines (Pintelon et al., 2012; Dopffel et al., 2021). Microbiologically influenced corrosion (MIC)
is closely associated with the presence of microbial biofilms on steel surfaces. Corrosion typically
occurs beneath a biofilm, where redox and chemical conditions are significantly altered due to
restricted diffusion (Dopffel et al., 2021). Additionally, by-products produced by microbial
reactions, such as H2S from sulfate reduction, ferrous iron from iron reduction, and methane from
methanogenesis, can accelerate the corrosion process. Beyond these known effects, the impact of
biofilms on rock wettability has not been thoroughly investigated. Rock wettability is crucial in
underground gas storage as it affects injectivity, recovery, storage capacity, and containment

 F-4

security. Most studies have focused on sandstone surfaces (Ali et al., 2023; Boon et al., 2024). Ali
et al. (2023) observed different effects of biofilms on water-wet and oil-wet surfaces; brine contact
angles on water-wet surfaces increased after incubation, while those on oil-wet surfaces decreased.
Alkan et al. (2019) conducted oscillating pendant drop measurements that demonstrated how
biofilms at the oil–water–rock interface contribute to cell-induced wettability alterations, changing
the mechanical equilibrium of forces at the interface and increasing the surface tension between
oil and rock surfaces.

Environmental Conditions Influencing Microbial Activity

 After understanding the side effects of microbial activities on UHS, it is important to
carefully select suitable sites for UHS to minimize these effects. Hence, it is necessary to
investigate which environmental conditions can affect the growth of microorganisms and how they
impact it. Several environmental conditions are discussed in this section, including temperature,
salinity, pH, nutrients, and pressure. The optimal conditions for microbial activity are also
discussed.

 Temperature

 Temperature plays a pivotal role in the activity and survival of microorganisms, with each
type adapted to thrive within specific temperature ranges. Mesophiles, commonly found in many
environments, optimally grow between 20° to 45°C. In contrast, psychrophiles are adapted to cold
environments, thriving between −20° to 10°C, thermophiles prefer temperatures ranging from 45°
to 80°C, and hyperthermophiles can survive in extreme temperatures from 80° to 122°C (Goldstein,
2007). Consequently, subsurface hydrogen storage sites maintaining temperatures above 122°C
are typically considered low risk for microbial activity due to these extreme conditions (Thaysen
et al., 2021; Ahn et al., 2022). High temperatures affect various cellular components and processes:

 • Protein denaturation: Proteins perform a myriad of cellular functions such as catalyzing
 chemical reactions and providing structural support. Many of these functions require that
 proteins maintain specific shapes and are lost under high-temperature conditions which
 cause proteins to lose those shapes or “denature” (Tanford, 1968; Lodish, 2008).

 • Membrane fluidity: Cell membranes become more fluid with rising temperatures.
 Excessive fluidity can compromise membrane integrity, leading to potential cell lysis and
 the breakdown of essential barriers needed for cell compartmentalization and function
 (Sinensky, 1974; Cooper and Adams, 2022).

 • DNA stability: High temperatures can cause the DNA helix to unwind or denature, an
 event that can interfere with processes such as replication and transcription, ultimately
 being lethal to the cell (Blake and Delcourt, 1998; Holden, 2009).

 • Energy expenditure and speed of reproduction: Acclimation to the stress of high
 temperatures requires thermophiles to expend extra energy in producing cellular materials
 that can tolerate such temperatures. This effort also requires extra time, which slows
 reproduction rates. While this tolerance provides competitive advantage at higher

 F-5

 temperatures, as temperatures decrease to the point where such accommodation is
 unnecessary, organisms without this overhead grow faster and outcompete thermophiles.

 Additionally, as highlighted by Thaysen et al. (2021), under constant pressure, the solubility
of gases in the liquid phase decreases with an increase in temperature. This reduction in solubility
makes gases less available to microbes, further limiting microbial activity in subsurface
environments with high temperatures.

 Salinity

 Salinity significantly influences microbial survival and activity, especially in contexts like
UHS where salt concentrations can vary widely (0–0.5 M NaCl) (Shi et al., 2020). Halophiles,
microorganisms that thrive in high-salinity environments, exhibit a range of salt tolerances.
Specifically, extreme halophiles require salinity levels of 2.5–5.2 M NaCl to grow. Moderate
halophiles prefer a range of 0.5–2.5 M NaCl, while slight halophiles flourish at 0.2–0.5 M NaCl.
Nonhalophilic microorganisms can only tolerate salinity up to 0.2 M NaCl (Maheshwari and Saraf,
2015). For example, in western North Dakota where bedded-salt caverns could be 7000 feet deep,
exhibiting temperatures of 160° to 200°F, saturated brine concentrations of more than 5.5 M are
possible.

 The primary challenge for microorganisms in high-salinity environments is osmotic stress
(Oren, 2016). This stress can dehydrate cells as water moves out to balance the salt concentration
between the cell interior and exterior, potentially leading to cell death. Microorganisms adapt
through osmoregulation, where they accumulate compatible solutes such as proline and glycine
betaine to retain water within their cells. Although this adaptation allows survival, it often comes
at the cost of inhibited growth, slower metabolic rates, and reduced metabolic efficiency (Kempf
and Bremer, 1998; Oren, 2001).

 The impact of salinity on microorganisms can be categorized into three aspects: water
activity, ionic strength, and membrane stability. Water activity diminishes with increased salinity,
reducing the availability of free water necessary for microbial processes. Microorganisms need a
minimum amount of water activity to grow, and high-salinity environments often fall below this
threshold for many species (Grant, 2004; Karan et al., 2012). High salinity also boosts the ionic
strength of the environment (Allen et al., 2011), potentially affecting enzyme activity by altering
protein structure and function. Some enzymes lose activity at high ionic strengths, whereas
enzymes in halophiles are adapted to function well under these conditions (Isaksson et al., 1982;
Zhao, 2005). Moreover, salinity impacts the fluidity and stability of microbial cell membranes.
High salt concentrations can denature membrane proteins and disrupt transport processes,
emphasizing the importance of efficient ion transport mechanisms for osmoregulation (Beney and
Gervais, 2001; Bergmann et al., 2013).

 Brine complexity is another factor that impacts water activity and ionic strength. Natural
brines contain a variety of dissolved ions, and their interactions can be highly complex. This
complexity may introduce physicochemical stressors that affect the habitability of brines, such as
low water activity, high ionic strength, and chaotropy—the ability to disrupt the network of

 F-6

hydrogen bonds between water molecules—or a combination of these factors (Thaysen et al.,
2021).

 pH

 Microorganisms have different pH preferences and are categorized into three main groups.
Acidophiles thrive in acidic environments with a pH below 5. Neutrophiles prefer neutral
environments, typically ranging from pH 6 to 8. Alkaliphiles grow in alkaline environments with
a pH above 9 (Oren, 2010). Deviating from these optimal pH ranges can slow metabolic processes,
inhibit growth, and potentially lead to cell death.

 The major concern is pH stress, which primarily affects microorganisms in two ways. In
low-pH environments, acidic stress can cause a proton influx, disrupting the intracellular pH
balance. This disturbance can lead to enzyme denaturation and impair cellular functions (Guan
and Liu, 2020). While in high-pH environments, alkaline stress can lead to the deprotonation of
cellular components. This change affects both membrane stability and enzyme activity, impacting
the overall health and function of the microorganisms (Krulwich et al., 2011).

 Nutrients

 Various nutrients are necessary for microorganisms’ growth and metabolic functions. For
commonly encountered microbes (e.g., aerobic heterotrophic bacteria) – as opposed to the
anaerobic extremophiles discussed so far – carbon is the primary energy source and building block
for cell structures. Nitrogen is essential for proteins, nucleic acids, and other cellular components.
Phosphorus is a critical nutrient for ATP, nucleic acids, and membrane lipids. Trace elements, such
as iron, magnesium, and sulfur, are necessary for enzyme function and cellular processes. There
are three typical nutrient sources in the UHS environments – organic matter, minerals, and injected
fluids (Pedersen and Karlsson, 1995; Choong et al., 2016; Brown et al., 2022).

 Microbial decomposition of organic matter releases carbon compounds that fuel microbial
growth, playing a significant role in the carbon cycle within UHS environments (Spohn, 2016).
Microorganisms can utilize these compounds through aerobic respiration in the presence of oxygen
and anaerobic respiration and fermentation under oxygen-limited or anaerobic conditions (Kelly
et al., 2001). A sufficient supply of carbon supports rapid microbial growth and high metabolic
activity. The growth of hydrogenotrophic microorganisms leads to increased use of hydrogen as
electron donors in their metabolic processes, which reduces the availability of hydrogen in UHS.
Additionally, the by-products of decomposition, such as organic acids, can stimulate the growth
of hydrogen-consuming microorganisms (Uroz et al., 2009; Gadd, 2010; Banfield and Nealson,
2018).

 The nutrients can come from the solution or directly from the minerals. Different types of
geologic formations can contribute various trace elements and inorganic nutrients. Sedimentary
rocks are often rich in minerals such as calcium, magnesium, and iron. Igneous rocks contain trace
elements like potassium, phosphorus, and sulfur (Blatt and Jones, 1975). Mineral weathering of
such rocks can release those key elements, including carbon, iron, phosphorus, and sulfur (Thaysen
et al., 2021). Additionally, in the salt cavern UHS sites, leaching is an important process to form

 F-7

caverns by injecting water in a controlled manner to dissolve salt. As soluble minerals are dissolved
into the injected water, more nutrients can be provided to microorganisms to growth (Tackie-Otoo
and Haq, 2024). In bedded salt formations, nonsalt units can be present within salt strata,
containing minerals that provide nutrients to microbes.

 In addition to the water injected for leaching, other fluids introduced before hydrogen
storage—such as hydraulic fracturing fluids and drilling fluids—may contain various nutrients.
For example, surfactants and polymers used to enhance oil recovery in depleted oil reservoirs
before repurposing for hydrogen storage can act as carbon sources for microorganisms (Sun et al.,
2013; Ortiz-Martínez et al., 2020; Khan, 2024). Furthermore, in UHS environments,
hydrogenotrophic microorganisms require H2 as an energy source, but they can only access H2 in
its aqueous phase. Therefore, the solubility of gaseous H2 is directly relevant to all H2-consuming
reactions. Thaysen et al. (2021) noted that under H2 storage conditions, there is no limitation by
H2 solubility on microbial growth.

 Pressure

 High pressure serves to affect microbial cells by impacting the integrity of cell membranes
and walls, altering membrane fluidity, and influencing DNA and protein synthesis. To survive in
high-pressure environments, microorganisms have developed various mechanisms and strategies.
These include evolving robust biocatalysts, maintaining membrane fluidity, and forming
extracellular polymeric substances (EPS) or spores (Abe et al., 1999; Jebbar et al., 2015; Yin et
al., 2019; Salwan and Sharma, 2020). Additionally, certain biochemical pathways may be more
favorable under high pressure, leading to changes in the types of metabolic products formed.
Moreover, high pressure enhances the solubility of gases in liquids, which increases the availability
of hydrogen for microbes that can metabolize it, potentially affecting their growth and metabolic
functions (Abe, 2007; Meersman et al., 2013).

 Optimal Environmental Conditions for Microbial Growth

 Thaysen et al. (2021) identified the primary factors that influence the growth of three major
types of microorganisms: methanogens, homoacetogens, and SSRM. They concluded that, in
substrate- and nutrient-rich environments, temperature and salinity are the most crucial factors
limiting microbial growth, whereas the effects of pressure and pH were secondary, partly due to
the relatively high osmotic pressure within the cytoplasm. At pressures ranging from 30 to 50 mPa,
the growth of most mesophiles is inhibited (Abe, 2007; Thaysen et al., 2021).

 The study found that pH does not similarly constrain microbial growth since the pH ranges
conducive to growth typically span two to three units. The nutrient requirements vary significantly
among different microbes. For instance, SSRM require a higher level of iron for growth compared
to other microorganisms, and sulfate reduction by SSR can occur at sulfate concentrations as low
as 5 to 77 µM. Methanogens have a higher sulfur requirement, with optimal levels ranging from
0.03 to 0.79 mM, and require 29.6 mM of magnesium for optimum growth, ceasing growth at
16.5 mM. However, there is a scarcity of literature and data on when nutrients become limiting
(Herbert et al., 1985; Vroblesky et al., 1996; Chen et al., 2008; Havig et al., 2017; Thaysen et al.,
2021).

 F-8

 Thaysen et al. (2021) suggested that to minimize microbial impacts on hydrogen storage,
sites with temperatures above 55°C and salinities over 1.7 M are preferred. Additionally, since the
highest recorded temperature for microbial growth is 122°C, sites with temperatures exceeding
this threshold could be considered sterile. Beside these proposed factors, Dopffel et al. (2024) also
pointed out that reservoir permeability in the millidarcy range lacks adequate space for microbes,
consequently preventing microbial growth.

Microbial Activity Across Various UHS Environments

 There are three major types of UHS environments: salt caverns, depleted oil/gas reservoirs,
and saline formations. Previous sections have discussed general biotic reactions, side effects of
microbial activities, and environmental conditions that affect microbial growth. However, each of
these UHS environments possesses unique characteristics that require further discussion.

 Salt Caverns

 Salt caverns present environmental conditions distinctly different from depleted oil/gas
reservoirs and saline formations, primarily due to their rock properties and high salinity (Tackie-
Otoo and Haq, 2004). This extreme environment restricts biological diversity to primarily
halophiles—organisms that thrive in high salt concentrations. These organisms span various
domains—Archaea, Bacteria, and Eucarya—and exhibit a wide range of metabolic diversity
(Schwab et al., 2022). The origins of these microbes can vary; they may come from leaching,
regular operations within the salt cavern, naturally occurring in the salt rock, or migrating through
the salt (Dopffel et al., 2024). As salinity increases, the diversity and potential activity of these
microbes typically decrease (Tackie-Otoo and Haq, 2004).

 For salt caverns, certain microbial activities become more significant due to the
environment's limitations on available nutrients. Dopffel et al. (2024) highlighted that the most
prominent microbial activities in salt caverns are sulfate reduction, methanogenesis, and
acetogenesis. This is due to the low availability of nitrate, reduced forms of iron, and manganese.
Of these, sulfate reduction poses a particular concern because it can lead to the production of
hydrogen sulfide (H2S), which severely impacts the health and safety of those working within or
around the UHS facility. H2S is not only toxic but can also compromise the integrity of the cavern
and associated infrastructure through corrosion. Additionally, microbial activities in salt caverns
pose economic concerns, notably the loss of stored hydrogen and the deterioration of gas quality
through sulfate reduction. This reduction can lead to increased costs associated with the
purification of hydrogen upon withdrawal and the need for ongoing maintenance to address
microbial-induced corrosion of tubing and other infrastructure.

 These issues underscore the importance of managing microbial activity and ensuring robust
safety and maintenance protocols in the operation of salt cavern UHS facilities. Effective
monitoring and control strategies are essential to mitigate these risks and safeguard the integrity
and functionality of the storage system.

 F-9

 Depleted Oil/Gas Reservoirs

 Depleted oil and gas reservoirs offer significant potential for underground gas storage due
to the availability of natural space and well-documented geological and mineralogical information
(Aftab, 2022). Various microbial communities have been identified in these hydrocarbon
reservoirs, including sulfate-reducing bacteria, methanogenic archaea, zymophytes, iron-reducing
bacteria, nitrate-reducing bacteria, and acetogenic bacteria (Xiong, 2023). Unlike other UHS
environments, the microbial communities in these reservoirs can be influenced by different
Improved Oil Recovery/Enhanced Oil Recovery (IOR/EOR) techniques. Lin et al. (2014) studied
microbial community structures in oil reservoirs subjected to water flooding, finding that
prolonged water flooding enriched the indigenous microbial communities. Gulliver et al. (2016)
noted that exposure to CO2 reduced microbial diversity and population sizes, with the most
significant effects at higher CO2 concentrations. For instance, the bacteria Pseudomonas was
dominant at low CO2 concentrations but significantly decreased at higher levels. These findings
highlight the need to characterize microbial communities in hydrocarbon reservoirs before
converting them for hydrogen storage, especially those utilizing IOR/EOR techniques to enhance
hydrocarbon production.

 Assessing the long-term effects of microbial activities is crucial for large-scale UHS in
depleted oil and gas reservoirs. Primary concerns include hydrogen consumption and containment
by microbial processes, and hydrogen loss due to potential gas leakage. Leakage may result from
structural weaknesses in the caprock and bottom seal, caused by fractures or changes in
permeability. Microbial activity can worsen these issues, as microbes produce metabolites like
organic acids, sulfides, and chelating agents that can increase porosity and permeability by causing
dissolution within the reservoir structure, as discussed in the Microbial Reactions during UHS
section. The presence of sensitive minerals like carbonates and sulfates in the caprock can
exacerbate this process (Zeng, 2023). Hemme et al. (2018), using a 1D reactive mass transport
model, highlighted hydrogen loss mechanisms through methanogenesis and sulfate reduction.
Their findings indicated that interfaces between the reservoir rock and caprock, and between the
reservoir rock and the underlying rock, are critical for hydrogen loss. These zones act as conduits
for the diffusion of gases such as CO2 and sulfate ions, facilitating the formation of H2S and CH4,
which accelerate hydrogen depletion. Additionally, the dissolution of minerals like calcite in the
caprock and anhydrite in the bottom seal contributes to hydrogen loss. In depleted gas fields, this
might increase reservoir porosity, leading to further leakage, especially in fractured reservoirs.
However, microbial-induced mineral precipitation can occur after dissolution. If precipitation
exceeds dissolution, it could preserve the integrity of the caprock, potentially mitigating some risks
associated with microbial activity (Muhammed, 2023). Nonetheless, porosity and permeability
near the wellbore region may still be compromised, impacting hydrogen injectivity into the storage
facility (Muhammed, 2022; Muhammed, 2023; Zeng, 2023).

 Saline Formations

 Saline formations, while sharing geological structures similar to depleted oil and gas
reservoirs, contain brine or water that must be displaced by hydrogen injection, a process which
significantly differs due to the original fluid stored (Sáinz-García, 2017). This difference in stored
fluids can lead to varied microbial communities within the saline formations compared to those in

 F-10

oil and gas reservoirs. Numerous studies have been conducted worldwide to monitor and identify
microbial compositions in saline formations, highlighting the importance of understanding these
biological aspects for effective management (Engel, 2011; Gray, 2013; Ko, 2016; Gagnon, 2023;
Vargas-García, 2023).

 In addition to concerns about caprock and bottom seal integrity to prevent leakage, there is
a biological concern related to the vicinity of the wellbore caused by biofilms (Kirkland, 2015;
Lemieux, 2019). Biofilms can influence the wellbore's integrity and the overall efficacy of
hydrogen storage. It is possible that during the hydrogen withdrawal period, a high bottom hole
flowing pressure could force the hydrogen to move back toward the wellbore, potentially
extending the injection time (Jones, 2016).

Conclusions

 The literature review indicates that microbial activity can affect underground hydrogen
storage systems by causing hydrogen loss, gas quality issues, and damage to equipment. Different
environments support different microbes. Salt caverns mainly have salt-loving microbes, which
can produce hydrogen sulfide, a safety hazard. Oil and gas reservoirs have more active microbes
that can consume hydrogen and cause mineral changes, affecting storage. Saline formations have
native microbes that may form biofilms near wells, impacting operations. Temperature and salinity
are key factors limiting microbial activity. Higher temperatures above 55°C and salinity above
1.7 M NaCl reduce microbial effects. Other factors like pH, pressure, and nutrients also influence
microbes. Considering these factors during site selection and operation helps reduce risks.

 Overall, integrating microbiological considerations into UHS site assessment, reservoir
engineering, and monitoring programs is imperative. Continued research into microbial behavior
under UHS conditions, the development of mitigation strategies such as biocide application or
environmental control, and real-time monitoring tools will be vital to ensuring the success of
underground hydrogen storage initiatives at both pilot and commercial scales.
