 APPENDIX G

NORTH DAKOTA HYDROGEN PRODUCTION
 POTENTIAL

 NORTH DAKOTA HYDROGEN PRODUCTION POTENTIAL

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

 North Dakota is positioned to become a leader in hydrogen production, leveraging its
abundant natural resources, including natural gas and wind energy. North Dakota’s hydrogen
production strategy could evolve through three key phases—near-term, mid-term, and long-term—
each making use of the state’s natural resources and advancing technologies. Below is a summary
of the technologies that could be applied in each phase.

1. Near-Term:
 • Blue hydrogen (from natural gas with carbon capture): This makes sense as a short-term
 goal because North Dakota has abundant natural gas and carbon capture and storage (CCS)
 technology is relatively mature and can be rapidly deployed to meet near-term hydrogen
 production targets.

 • Gray hydrogen (from natural gas without carbon capture): Although gray hydrogen does
 not contribute to decarbonization, it can serve as an initial step because of existing
 infrastructure, especially while CCS technology scales up.

2. Mid-Term (Developmental Phase) – Green hydrogen (via electrolysis using wind and solar
 energy): This is appropriate for the mid-term as renewable energy development, particularly
 wind, is already progressing in North Dakota. The transition to green hydrogen aligns with the
 state’s potential to harness renewable energy and reflects a shift toward a more sustainable
 approach to hydrogen production as the technology becomes more commercially viable.

3. Long-Term (Theoretical and Advanced Approaches) – Black hydrogen (coal gasification
 with CCS) and solar thermochemical processes: These longer-term technologies focus on
 innovation, research, and development. Black hydrogen from coal aligns with North Dakota’s
 coal reserves, but integrating CCS is critical to reducing emissions. Solar thermochemical
 processes are still under development but offer future promise as sustainable, high-efficiency
 hydrogen production methods.

 In the near-term, the focus is on blue hydrogen produced from natural gas coupled with
established CCS technologies, ensuring a rapid response to hydrogen production targets. Gray
hydrogen serves as an initial step, utilizing existing infrastructure while CCS technology is scaled
up.

 Transitioning into the mid-term, the report emphasizes green hydrogen production through
electrolysis powered by renewable wind energy. This shift aligns with North Dakota’s renewable
energy goals and addresses the need for sustainable solutions in the hydrogen market.

 Looking to the long-term, innovative approaches that capitalize on the state’s abundant coal
reserves while significantly reducing its carbon footprint such as black hydrogen from coal

 G-1

gasification with CCS and solar thermochemical processes will be explored, showcasing North
Dakota’s commitment to a diverse and sustainable hydrogen production landscape.

 This narrative demonstrates the interconnectedness of resources, technologies, and socio-
economic impacts, ultimately outlining the significant benefits of hydrogen development. The full
report highlights job creation, economic growth, and reduction in emissions, reinforcing the
importance of stakeholder engagement and community benefits.

 As North Dakota navigates these phases, it will also face challenges, particularly regarding
the need for significant water resources for hydrogen production technologies. Addressing these
challenges through innovative approaches, such as utilizing produced water from oil and gas
operations, is essential for sustainable development. The report outlines the potential mitigation
strategies to ensure that water usage does not hinder hydrogen production.

 By adopting this comprehensive approach, North Dakota can successfully navigate its path
toward a robust hydrogen economy, benefiting both the state and society at large.

 G-2

 NORTH DAKOTA HYDROGEN PRODUCTION POTENTIAL

BACKGROUND AND CONTEXT

 State Energy Overview

 North Dakota’s energy sector includes a variety of fossil fuels and renewable resources,
making it an important player in the national energy scene. The state has significant reserves of
crude oil, natural gas, and lignite coal, along with increasing investments in renewable energy like
wind power. This overview will look at the state’s main energy resources and introduce its potential
for hydrogen production.

 North Dakota has become a major player in crude oil production, ranking the third-largest
oil producer in the United States after Texas and Alaska. The Bakken Formation, a key geologic
area in the state, has driven this growth. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration
(EIA), North Dakota’s daily oil production reached about 1.2 million barrels in 2024 (U.S. Energy
Information Administration, 2025). This increase is largely due to improvements in hydraulic
fracturing and horizontal drilling techniques. This boost in oil production has helped strengthen
the state’s economy and is important for national energy security.

 Alongside oil production, North Dakota has experienced significant growth in natural gas
output. In 2023, the state produced nearly 1.2 trillion cubic feet (Tcf) of natural gas, a notable
increase from previous years. This rise is largely due to increased drilling activities in the Bakken
region, where natural gas is extracted along with crude oil. The North Dakota Industrial
Commission (NDIC) points out that much of this natural gas is now being captured and used,
which reduces flaring rates and promotes more sustainable energy practices (North Dakota
Industrial Commission, 2023).

 North Dakota is home to the largest lignite coal deposit in the world, mainly found in the
western part of the state. Lignite, a type of soft coal, is primarily used for generating electricity.
The state’s lignite reserves are estimated at over 25 billion tons, making it a reliable and affordable
energy source for both local and regional power plants. The lignite industry has actively adopted
clean coal technologies to reduce environmental impacts. According to the North Dakota Lignite
Energy Council (LEC), these technologies not only improve the efficiency of coal-fired power
plants but also lower greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions (North Dakota Lignite Energy Council,
2022).

 Recently, North Dakota has made great progress in using renewable energy, especially wind
power. The state has some of the best wind energy resources in the country, with wind turbines
generating around 36% of its total electricity in 2023. This impressive contribution is supported
by the state’s wide-open spaces and consistent wind patterns. The National Renewable Energy
Laboratory (NREL) notes that North Dakota’s wind potential could power the entire state multiple
times over, highlighting significant opportunities for future development in this area (National
Renewable Energy Laboratory, 2023). While hydroelectric power and biomass are more limited,
they still play a role in the state’s effort to diversify its energy sources.

 G-3

 North Dakota’s wind resources present a significant opportunity for producing green
hydrogen. By harnessing the state’s abundant and consistent wind energy, particularly when it is
in excess or constrained, North Dakota can establish wind-to-hydrogen facilities. These facilities
will use wind energy to power electrolysis plants, where water is split into hydrogen and oxygen,
thereby producing green hydrogen.

 The oxygen generated as a by-product of the electrolysis process has a valuable role in the
context of hydrogen production from coal. Specifically, it can be utilized in oxy-gasification
processes, where pure oxygen is used instead of air. This integration enhances the efficiency of
coal gasification, reduces nitrogen dilution in the syngas, and improves overall hydrogen yield.

 Produced water is the water that is extracted alongside oil and gas during extraction
processes. In North Dakota, significant volumes of produced water are generated, particularly from
the Bakken Formation, with estimates reaching over 12 million barrels per day during peak
production periods (North Dakota Department of Mineral Resources, 2021). This water typically
contains a mix of salts, hydrocarbons, and various contaminants, making its management a
significant challenge.

 Disposal of produced water poses environmental and regulatory hurdles. Traditional
methods, such as deep-well injection, can lead to concerns about groundwater contamination and
seismic activity. Moreover, the costs associated with transportation and disposal can be substantial
for operators (Zhang et al., 2019).

 However, produced water presents an opportunity for hydrogen production if effectively
treated. By implementing advanced desalination and purification techniques, such as reverse
osmosis and advanced filtration, it is possible to remove the troublesome salts and impurities from
produced water, transforming it into a viable feedstock for hydrogen production through
electrolysis (Wang et al., 2021).

 Utilizing produced water as a resource for hydrogen production not only addresses disposal
challenges but also supports the development of a more sustainable hydrogen economy in North
Dakota. This dual approach could enhance water resource management while contributing to the
state’s energy transition efforts.

Hydrogen Types

 Hydrogen production can be categorized into various types, often referred to by colors, each
representing different methods and their environmental impacts. Understanding these distinctions
is crucial as we move toward a more sustainable energy future.

 Green hydrogen is produced through electrolysis powered by renewable energy sources like
wind and solar. This method emits no GHGs, making it the most environmentally friendly option
available. It has significant potential for applications in transportation, industry, and energy
storage.

 G-4

 Blue hydrogen is created from natural gas using steam methane reforming (SMR) paired
with carbon capture and storage (CCS) technology to capture most of the CO2 emissions. This
makes blue hydrogen a more sustainable choice compared to traditional fossil fuel methods, acting
as a transitional solution while moving toward greener options.

 Turquoise hydrogen is produced through methane pyrolysis, which converts natural gas into
hydrogen and solid carbon without releasing CO2. This process is considered a promising way to
create hydrogen with a lower carbon footprint.

 Gray hydrogen, like blue hydrogen, is produced from natural gas through SMR but without
any carbon capture. As a result, it generates significant CO2 emissions, making it less sustainable
and a major contributor to GHG emissions.

 Black hydrogen comes from coal through gasification, while brown hydrogen is derived
from lignite (brown coal). Both are associated with high carbon emissions and are generally
considered the least environmentally friendly options in the hydrogen spectrum.

 Pink or purple hydrogen is produced through electrolysis powered by nuclear energy. While
it does not produce direct emissions, the sustainability of this method depends on the overall life
cycle of nuclear power and its environmental considerations.

 Yellow hydrogen is generated through electrolysis that uses a combination of renewable
energy and grid electricity, which may include fossil fuels. Its sustainability can vary widely based
on the energy mix used in the process.

HYDROGEN PRODUCTION STRATEGIES

 Near-Term Hydrogen Production Strategies

 For near-term hydrogen production in North Dakota, the focus is on established technologies
and existing resources that are readily available to immediately initiate hydrogen production. This
phase focuses primarily on blue hydrogen, which is produced from natural gas integrated with
CCS, and gray hydrogen, generated from natural gas without carbon capture.

 SMR with CCS for Blue Hydrogen

 SMR is a well-established process for hydrogen production and is widely used in industrial
processes for producing hydrogen from natural gas, specifically methane. It is the most common
method for large-scale hydrogen production and is key in various industrial applications, including
ammonia synthesis and refining processes. The following is a summary of the general process.

 The primary feedstock for SMR is natural gas (mainly composed of methane, CH4). Water
(steam) is also necessary for the reforming and water–gas shift (WGS) reactions. Options for
energy input include the following:

 G-5

 Natural gas – Often, natural gas itself is used to provide the necessary heat through
 combustion.

 Electricity – Electric heating systems can also be utilized, especially in facilities looking to
 reduce carbon emissions or transition to renewable energy.

 Renewable energy – In the long term, integrating renewable energy sources like wind or
 solar could supply the necessary heat, especially if coupled with electrolysis for hydrogen
 production.

 Combined heat and power (CHP) systems – These systems can generate both electricity and
 usable heat, optimizing energy efficiency.

 SMR involves two main reactions, reforming and WGS:

 Reforming reaction – In the first step, methane reacts with steam (water vapor) at high
 temperatures (typically 700°–1000°C) in the presence of a catalyst (usually nickel-based):

                                     CH4 + H2O → CO + 3H2

 WGS reaction – In the second step, the carbon monoxide produced reacts with more steam
 to produce additional hydrogen and carbon dioxide:

                                      CO + H2O → CO2 + H2

 The main products of SMR are hydrogen and carbon dioxide. The hydrogen produced can
be used in various applications, while CO2 is typically a by-product. SMR is known for its high
efficiency in converting methane to hydrogen, often exceeding 70% hydrogen yield based on the
feedstock. As SMR generates carbon dioxide as a by-product, it is categorized as a method for
producing gray hydrogen. Efforts are ongoing to integrate CCS technologies to mitigate the
environmental impact.

 Partial Oxidation (POX) for Producing Gray Hydrogen (with minimal
 infrastructure changes)

 POX is an established method for producing hydrogen from hydrocarbons, particularly
useful in scenarios where infrastructure changes need to be minimized. This process involves the
reaction of hydrocarbons with oxygen at high temperatures (typically 900°–1500°C) under
conditions that limit the amount of oxygen available. As a result, the hydrocarbon is not fully
oxidized to carbon dioxide and water but instead yields hydrogen and carbon monoxide as primary
products.

 POX is commonly used in industries requiring hydrogen, including ammonia production,
fuel cell technology, and as a feedstock for various chemical processes. However, it is less widely
adopted compared to SMR. The primary feedstock for POX can include natural gas, but it is also

 G-6

adaptable to heavier hydrocarbons, allowing for versatility in feedstock selection. Options for
energy input include the following:

 Natural gas – Often, natural gas itself is combusted to provide the necessary heat for the
 POX reaction. This is a common practice, especially in facilities where natural gas is readily
 available and can serve both as a feedstock and a fuel source.

 Electric heating – Electric heating systems can be employed to provide the required thermal
 energy for the reaction. This approach can be particularly beneficial for facilities aiming to
 reduce carbon emissions by using renewable electricity.

 Oxygen-enriched air – Instead of using pure oxygen, oxygen-enriched air can be introduced
 into the reaction to facilitate the POX of hydrocarbons. This method can improve reaction
 efficiency while still maintaining the necessary oxygen levels.

 Renewable energy – In the long term, integrating renewable energy sources, such as wind
 or solar, could be used to provide the heat necessary for the POX process. This integration
 could enhance the sustainability of hydrogen production.

 Heat recovery systems – Systems designed to recover heat from other processes (such as
 waste heat from industrial operations) can be utilized to provide additional thermal energy
 to the POX reaction, improving overall energy efficiency.

 CHP systems – CHP systems can generate both electricity and usable heat simultaneously,
 optimizing the energy efficiency of the facility and providing the necessary energy for the
 POX process.

 The core chemical reactions in a POX system can be summarized as follows:

 Feedstock reaction – The primary reaction for POX can be expressed as:

 CnHm + 21O2 → H2 + CO + other products

 Here, CnHm represents the hydrocarbon feedstock, which can vary from natural gas to
 heavier hydrocarbons.

 Secondary reactions – Additional reactions can occur in the POX process, including the
 WGS reaction:

                                   2CO + H2O → CO2 +H2
 This reaction increases hydrogen yield by converting carbon monoxide into carbon dioxide
 and additional hydrogen.

 Formation of by-products – Depending on the feedstock and operating conditions, other by-
 products such as methane (CH4) and various hydrocarbons may also be produced.

 G-7

 Mid-Term (Developmental Phase) Hydrogen Production Strategies

 In the mid-term phase of hydrogen production in North Dakota, the emphasis shifts toward
advancing technologies that have demonstrated potential through pilot projects and are
approaching commercial viability. This phase will prioritize the development of green hydrogen,
produced through electrolysis using renewable energy sources such as wind and solar power.

 Additionally, advancements in blue hydrogen technology will continue, with enhanced
carbon capture efficiencies and the integration of more robust storage solutions. The focus will
also be on optimizing the supply chain and infrastructure necessary to support hydrogen
distribution, including the potential for local production hubs. Collaborations with industry
partners and research institutions will be crucial for scaling up these technologies, ensuring they
are both economically viable and environmentally sustainable.

 Green Hydrogen via Electrolysis

 Green hydrogen is produced through the electrolysis of water, utilizing renewable
energy sources. This process is critical for transitioning to a sustainable hydrogen economy,
as it emits no GHGs when powered by clean energy.

 The primary feedstock is water (H2O). Renewable energy sources such as wind, solar, or
hydropower are used to generate electricity for the electrolysis process. During electrolysis, water
is split into hydrogen and oxygen using an electric current. The chemical reaction can be
represented as follows:

                                     2H2O(l) → 2H2(g) + O2(g)

 This reaction occurs in an electrolyzer, which can be either an alkaline electrolyzer or a
proton exchange membrane (PEM) electrolyzer.

 In an alkaline electrolyzer, the process involves the following half-reactions:

 At the anode (oxidation reaction) – 2OH−(aq) → O2(g) + 2H2O(l) + 4e−

 At the cathode (reduction reaction) – 2H2O(l) + 2e− → H2(g) + 2OH−(aq)

 In a PEM electrolyzer, the reactions are similar but occur across a membrane that allows
protons to pass through while blocking electrons. The half-reactions are as follows:

 At the anode (oxidation reaction) – 2H2O(l) → O2(g) + 4H+(aq) + 4e−

 At the cathode (reduction reaction) – 4H+(aq) + 4e−→ 2H2(g)

 The resulting hydrogen gas can be stored or directly used in various applications, from fuel
cells to industrial processes.

 G-8

 Advanced Blue Hydrogen Production

 Blue hydrogen is produced from natural gas with integrated CCS technologies that
significantly reduce CO2 emissions. This approach allows for the continued use of existing fossil
fuel resources while minimizing their environmental impact.

 Natural gas (primarily methane) serves as the feedstock for hydrogen production. The
process may require energy inputs from natural gas, electricity, or heat, depending on the
technology used (steam methane reforming or autothermal reforming). In SMR, methane reacts
with steam at high temperatures to produce hydrogen and carbon monoxide. The carbon monoxide
can be further reacted with steam in a WGS reaction to generate additional hydrogen. The carbon
dioxide produced is captured and stored, preventing its release into the atmosphere, thereby
achieving a lower carbon footprint.

 Solar Thermochemical Hydrogen Production

 Solar thermochemical hydrogen production harnesses concentrated solar energy to drive
chemical reactions that produce hydrogen. This innovative approach promises to utilize abundant
sunlight efficiently, contributing to a sustainable energy future. The primary feedstock can vary,
including water and metal oxides or other reactive materials. Concentrated solar power systems
provide the necessary thermal energy. In this process, concentrated solar energy heats a metal oxide
(such as zinc or cerium oxide) to high temperatures, causing it to release oxygen and form a
reduced metal. The metal can then react with water at lower temperatures to produce hydrogen.
This method not only utilizes renewable energy but also has the potential for high efficiency and
scalability.

 Biomass Gasification

 Biomass gasification converts organic materials into hydrogen-rich syngas, offering a
sustainable solution for hydrogen production while utilizing waste materials. This process
contributes to the circular economy and reduces reliance on fossil fuels. Feedstock includes
organic materials such as agricultural residues, forestry waste, or dedicated energy crops. The
process requires heat, which can be generated through combustion or provided by external energy
sources.

 During biomass gasification, organic material is subjected to high temperatures in a low-
oxygen environment, breaking it down into syngas, primarily composed of hydrogen and carbon
monoxide. This syngas can then be purified to separate hydrogen or further processed in a gas-to-
liquid process to create synthetic fuels. The overall process not only generates hydrogen but also
reduces waste and GHG emissions.

 Oxy-gasification is an advanced variation of biomass gasification that uses pure oxygen
instead of air as the gasification agent. Oxy-gasification operates at higher temperatures and can
increase hydrogen yields while reducing nitrogen dilution in the syngas. The process allows for
more efficient carbon capture, minimizing GHG emissions and making it particularly suitable for
integrated carbon capture and utilization strategies.

 G-9

 Through these processes, North Dakota will position itself at the forefront of sustainable
hydrogen production, ensuring a diverse and resilient energy landscape for the future.

 Long-Term (Theoretical and Advanced Approaches) Hydrogen Production Strategies

 In the long-term phase of hydrogen production in North Dakota, the focus will transition
from black hydrogen, produced through coal gasification, to green hydrogen, leveraging
advancements in renewable technologies. This evolution will encompass the integration of various
methods for hydrogen production, including solar thermochemical processes and nuclear energy,
which will offer sustainable and efficient pathways to produce hydrogen with minimal
environmental impact.

 Additionally, the exploration of biomass gasification will be prioritized, utilizing organic
materials as a feedstock for hydrogen generation. This approach not only enhances the
sustainability of hydrogen production but also contributes to waste reduction and resource
utilization.

 The development of advanced hydrogen storage and transportation technologies will remain
crucial, ensuring safe and efficient distribution across regions. Collaborative efforts among
research institutions, industry partners, and government bodies will be essential to establish a
resilient hydrogen economy. Ultimately, North Dakota aims to become a leader in both the
production and application of clean hydrogen technologies, significantly contributing to national
and global decarbonization efforts while supporting economic growth and energy security.

 Black Hydrogen via Coal Gasification

 Black hydrogen is produced through the gasification of coal, providing a reliable source of
hydrogen while utilizing existing fossil fuel resources. While this method generates significant
carbon emissions, it sets the stage for enhanced carbon capture technologies in the future. The
feedstock is coal, typically in the form of pulverized black coal. The process relies on heat and
steam, which can be generated through the combustion of additional coal or other fuels.

 During coal gasification, coal is converted into syngas (a mixture of hydrogen and carbon
monoxide) through a series of reactions. The primary chemical reaction can be represented as
follows:

                                      C + H2O → CO + H2

 This is accompanied by the WGS reaction, which increases hydrogen yield:

                                     CO + H2O → CO2 + H2

 The carbon dioxide produced can be captured and stored through advanced CCS
technologies, reducing the overall carbon footprint of hydrogen production.

 G-10

 Green Hydrogen via Solar Thermochemical Processes

 Solar thermochemical hydrogen production leverages concentrated solar energy to drive
chemical reactions, efficiently converting solar heat into hydrogen. This method represents a
sustainable approach to hydrogen production with the potential for high efficiency. The feedstock
is water and metal oxides (such as zinc or cerium oxide). Concentrated solar power provides the
necessary thermal energy.

 The solar thermochemical process generally involves two main steps:

 Reduction reaction – MO + Sunlight → M + 21O2 (where MO represents a metal oxide,
 and M is the reduced metal)

 Water-splitting reaction – M + H2O → MO + H2

 In this process, the reduced metal reacts with water to regenerate the metal oxide while
producing hydrogen gas.

 Biomass Gasification

 Biomass gasification is an innovative method that converts organic materials into hydrogen-
rich syngas, promoting a sustainable approach to hydrogen production while utilizing waste
resources. Feedstock comprises organic materials, such as agricultural residues, forestry waste, or
dedicated energy crops. The process requires heat, which can be generated through combustion or
provided by external energy sources.

 During biomass gasification, organic matter is subjected to high temperatures in a low-
oxygen environment, breaking it down into syngas. The primary chemical reaction can be
summarized as:

 Cbiomass + H2O → CO + H2

 The syngas produced undergoes further reactions, including:

 WGS reaction – CO + H2O → CO2 + H2

This reaction maximizes hydrogen yield by converting CO into additional hydrogen, thus
enhancing the overall efficiency of the process. Furthermore, capturing and utilizing CO2 during
this stage contributes to the sustainability of biomass as a hydrogen source, reducing the overall
carbon footprint.

 G-11

CHALLENGES AND MITIGATION

 Freshwater Resource Constraints

 The development of hydrogen production in North Dakota faces significant challenges
related to freshwater resource constraints. As the state aims to expand its hydrogen capacity, the
substantial amount of water required for various production technologies raises concerns about
sustainability and resource availability.

 The Challenge of Water Usage in Hydrogen Production

 One of the most significant challenges facing hydrogen production in North Dakota is the
substantial amount of water required for various production technologies. As the state endeavors
to expand its hydrogen production capacity, the implications of water usage on local resources,
agriculture, and communities must be carefully considered. The following technologies are
particularly water-intensive:

 • Electrolysis
 − Description: Electrolysis splits water (H2O) into hydrogen (H2) and oxygen (O2) using
 electricity.
 − Water requirement: This method requires significant quantities of water, especially at
 an industrial scale.
 − Impact: Increased demand for freshwater could strain local supplies, particularly
 during periods of drought.

 • SMR
 − Description: SMR produces hydrogen from natural gas by reacting it with steam at
 high temperatures.
 − Water requirement: A considerable amount of water is needed for steam generation.
 − Impact: This reliance on water could lead to competition for resources, especially in
 agricultural regions where irrigation is essential.

 • Coal gasification
 − Description: Black hydrogen is produced through coal gasification, converting coal
 into synthesis gas (syngas) by reacting it with steam and oxygen.
 − Water requirement: Water is required for both the gasification process and cooling.
 − Impact: The water-intensive nature of coal gasification could exacerbate scarcity
 issues, affecting both hydrogen production and agricultural practices.

 • Water-dependent hydrogen purification processes
 − Description: Postproduction purification of hydrogen often involves water-cooled
 systems to maintain efficiency.
 − Water requirement: A continuous water supply is needed for cooling and purification.
 − Impact: Increased industrial water demands could further stress local resources,
 particularly in regions already experiencing water scarcity.

 G-12

 Mitigation

 As North Dakota seeks to expand its hydrogen production capacity, the increasing challenge
of water scarcity highlights the necessity to explore alternative water sources. One promising
solution is the utilization of produced water—water extracted as a by-product of oil and gas
operations—for hydrogen production. This approach not only addresses water resource challenges
but also supports the sustainable growth of the hydrogen industry. The following list highlights the
benefits of utilizing produced water:

 • Resource efficiency – Utilizing produced water allows for a more efficient use of
 available resources. Instead of relying solely on freshwater sources, which are often
 limited, hydrogen production can tap into an existing supply of water that is currently
 being underutilized. This can alleviate pressure on local freshwater supplies and help
 sustain agricultural and community needs.

 • Environmental benefits – Reusing produced water for hydrogen production can mitigate
 environmental impacts associated with its disposal. This practice reduces the need for
 traditional disposal methods, such as deep well injection, which can pose risks to
 groundwater and surface water quality. By converting produced water into hydrogen, the
 industry can adopt a more environmentally responsible approach.

 • Cost-effectiveness – Utilizing produced water can potentially lower the overall costs
 associated with hydrogen production. The availability of produced water can reduce the
 need for expensive water sourcing and treatment processes, making hydrogen production
 more economically viable. This is particularly important for scaling up hydrogen
 production to meet growing demand.

 • Alignment with energy transition goals – The hydrogen economy plays a crucial role in
 achieving energy transition goals by providing a cleaner alternative to fossil fuels. By
 integrating produced water into hydrogen production, North Dakota can further align its
 hydrogen strategy with broader sustainability objectives, contributing to reduced carbon
 emissions and promoting a cleaner energy future.

 • Support for the oil and gas industry – The oil and gas industry is a significant part of
 North Dakota’s economy. By finding beneficial uses for produced water, hydrogen
 production can strengthen partnerships between sectors, fostering innovation and
 collaboration. This can lead to the development of integrated solutions that benefit both
 industries.

 • Technological innovation – Exploring the use of produced water in hydrogen production
 encourages technological advancements in water treatment, hydrogen production
 methods, and resource management. Innovations in these areas can improve efficiency,
 reduce costs, and enhance the overall sustainability of the hydrogen production process.

 In conclusion, utilizing produced water as a resource for hydrogen production presents a
viable solution to address the challenges of water scarcity while supporting the growth of North

 G-13

Dakota’s hydrogen economy. This approach not only promotes efficient resource use and
environmental sustainability but also aligns with the state’s economic and energy transition goals.
By investing in technologies that enable the safe and effective use of produced water, North Dakota
can lead the way in developing a more sustainable hydrogen production landscape.

POTENTIAL IMPACT

 The development of hydrogen production in North Dakota offers significant societal,
economic, and environmental benefits, positioning the state as a leader in the clean energy
transition. These impacts include:

 • Job creation and economic growth. The establishment of hydrogen production facilities,
 particularly in sectors such as natural gas processing, renewable energy, and industrial
 applications, will create new high-quality jobs. This includes roles in engineering,
 construction, operations, and maintenance of hydrogen infrastructure. The growth of a
 hydrogen economy can attract investment, boost local economies, and increase North
 Dakota’s role in the national energy landscape.

 • Reduction in carbon emissions. Hydrogen, particularly green and blue hydrogen,
 presents a major opportunity to decarbonize sectors like transportation, industry, and
 power generation. By using hydrogen in place of fossil fuels, North Dakota can
 significantly reduce its GHG emissions, contributing to state and national climate goals.
 CCS paired with hydrogen production from natural gas can also help mitigate CO2
 emissions, making it a valuable transition strategy.

 • Energy security and diversification. Hydrogen production enhances North Dakota’s
 energy security by diversifying its energy portfolio. By tapping into renewable wind
 energy for green hydrogen and using its natural gas resources for blue hydrogen, the state
 can reduce its dependence on external energy sources and build a resilient, future-proof
 energy system. The deployment of hydrogen as an energy carrier and storage solution
 also stabilizes the energy supply, particularly when integrated with renewable energy
 sources.

 • Technological leadership. North Dakota has the potential to become a hub for innovation
 in hydrogen production technologies, including electrolysis, CCS, and methane pyrolysis.
 By advancing these technologies, the state can position itself as a center of excellence in
 hydrogen research and development, attracting both national and international
 collaborations.

 • Environmental benefits. Along with reducing carbon emissions, hydrogen technologies
 can improve air quality by reducing reliance on fossil fuels in sectors like transportation
 and heavy industry. The use of hydrogen fuel cells in vehicles, for example, eliminates
 tailpipe emissions, helping to reduce air pollution and associated health impacts in local
 communities.

 G-14

CONCLUSION

 North Dakota’s hydrogen production potential is vast, spanning multiple resources such as
natural gas and wind energy. By adopting a phased approach—beginning with blue hydrogen in
the near-term and transitioning to green hydrogen in the mid- and long-term—the state can
strategically position itself as a leader in the hydrogen economy. Each phase leverages existing
technologies and resources while fostering the development of new innovations such as CCS and
renewable energy-based electrolysis.

 The deployment of hydrogen as a clean energy solution offers far-reaching benefits. This
transition will not only reduce GHG emissions and promote cleaner air but will also drive job
creation, economic growth, and energy security. North Dakota stands to gain significant
technological leadership, particularly as advancements in hydrogen production become more
efficient and scalable.

 However, the pursuit of hydrogen production also brings challenges, particularly concerning
the substantial water resources required for various production technologies. Addressing these
challenges through innovative solutions, such as utilizing produced water from oil and gas
operations, is crucial for ensuring sustainable development in the hydrogen sector.

 Furthermore, the economic viability of hydrogen, combined with the state’s renewable and
fossil energy resources, sets a strong foundation for long-term success. North Dakota’s
commitment to energy diversification, coupled with a clear road map for integrating hydrogen
technologies, ensures that the state remains at the forefront of the clean energy transition.

 As the state continues to implement pilot projects and invest in hydrogen infrastructure,
engagement with local communities, industry partners, and policymakers will be essential. North
Dakota’s hydrogen strategy has the potential to create a sustainable energy future, benefiting the
economy, the environment, and the community at large.

 By taking these steps, North Dakota will not only meet its own energy needs but also
contribute to the growing national and global hydrogen economy, driving innovation and
supporting a cleaner, more resilient energy system for the future.
